# Findings: DynamoDB / S3 / Amplify migration review

**Date:** 21/09/2026  
**Status:** Review complete — decision pending founder sign-off  
**Scope:** The five-part proposal — migrate the platform to DynamoDB; S3 presigned direct uploads with SSE; build and host on AWS Amplify; a five-subdomain topology (`platform.` / `university.` / `auth.` / apex / `assistant.`); a separate university login.  
**Method:** Ten parallel reviews against the live repository, the live `pattadar` database and live DNS/HTTP endpoints. Every finding was then put to three independent adversarial verifiers — one checking the cited evidence against the actual files, one checking the AWS limits and prices, one recalibrating severity for a pre-launch solo-founder platform — and dropped on a majority refutation. 137 findings raised, 133 survived, 4 refuted.

Companion to [2026-09-21-dynamodb-migration-review.md](./2026-09-21-dynamodb-migration-review.md), which carries the verdict, target architecture and phased plan.

Findings refuted by a majority of verifiers are listed at the end of each section, struck through, so they are not re-raised later.

---

## Query shapes vs DynamoDB

I extracted every SQL statement in services/api, services/gateway and services/assistant by AST-parsing the Python (519 distinct SELECT sites across 105 relations, plus 415 writes) and measured the real data in the local `pattadar` database. The honest ratio: 39% of reads (202/519) are already clean single-key lookups and port to DynamoDB with no thought; 29% (148/519) need a GSI; 30% (159/519) need denormalisation, stream-maintained aggregates, or multi-round-trip fan-out; and 2% (10/519) cannot be done in DynamoDB at all without OpenSearch. That last 2% is not a rounding error — it is the trigram name search over land.party (140,527,280 rows), the HNSW vector search over 44,360,232 halfvec(1024) boundary embeddings, and the associate directory search, i.e. three of the product's headline features. There are four genuine hard blockers (two item-size violations measured at 781KB and 25MB against the 400KB limit, plus vector and fuzzy search), and a second tier of expensive-but-solvable problems around GSI eventual consistency on the payment and token-redemption paths, four `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` work queues, and a single-row hash-chained audit ledger. The good news is real and worth stating: binary payloads are already externalised to S3 behind `file_ref`, the schema already evolves by additive `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS`, there is no PostGIS anywhere, and only 2 OFFSET uses exist. The S3-presigned-upload half of the proposal is sound and independently valuable. The DynamoDB half, as a wholesale migration, is a 9-18 month solo rewrite of a 25,815-line data layer that ends with you still paying for OpenSearch and an analytics store.

### `blocker` village_maps.geojson holds whole-village cadastre inline — measured 781,028 bytes vs DynamoDB's 400KB hard item limit

**Evidence.** `village_maps.geojson TEXT NOT NULL` (services/api/src/web360.py:462-478; psql \d village_maps confirms plain text, no bytea). Served whole from one row: services/api/src/main.py:7286 `SELECT geojson FROM village_maps WHERE file_name=%s`, and the code's own comment at main.py:7282 says "it is up to a megabyte of geometry". Measured against the shipped corpus in apps/web/public/vm: munagapadu.geojson = 781,028 B, chinthagunta.geojson = 592,303 B, marripalem.geojson = 276,317 B, ambapuram.geojson = 258,513 B — 2 of 8 villages already exceed 400KB. The parser docstring (services/api/src/village_map.py:4-6) states CHINTHAGUNTA is 2,100 plots / 4 MB raw, reduced by 6-decimal truncation. Telangana has ~10,000 revenue villages; the shipped 8 are the small end. DynamoDB's 400KB maximum item size is a hard service limit — not raisable by support request.

**Impact.** The single largest village map cannot be stored as a DynamoDB item, today, with the data already in the repo. Splitting a village into N chunk items means a Query returning ~2 items per 1MB page and the client reassembling GeoJSON across pages, which breaks the HTTP caching the endpoint deliberately relies on (main.py:7289 sets Cache-Control: public, max-age=300 on a plain JSON body precisely so the browser caches it like a file).

**Recommendation.** Do not put this in DynamoDB at any size. Move the geojson bytes to S3 behind CloudFront and keep only the index row (key, village, plots, acres, centre_lat/lon, outline) in the database — which is exactly what `village_maps_index` at main.py:7259 already selects, and exactly what the desk-built path already does by writing static files to apps/web/public/vm. This is a 1-day change that is correct regardless of what you decide about DynamoDB, and it makes the upload route a presigned PUT.

**Effort.** 1 day

> **Verifier correction.** village_maps.geojson is stored as plain `TEXT` in one row (web360.py:468; confirmed by `\d village_maps`) and served whole from that row (main.py:7286), and the shipped corpus already has 2 of 8 villages over DynamoDB's 400KB hard item limit — munagapadu.geojson 781,028 B and chinthagunta.geojson 592,303 B (exact `wc -c`). As stored today (raw JSON text) the largest village map cannot be a DynamoDB item. It is not strictly unstorable, though: gzipped into a Binary attribute those same files are 127,699 B and 90,202 B, so compression fits with ~3x headroom and `Content-Encoding: gzip` keeps the browser caching at main.py:7291 intact — chunking across items is not forced. The correct severity is therefore "no durable margin + wrong storage class", not "impossible today": 400KB is a hard, unraisable ceiling that after compression corresponds to only a ~2.4 MB / ~9,000-plot village, and village_map.py:4-6 records CHINTHAGUNTA alone at 4 MB raw, so bigger villages in Telangana's ~10,000 will breach it. Compressing also buys a decompress hop and 100+ WCUs per write for an immutable, public, CDN-shaped blob. Recommendation stands unchanged: put the bytes in S3 behind CloudFront via a pres

### `blocker` document_read_jobs.source is a 25MB BYTEA held in the row, and the queue claim does SELECT * on it under a lock

**Evidence.** `MAX_BYTES = 25 * 1024 * 1024` (services/api/src/ai_reading/jobs.py:24), enforced at jobs.py:65-66, inserted straight into `document_read_jobs(... source ...)` at jobs.py:94 and jobs.py:108 where the DDL declares `source BYTEA` (jobs.py:35). The worker claims with `SELECT * FROM document_read_jobs WHERE state='queued' AND created_at > now()-interval '1 day' ORDER BY created_at FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED LIMIT 1` (jobs.py:141) — `SELECT *` pulls the full 25MB blob into the claim transaction — then nulls it at completion (jobs.py:171, jobs.py:184). docs/compliance/gdpr-dpdp.md:13 classifies this column "Very high" sensitivity.

**Impact.** 25MB is 64x DynamoDB's 400KB item limit. This table cannot migrate as-is. It is also the AI reading path — the revenue feature — so a workaround here is on the critical path, not a side quest.

**Recommendation.** Put the source bytes in S3 under the job id with SSE-KMS (services/gateway/src/storage.py:165 already does exactly this), store only the S3 key in the job row, and delete the object on completion instead of `SET source=NULL`. This also removes a 25MB blob from every RDS backup and WAL segment today. Again: worth doing whether or not DynamoDB happens, and it is the natural home for the presigned-upload idea — the client PUTs to S3 directly and the API only ever sees a key.

**Effort.** 3-5 days

> **Verifier correction.** Correct as stated, with two citation nits: the 25MB blob is inserted at jobs.py:107-110 (jobs.py:94-95 is the dedupe insert, which explicitly writes `source` as NULL), and the SSE-KMS args are at services/gateway/src/storage.py:162-167 (`ServerSideEncryption: "aws:kms"` on line 164), guarded by `if not self._allow_unencrypted_local`. Also worth adding: `source` is nulled in three places, not two — jobs.py:171 (cancel after dispatch), jobs.py:184 (completion), and jobs.py:204 (a reaper for jobs stuck `running` >15 minutes) — so an S3-key redesign must also delete the object on that third path.

### `blocker` pg_trgm fuzzy name search over land.party (140,527,280 rows / 35 GB) has no DynamoDB equivalent

**Evidence.** services/assistant/src/public_records/service.py:401-413: `SELECT p.rid, p.s_no, p.name, ..., similarity(p.name_norm, %(query)s) AS score ... FROM land.party p JOIN land.real_estate_records r USING (rid, s_no) WHERE p.name_norm %% %(query)s ORDER BY score DESC, r.reg_date DESC NULLS LAST LIMIT %(limit)s`, with `local_settings={"pg_trgm.similarity_threshold": threshold}`. Repeated at service.py:546-557 with cap=1000. Backed by `idx_party_name_trgm`. Village resolution does the same at service.py:197 and :210 (`max(similarity(village_norm, %(n)s)) ... GROUP BY village_norm ORDER BY sim DESC`). pg_class: land.party reltuples = 140,527,280.

**Impact.** DynamoDB supports exactly two string predicates on a key: equality on the partition key and begins_with/between on the sort key. `contains` exists only as a FilterExpression, which is applied after the read and therefore requires a full table Scan of 35 GB — and it is still exact substring, not fuzzy. Indian name transliteration is precisely where fuzzy matching earns its keep (VENKATESWARLU / VENKATESHWARLU). This feature cannot be ported; it can only be rebuilt on OpenSearch.

**Recommendation.** Leave the land corpus on Postgres. If you ever do need this at higher scale, the answer is an OpenSearch index of the ~140M name_norm values, not DynamoDB — but note that is an additional store you pay for on top of DynamoDB, not a replacement for it.

**Effort.** n/a — do not migrate

> **Verifier correction.** Evidence stands as written, with two clarifications: (a) the fuzzy village lookups at service.py:197 and :210 hit `land.real_estate_records.village_norm` (backed by `idx_village_trgm`, ~45.1M rows), not land.party; (b) the `_party_report` query at service.py:546-557 orders by `r.reg_date DESC NULLS LAST` only (score is selected but unused as a sort key) with a hardcoded threshold of 0.35 — it is `find_properties_by_party` (403-408) that orders by `score DESC, r.reg_date DESC`. Also worth noting for the recommendation: service.py:510 already uses `local_settings={"hnsw.ef_search": ...}`, i.e. a pgvector HNSW index exists in the same corpus, so the "would need OpenSearch" statement should read "would need a separate search/vector store" — Postgres currently carries both the trigram and the vector path.

### `major` 30% of the read surface (159/519) needs denormalisation or stream-maintained aggregates: 27 GROUP BY, 37 JOIN, 98 aggregate-function queries

**Evidence.** AST extraction over services/api/src/*.py, services/gateway/src/*.py, services/assistant/src/**/*.py yields 519 SELECT sites across 105 relations. Construct counts: ORDER BY 153 (75 distinct order expressions), aggregate functions 98, LIMIT 72, subquery 58, JOIN 37, GROUP BY 27, `= ANY(array)` 23, DISTINCT 12, UNION 7, ILIKE 5 literal (17 including dynamically-built fragments), pg_trgm similarity 4, DISTINCT ON 3, WITH RECURSIVE 4, OFFSET 2. Window functions: 0. PostGIS ST_*: 0. Classifying each site by what DynamoDB would need: 202 pure key-value (39%), 148 need a GSI (29%), 159 need denormalisation/aggregate maintenance/fan-out (30%), 10 need an external index (2%).

**Impact.** The 30% bucket is where the calendar time goes. Every GROUP BY becomes a counter item maintained by a DynamoDB Streams consumer, which introduces a new failure mode the current code does not have: the counter can drift from the truth and nothing detects it. The codebase explicitly rejects this design today — services/api/src/web360.py:3727 comments "A COUNT, every time, and never a stored counter."

**Recommendation.** Treat this as the real scope number: roughly 160 query sites need new designs, not translations, plus a Streams consumer per aggregate with a reconciliation job per counter. Budget accordingly before committing.

**Effort.** 6-12 months for the 159 sites alone

> **Verifier correction.** Two fixes. (1) The comment is at services/api/src/web360.py:3725, not :3727 — and the stronger citation is web360.py:2281-2283, where the `AssociateDiscipline` docstring names the failure mode outright: "There is no stored counter for it to disagree with: a counter incremented on assignment and forgotten on completion freezes the roster after three jobs each, and it does it silently." (2) The headline should read "roughly a quarter to a third of the read surface (~134-159 of ~520-580 SELECT sites)" rather than a precise "30% (159/519)": the union of GROUP BY ∪ JOIN ∪ aggregate sites is mechanically 134, and the 159 figure plus the 202/148/159/10 split are hand classifications layered on top of the mechanical counts. The scope conclusion — ~150 query sites needing new designs plus a Streams consumer and reconciliation job per aggregate — stands unchanged.

### `major` The associates desk roster is one SQL statement that becomes ~5 GSIs + 2 stream-maintained aggregates + an OpenSearch index

**Evidence.** services/api/src/web360.py:3807-3853 is a single statement: `SELECT a.*, COALESCE(j.open_jobs,0), ..., COALESCE(rv.rating_average,0) FROM associates a LEFT JOIN (SELECT assignee_ref, COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE closed=false) AS open_jobs, COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE closed=true) AS done_jobs, MAX(...) FILTER (WHERE closed=true) FROM work_requests WHERE assignee_ref <> '' GROUP BY assignee_ref) j ON j.assignee_ref=a.id LEFT JOIN (SELECT associate_id, COUNT(*), AVG(rating) FROM associate_reviews GROUP BY associate_id) rv ON rv.associate_id=a.id WHERE true` — then optional `AND EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM associate_disciplines ...)` (3835), `AND EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM associate_areas ...)` (3841), a 9-column ILIKE OR including a correlated EXISTS (3845-3850), and `ORDER BY a.name, a.id LIMIT %s` (3852). Followed by four more `= ANY(%s)` fan-outs at 3862, 3866, 3869 and `_open_kinds_of` (web360.py:3748).

**Impact.** `WHERE true` on a global directory means there is no partition key — in DynamoDB this is a Scan. The two derived GROUP BYs become counter items on every associate, updated by a Streams consumer on every work_requests and associate_reviews transition. The 9-column ILIKE is impossible without OpenSearch. `ORDER BY a.name` across the whole directory needs a GSI with a constant partition key, which is the textbook hot-partition antipattern (capped at 3,000 RCU/s / 1,000 WCU/s for that one key, forever). This is the admin surface the founder's own memory notes as designed-but-unbuilt phases 2-5 — it dies first.

**Recommendation.** If DynamoDB happens at all, the associates directory stays relational. It is a small, low-write, high-query-variety table — the worst possible DynamoDB candidate and a trivial Postgres one.

**Effort.** 3-4 weeks to rebuild, and it would be worse

> **Verifier correction.** The SQL quote and its consequences are correct, but the severity framing needs two corrections. First, `_roster` is not always keyless: web360.py:3829 supports `a.id = ANY(%s)` and 3831 supports `a.state = %s`, and four of its ten call sites (4219, 6362, 9566, 9601) pass explicit ids — those become ordinary GetItem/BatchGetItem. Only the directory-browse call sites (6263, 6349, 6464, 6499, 6532, 6623) are keyless. Second, `limit` is capped at 500 at web360.py:3853 and the docstring at 3801-3806 states the desk deliberately reads the whole roster into memory, so the DynamoDB cost is a bounded ~500-item Scan, not an unbounded one, and the "hot partition capped at 3,000 RCU/s forever" argument does not bite at that size. The genuine DynamoDB problems are the two stream-maintained aggregates (open_jobs/done_jobs/last_completed_at off work_requests, rating_count/rating_average off associate_reviews) and the 9-column ILIKE with a correlated EXISTS over associate_areas, which needs OpenSearch or a client-side filter over the scanned page. Recommendation stands: keep the associates directory relational.

### `major` The coverage grid GROUP BYs every tenant's parcels and properties with no owner predicate — a cross-tenant Scan in DynamoDB

**Evidence.** services/api/src/web360.py:4410-4414: `SELECT pb.state, pb.district, pb.mandal, pb.village, COUNT(*) AS n FROM parcels p JOIN passbooks pb ON pb.id = p.passbook_id WHERE p.archived = false GROUP BY pb.state, pb.district, pb.mandal, pb.village`; web360.py:4419-4421: `SELECT '' AS state, district, city AS mandal, locality AS village, COUNT(*) AS n FROM properties WHERE archived = false GROUP BY district, city, locality`; web360.py:4434-4436: `SELECT area_key, kind, COUNT(*) AS n FROM work_requests WHERE closed=false AND area_key <> '' GROUP BY area_key, kind`. 34 SELECT sites have no WHERE clause or `WHERE true` at all, including 10 `SELECT count(*) FROM <table>` admin counters at main.py:6496-6744.

**Impact.** A four-level JOIN + GROUP BY over every account's land, plus a second one over every account's properties, with no key to partition on. In DynamoDB: Scan parcels, Scan passbooks, hash-join in Python. At 1,000 accounts × 100 records that is ~100,000 items per Scan per page load; the 1MB Query/Scan page limit means ~40-60 sequential round trips per render.

**Recommendation.** This whole surface (coverage grid, admin counters, audit browse, governance) is analytics, not OLTP. If you migrate, these queries must move to a read replica or an Athena-over-S3 export — which means you are running two datastores and a replication pipeline where you currently run one.

**Effort.** 4-6 weeks plus an ongoing export pipeline

> **Verifier correction.** The coverage grid at services/api/src/web360.py:4409-4435 runs three deliberately cross-owner aggregates — a parcels⋈passbooks four-level GROUP BY, a properties GROUP BY, and a work_requests GROUP BY — with no tenant predicate; the function's own docstring (4363-4368) calls it "Aggregate-only and cross-owner". Worse than stated: the state/district drill-down filters in Python (4385-4388), not in SQL, so every level of the grid reads every account's rows. In DynamoDB this is Scan parcels + Scan passbooks + hash-join in the app, paged at 1MB. Drop the claims about "10 admin counters at main.py:6496-6744" (those 13 statements are one-time boot seed guards, `if cnt == 0: executemany(INSERT ...)`) and about "34 SELECT sites with no WHERE" (main.py has 29 `SELECT count(*)` total, mostly `WHERE owner_user_id=%s`). Note also that `coverage` is admin-gated (web360.py:6491), so the cost is per associates-desk render. The recommendation stands: this surface is analytics and needs a relational replica or an Athena-over-S3 export, i.e. two datastores plus a replication pipeline.

### `major` 16 of 46 pessimistic row locks are acquired through a non-primary-key lookup — and DynamoDB GSIs cannot be read strongly consistently

**Evidence.** DynamoDB has no ConsistentRead on a global secondary index (hard, documented limit). The codebase locks through secondary keys on the money and credential paths: services/api/src/payments.py:244 `SELECT * FROM payment_intents WHERE order_id=%s FOR UPDATE` inside the Razorpay webhook transaction; payments.py:213 `SELECT * FROM payment_operations WHERE intent_id=%s AND kind=%s FOR UPDATE`; services/api/src/main.py:2557 `SELECT * FROM invitations WHERE token=%s ORDER BY id FOR UPDATE`; main.py:4710 `SELECT * FROM inactivity_capabilities WHERE token_hash=%s FOR UPDATE`; services/api/src/web360.py:7246 `SELECT request_hash,order_count FROM service_order_intents WHERE owner_user_id=%s AND intent_key=%s FOR UPDATE`; main.py:2844/2871/2885 on inactivity_escalations; main.py:2553 `WHERE id=%s OR legacy_beneficiary_id=%s FOR UPDATE` (an OR across two attributes — two GSIs and a merge). 46 FOR UPDATE sites total.

**Impact.** Razorpay retries webhooks aggressively. Two deliveries ~100ms apart both Query the order_id GSI; the second may not see a write the first just made, because GSI propagation is asynchronous and unbounded. Result: double-capture on the payment path and double-redemption on invitation and inactivity-capability tokens. TransactWriteItems cannot save you here because it has no read-then-branch — the webhook handler at payments.py:244-260 reads the intent, checks mode, conditionally inserts an event, and conditionally updates operations, which must be restructured into an optimistic retry loop.

**Recommendation.** Every one of these needs the secondary key promoted to the partition key of its own item (e.g. an item keyed `ORDER#<order_id>` carrying the intent id) so the lookup is a strongly-consistent GetItem on the base table, then a conditional write. That is a per-site redesign, and getting it wrong on the payment path costs real money. Do not do this before launch.

**Effort.** 4-6 weeks, and it is the highest-risk part of the migration

> **Verifier correction.** Keep the finding; fix one sentence of the impact narrative.

"Razorpay retries webhooks aggressively. Two deliveries ~100ms apart ... Result: double-capture" is not the live failure mode, because a *retry* carries the same `x-razorpay-event-id` and is already deduped by a primary-key conditional write at payments.py:246-249:

    stored = await _row(conn, 'INSERT INTO payment_webhook_events(id,digest,intent_id,event) VALUES(%s,%s,%s,%s)'
                        ' ON CONFLICT(id) DO NOTHING RETURNING id', (event_id, digest, ...))

`payment_webhook_events.id TEXT PRIMARY KEY` (payments.py:54), so that guard ports to DynamoDB cleanly as a conditional PutItem on the partition key and stays strongly consistent. Retry-of-the-same-event is NOT the exposure.

The real exposure is concurrent *distinct* events — `payment.authorized`, `payment.captured` and `order.paid` all route to `_request_verification` (payments.py:255) and can arrive within milliseconds of each other with different event ids, so the dedupe row does not stop them. Today the only thing serialising them is the `FOR UPDATE` row lock taken at line 244 through `order_id`. On DynamoDB that lookup becomes an eventually-consisten

### `major` Four FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED work queues have no DynamoDB equivalent

**Evidence.** services/api/src/audit.py:853 `SELECT * FROM audit_outbox WHERE state='pending' ORDER BY enqueued_at FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED LIMIT 1`; services/api/src/payments.py:367-371 `SELECT o.* FROM payment_operations o JOIN payment_intents i ON i.id=o.intent_id WHERE i.mode=%s AND ((o.status IN (...) AND o.next_at<=now()) OR (o.status='processing' AND o.lease_until<now())) ... ORDER BY o.next_at FOR UPDATE OF o SKIP LOCKED LIMIT 1`; services/api/src/notify.py:285 `... ORDER BY next_at LIMIT %s FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED) RETURNING *`; services/api/src/ai_reading/jobs.py:141 the document-reading claim.

**Impact.** DynamoDB offers no queue semantics — no visibility timeout, no atomic claim-and-lease, no ordering. The payments one additionally JOINs to payment_intents to filter by mode, which DynamoDB cannot express in a single read at all. The safe port is SQS (or EventBridge Scheduler for the next_at/backoff logic), which means the writes that enqueue work and the queue itself now live in different services with no shared transaction — the outbox pattern stops being atomic unless you add DynamoDB Streams as the enqueue path.

**Recommendation.** Plan on SQS + DynamoDB Streams for all four, and accept that the audit outbox's exactly-once story changes shape. This is well-trodden but it is four separate rewrites, not a config change.

**Effort.** 3-4 weeks

> **Verifier correction.** Evidence is accurate as written. One strengthening detail the finding omits: the audit outbox worker takes a *second* lock in the same transaction — audit.py:862-863 `SELECT seq, head_hash FROM audit_chain_head WHERE id=1 FOR UPDATE` — to serialize a gapless `seq` and `prev_hash` hash chain (the docstring at audit.py:843-849 spells out the two-lock ordering). So the audit port is not merely "queue semantics"; it also needs a globally serialized monotonic append, which on DynamoDB means a single hot partition key with a conditional-write retry loop (DynamoDB Streams orders only per partition key, so it does not supply this for free). That makes audit the hardest of the four, not an equal peer. Also: payments.py already implements a 90s `lease_until` visibility timeout in SQL, so the SQS port there replaces existing semantics rather than adding new ones.

### `major` The hash-chained audit ledger depends on a single-row FOR UPDATE counter and a Postgres trigger that reads DB roles

**Evidence.** services/api/src/audit.py:488-501 declares `audit_chain_head` as "One row, ever. Appending locks it FOR UPDATE, which serializes chain" — and audit.py:863-890 does exactly that: `SELECT seq, head_hash FROM audit_chain_head WHERE id=1 FOR UPDATE`, then `seq = head['seq'] + 1`, `row_hash = chain_hash(prev_hash, integrity, seq)`, insert, then `UPDATE audit_chain_head SET seq=%s, head_hash=%s WHERE id=1`. Append-only is enforced by a trigger that calls `pg_has_role(current_user, 'pattadar_audit_maintainer', 'USAGE')` and rejects any change to `seq`/`prev_hash` (audit.py:527-560), with a UNIQUE index on seq (audit.py:480). Verification walks the chain with `SELECT * FROM audit_events_v2 WHERE seq IS NOT NULL ... ORDER BY seq` (audit.py:989-994).

**Impact.** A gapless global sequence is a single hot item. DynamoDB caps one partition-key value at 1,000 WCU/s, and under conditional-update contention the effective throughput is far lower because every loser retries. More seriously, DynamoDB has no triggers and no database roles: the append-only guarantee that docs/compliance/soc2-controls.md rests on becomes an IAM policy plus application discipline, which is a materially weaker control and a harder thing to put in front of an auditor. Chain verification (`ORDER BY seq` over the whole ledger) becomes a full Scan.

**Recommendation.** Keep the audit ledger relational. It is append-only, low-volume, and its entire value is the integrity property Postgres is enforcing for you today. If you must move it, use QLDB-style semantics or S3 Object Lock for the seal — not a DynamoDB counter item.

**Effort.** n/a — do not migrate

> **Verifier correction.** Evidence verified as quoted. Two refinements for accuracy: (1) the chain append runs in an outbox drain worker, not the request path — `_drain_one` takes the outbox row with `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` (audit.py:852-856) before locking the head (audit.py:863), and only advances the head when the INSERT actually landed (`if cur.rowcount == 1`, audit.py:887-890), so the hot-item ceiling is the audit event rate, not the API request rate. (2) Chain verification is not unconditionally a full scan: `verify_chain` accepts `from_seq` and a linking `prev_hash` for incremental windows (audit.py:974-996), and in DynamoDB a (chain-id, seq) key would make that a Query; the real DynamoDB losses are the gapless counter item (1,000 WCU/s per partition key, less under conditional-write contention) and the absence of triggers/DB roles, which turns the live `audit_events_v2_guard` trigger plus the `pattadar_audit_maintainer` role (both confirmed present in the local DB) into IAM policy plus application discipline. Note the code itself concedes the trigger "is not a privilege boundary" and fails OPEN when the maintainer role is absent (audit.py:520-526).

### `moderate` DynamoDB's Limit applies before FilterExpression — 17 filtered-LIMIT queries will silently return fewer results than exist, including typeahead

**Evidence.** services/api/src/web360.py:5915-5916: `SELECT id, name, subtitle, shelf FROM documents WHERE owner_user_id=%s AND name ILIKE %s ORDER BY name LIMIT 5`. services/api/src/web360.py:5922-5924: `SELECT DISTINCT ON (person_name) id, person_name, role, record_id FROM record_people WHERE owner_user_id=%s AND person_name ILIKE %s ORDER BY person_name, id LIMIT 5`. services/api/src/web360.py:2013 and :2030: `ORDER BY (name ILIKE 'FMB%%') DESC, sort LIMIT 1`. 72 SELECT sites use LIMIT; 17 of those combine LIMIT with a predicate that would become a FilterExpression.

**Impact.** In DynamoDB, `Limit` bounds the number of items *evaluated*, not the number returned after filtering. A Query with Limit=5 and a FilterExpression can legitimately return 0 items while matches exist further in the partition. Typeahead search would appear broken for exactly the users with the most documents — the paying ones. This is a silent correctness bug, not a performance one, and it is easy to miss in testing with seed data (the local DB has 77 documents).

**Recommendation.** Any filtered LIMIT must become a paginate-until-N loop with LastEvaluatedKey. Note the `DISTINCT ON` and the `ORDER BY (expr) DESC, col` forms have no DynamoDB expression at all and need precomputed composite sort keys written at insert time.

**Effort.** 1-2 weeks

> **Verifier correction.** Same finding, with two detail fixes: (a) the `record_people` typeahead query is at services/api/src/web360.py:5923-5925, not 5922-5924 (the other three citations — 5915-5916, 2013, 2030 — are exact); (b) the aggregate counts should be stated as reproducible numbers rather than "72 SELECT sites / 17 filtered": a repo-wide `grep -rn "LIMIT" --include=*.py` over services/api/src, services/gateway/src and services/assistant/src yields 96 LIMIT-bearing lines (31 in web360.py, 19 in main.py), and the exact filtered-LIMIT subset needs to be enumerated per-query rather than asserted. Severity, mechanism, impact and recommendation all stand unchanged.

### `moderate` Multi-table transactions exceed TransactWriteItems' 100-item / 4MB cap, and 57 of 141 UPDATE/DELETE statements use a non-key predicate

**Evidence.** services/api/src/main.py:4335 (`delete_group`) runs inside `conn.transaction()` and touches 10 tables — passbooks, properties, parcels, family_members, family_notifiers, favourites, groups, inactivity_capabilities, inactivity_deliveries, inactivity_escalations — via predicates like `UPDATE passbooks SET group_id='' WHERE group_id=%s AND owner_user_id=%s` (main.py:4340) and `DELETE FROM family_members WHERE group_id=%s AND owner_user_id=%s` (main.py:4346). main.py:3081 does `DELETE FROM parcel_owners WHERE parcel_id IN (SELECT id FROM parcels WHERE passbook_id=%s)`. web360.py:7072 is a 7-table transaction. Scripted count: 415 writes (212 UPDATE, 134 INSERT, 69 DELETE), 54 `ON CONFLICT` upserts, and 57 of the 141 UPDATE/DELETE statements filter on something other than the primary key. There are 96 explicit transaction blocks.

**Impact.** DynamoDB has no update-by-query: each of those 57 becomes Query-a-GSI → paginate → BatchWriteItem in batches of 25. TransactWriteItems caps at 100 items and 4MB, so a household whose deletion touches more than 100 rows cannot be deleted atomically — you get a partially-deleted account, which is a DPDP erasure problem, not just a bug. `DELETE ... WHERE parcel_id IN (SELECT ...)` needs a full extra read pass first.

**Recommendation.** Each of these needs a saga with a durable progress marker and idempotent retry. Verify the 100-item/4MB ceiling against the largest real household before assuming it fits.

**Effort.** 6-8 weeks

### `moderate` Hot partition: one village_norm value extrapolates to ~637,000 records, and a single partition key is capped at 3,000 RCU/s regardless of table capacity

**Evidence.** `SELECT village_norm, count(*) FROM land.real_estate_records TABLESAMPLE SYSTEM (0.3) GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 2 DESC` returns: empty-string village_norm = 1,910, VIJAYAWADA(URBAN) = 1,019, GUNTUR (U) = 796 in the sample — extrapolating ×333 gives ~637,000, ~340,000 and ~265,000 records for single keys. Distinct village_norm in the same sample: 16,036. land.real_estate_records is 45,144,496 rows / 28 GB, so ~620 bytes per record. The natural access pattern keys on village: services/assistant/src/public_records/service.py:327-337 builds `village_norm = %(v)s AND survey_no ~ %(survey_no)s` with a POSIX regex, then `ORDER BY reg_date ASC NULLS LAST, rid, s_no LIMIT %(page_size)s OFFSET %(page_offset)s` (service.py:339-342).

**Impact.** DynamoDB's adaptive capacity can split a partition but cannot split a single partition-key value — 3,000 RCU/s and 1,000 WCU/s is a hard ceiling for VIJAYAWADA(URBAN) forever. Reading that key's ~210 MB at the 1MB page limit is ~210 sequential Query round trips. And `survey_no ~ regex` has no DynamoDB expression at all (no regex, only begins_with/contains), so it becomes a FilterExpression: you pay read units for all 210 MB to return a handful of rows. The `OFFSET` pagination contract exposed to the assistant as `next_offset` also has no equivalent — DynamoDB pagination is forward-only cursors.

**Recommendation.** Another reason the land corpus stays on Postgres. If you ever shard it, the key must be composite (village + year, or village + survey prefix), and the regex must be replaced with a stored normalised survey key — which changes the tool contract the assistant depends on.

**Effort.** n/a — do not migrate

### `moderate` Storage cost goes the wrong way: 393 bytes of repeated attribute names per row on a 45M-row table

**Evidence.** pg_attribute: land.real_estate_records has 40 columns whose names sum to 393 bytes; land.party has 8 columns / 41 bytes; the 85 public tables average 14 columns / 126 bytes of names. DynamoDB counts attribute names toward both item size and billed storage on every item. 45,144,496 rows × 393 B = 17.7 GB of nothing but column names on a table that is 28 GB in Postgres — ~63% inflation before a single value. land.party: 140,527,280 × 41 B = 5.8 GB. Total land schema today: 290 GB (sum of pg_total_relation_size). Local public schema for reference: 85 tables, 116 secondary indexes, 44 FK constraints, 201 indexes total; widest table is `properties` at 43 columns in `public` plus 39 in `app` (that is where the 82 figure comes from — they are two different tables, not one).

**Impact.** At ap-south-1 list (verify before relying on these): DynamoDB Standard storage ≈ $0.285/GB-month vs RDS gp3 ≈ $0.127/GB-month. The 290 GB corpus at ~1.4x item inflation is ~400 GB ≈ $114/month in DynamoDB vs ~$37/month in RDS — before any read or write charge. The one-off load is ~274M write request units (140.5M + 45.1M at 1 WRU, 44.4M vectors at 2 WRU) ≈ $195-390 depending on current on-demand pricing, and is throttled by the 40,000 WCU on-demand table default to ~2 hours at absolute best burst. Against a costing model that budgets ₹15/account-month for all shared infrastructure (docs/specs/2026-09-19-platform-costing-model.md:45, i.e. ~$176/month total at 1,000 accounts), DynamoDB storage for the corpus alone eats two-thirds of the budget.

**Recommendation.** Shorten attribute names aggressively if any large table does move (a 40-column item with 3-char names saves ~14 GB here), but the better answer is not to move the corpus at all. Re-run the numbers against live ap-south-1 pricing before any decision — these are list-price estimates, not a bill.

**Effort.** n/a — pricing input

> **Verifier correction.** Same claim with three details fixed: (a) the 44 foreign-key constraints are NOT in `public` -- `public` has only 2; the 44 is the cross-schema total (app 40, public 2, ref 2), and `app` is where the relational shape actually lives (16 tables); (b) `public` has 201 indexes of which 92 back PK/UNIQUE constraints, so 109 secondary indexes, not 116; (c) the attribute-name inflation on land.real_estate_records is 17.74 GB against an actual 28.1 GiB table = ~59% (63% only if you compare against a decimal 28 GB). Everything else -- 393 bytes / 40 cols, 41 bytes / 8 cols, 126 bytes / 14 cols average, 45,144,496 and 140,527,280 rows, 44.36M boundary vectors, 289.8 GB land total, and the verbatim ₹15/account-month reserve at costing-model line 45 -- is exact. The claim is also conservative: it omits DynamoDB's ~100 bytes per item of storage overhead, which adds a further ~4.5 GB on real_estate_records and ~14 GB on party.

### `positive` POSITIVE: binary payloads are already externalised to S3, so the presigned-upload half of the proposal is sound and low-risk

**Evidence.** parcel_photos and property_photos carry `file_ref TEXT` and no bytes — measured max pg_column_size across all rows is 332 and 332 bytes respectively; family_members.photo max octet_length is 0. The only inline blobs in the whole public schema are `document_read_jobs.source` (the single bytea column) and `village_maps.geojson`. services/gateway/src/storage.py:165-166 already sets `ServerSideEncryption: aws:kms` with an explicit `SSEKMSKeyId`, so the encryption half is built. The storage service already models nodes/versions/shares relationally (services/gateway/sql/init.sql, 9 tables) with the bytes in S3.

**Impact.** Direct-to-S3 presigned PUT removes the API from the upload path entirely, which is the single biggest latency and cost win available — it eliminates the 25MB body through ECS, and it fixes the two item-size blockers above as a side effect.

**Recommendation.** Do this first, independently, and do not couple it to the DynamoDB decision. Two caveats worth pinning down: a presigned PUT must carry `x-amz-server-side-encryption: aws:kms` and the matching key id as signed headers or the object lands unencrypted-by-your-key, and the bucket policy should deny `s3:PutObject` without them; and presigned URL lifetime is capped at 7 days for SigV4, and at the IAM role's session length when signed by a role (typically 1 hour on ECS/Lambda) — sign short and refresh.

**Effort.** 1-2 weeks for both blockers

### `positive` POSITIVE: the schema already evolves additively, there is no PostGIS, and 39% of reads are single-key — the codebase is not hostile to DynamoDB, it is just bigger than DynamoDB's shape

**Evidence.** Schema is applied at boot by idempotent `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` blocks under an advisory lock (e.g. services/api/src/web360.py:475-478 for village_maps; the same pattern in audit.py, governance.py, payments.py, associates.py, geography.py) — there are no down-migrations to port and the code already tolerates missing attributes, which is DynamoDB's native model. `ST_*` count across all SQL: 0 — geometry is handled in pure Python (services/api/src/fmb_geometry.py, services/api/src/village_map.py), so there is no spatial-index blocker on the app tables. Window functions: 0. OFFSET: 2 sites only (services/assistant/src/public_records/service.py:340, services/assistant/src/adapters/conversation_store.py:48). 202 of 519 SELECTs are single-key lookups. apps/university/ARCHITECTURE.md:280 already states the right policy in the founder's own words: "Move learning progress and tutor sessions to DynamoDB when measured throughput or cost supports the migration. Keep catalog authoring and financial workflows relational unless their access patterns clearly justify a move."

**Impact.** A targeted DynamoDB adoption is genuinely viable where the access pattern is `PK=<tenant or entity>, SK=<time or child id>` with high write volume and no cross-tenant reporting — university learning progress, tutor/assistant conversation state (services/assistant/src/adapters/conversation_store.py, r_conversations/r_conversation_messages, 18 SELECT sites), and idempotency/session records. Those are additive new tables, not migrations.

**Recommendation.** Adopt the university doc's policy for the whole platform: DynamoDB for new, high-write, single-tenant-keyed state; PostgreSQL stays for the 85-table relational core, the audit ledger, payments, and the 290 GB land corpus. That gets you most of the operational benefit for roughly 5% of the risk, and it does not put a pre-launch product through a data-layer rewrite. A full migration of the existing surface is a 9-18 month solo rewrite that ends with you running DynamoDB *plus* OpenSearch *plus* an analytics store where you run one Postgres today.

**Effort.** policy decision — 0 days

## Transactions, invariants, audit chain

The codebase is not "SQL that happens to use Postgres" — it is built on four Postgres primitives DynamoDB does not have: SAVEPOINT (nested `conn.transaction()`), `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` row locks, partial unique indexes, and privilege separation via GRANT/REVOKE plus a BEFORE UPDATE trigger. The audit hash chain and the payments state machine are both correct today *because* of those primitives, and both have specific code paths that TransactWriteItems cannot express at all — not "would be slow", but "is an illegal request": two updates to the same item in one transaction (payments.py:440 and payments.py:449 both write `payment_intents`), and insert-then-read-own-write-then-update on one item (payments.py:206-215). There are some genuine positives: zero SERIAL columns and zero sequences in the live `public` schema (verified against `pg_sequences`), all ids application-generated, and the idempotency ledger is already a pure compare-and-set that maps 1:1 onto a conditional PutItem. But 34 non-PK unique indexes (16 of them *partial*, which DynamoDB cannot express in any form), 11 `COALESCE(MAX(sort),0)+1` counters that are only safe inside a transaction, and an erasure runner that discovers its own delete order by querying `pg_constraint` at runtime are all load-bearing and all have to be hand-rebuilt. My honest read: migrating the audit trail and payments to DynamoDB is roughly six months of rewrite for a solo founder, and the tamper-evidence story comes out weaker, not stronger. The rest of the platform (records, parcels, documents, university learning state) is a much more reasonable DynamoDB candidate — which is exactly what the repo's own apps/university/ARCHITECTURE.md:128 already says.

### `blocker` TransactWriteItems forbids two operations on the same item — the payment capture path does exactly that, twice

**Evidence.** services/api/src/payments.py:440 `UPDATE payment_intents SET payment_id=%s,status='captured',error='',updated_at=now() WHERE id=%s` and then, in the same `async with conn.transaction():` block opened at payments.py:413, services/api/src/payments.py:449 `UPDATE payment_intents SET status='settlement_pending',settlement_plan=%s WHERE id=%s` (the late-capture-after-close refund branch). Same row, two writes, one transaction. Same shape in services/api/src/web360.py:8775 (`_move` → `UPDATE work_requests SET status...`, web360.py:2933) followed by web360.py:8782 `UPDATE work_requests SET outcome_note=%s, accepted_at=%s, cost=%s WHERE id=%s`. And services/api/src/payments.py:208-215 is worse: `_enqueue` INSERTs a payment_operations row (payments.py:114-118), then payments.py:210 `SELECT * FROM payment_operations WHERE intent_id=%s AND kind=%s FOR UPDATE` reads that just-inserted row, then payments.py:216 UPDATEs it — insert, read-own-write, update, on one item, inside one transaction. AWS limit: TransactWriteItems "cannot target the same item with more than one operation" and returns ValidationException; transactions also cannot read their own writes.

**Impact.** These are not throughput problems that a bigger table fixes — they are requests DynamoDB rejects. Each has to be re-derived into a single merged UpdateItem with a conditional expression that computes the final state in one shot, which means the branch logic at payments.py:436-452 (did this capture land on an already-closed ticket? does it need a refund enqueued?) has to be decided *before* the write rather than during it. Get that merge wrong on the capture path and the failure is silent: an intent left in `captured` with a refund operation enqueued but `settlement_plan` never set, so `route_ledger_plan` (payments.py:287) sees `settlement_plan IS NULL` and reserves a *second* settlement for money already being refunded. That is a double payout against one captured payment, and nothing in the code would report it — `process_one` would mark both operations `done`.

**Recommendation.** Do not migrate payments. Keep payment_intents / payment_operations / payment_webhook_events / service_payments on Postgres (RDS or Aurora Serverless v2, which scales to 0.5 ACU and is credit-eligible). If you insist on moving them, budget for rewriting each multi-write transaction as a single-item state machine with an explicit version attribute and a CAS loop, plus a reconciliation job that can detect a double settlement — you do not have one today because Postgres made it impossible.

**Effort.** 6-10 weeks for payments alone, plus a reconciliation harness that does not exist yet

> **Verifier correction.** Two of the three cited paths are real same-item-twice-in-one-transaction writes that TransactWriteItems would reject with ValidationException: (1) payments.py:437 `UPDATE payment_intents SET payment_id=%s,status='captured',...` followed by payments.py:446 `UPDATE payment_intents SET status='settlement_pending',settlement_plan=%s` inside the transaction opened at payments.py:413 (the finding cited these as :440 and :449 — off by three; :449 is a different branch); (2) web360.py:8778 `_move(...)` → `UPDATE work_requests SET status,stage,needs_you,closed,status_at` at web360.py:2967, followed by web360.py:8781 `UPDATE work_requests SET outcome_note,accepted_at,cost`, both inside `_ticket_transaction()`.

Drop the third example: `_request_verification` (payments.py:207-220) does NOT write the same payment_operations item twice — the UPDATE at payments.py:219 is gated at :214 on the payload differing from the payment_id just inserted at payments.py:118, so it fires only when the INSERT was a no-op on a pre-existing row. What it does do is read a row it may have just inserted in the same transaction, which DynamoDB transactions also cannot express — a real but lesser constraint.

### `blocker` The audit module's fail-open/fail-closed policy depends on SAVEPOINT, which DynamoDB has no equivalent for at any price

**Evidence.** services/api/src/audit.py:771-798 `enqueue()` wraps its INSERT in `async with conn.transaction():` — a SAVEPOINT when the caller is already in a transaction — and its docstring states why: "in PostgreSQL a failed statement aborts the whole surrounding transaction, so without a savepoint a swallowed enqueue error would poison the caller's business transaction." services/api/src/web360.py:3621-3627 spells out the money consequence: "Callers inside `_ticket_transaction` share one database transaction with money rows, and a failed statement aborts that transaction whether or not its exception is caught — so swallowing without one would turn a missing history line into a released payout that never commits." The opposite policy also exists: audit.py:239-247 `CRITICAL_ACTIONS` (reveal_aadhaar, desk_read, account.export, account.erasure_requested) must fail CLOSED — audit.py:830-833 re-raises so the business transaction rolls back. TransactWriteItems is strictly all-or-nothing: no savepoints, no nesting ("a transaction cannot contain another transaction"), and any cancelled item cancels all of them.

**Impact.** There are only two placements and both are wrong. Put the audit items inside the TransactWriteItems and a throttled or malformed audit write rolls back the payout — precisely the behaviour web360.py:3624 forbids. Put them outside and you lose what audit.py:773-776 calls "the atomicity guarantee the old best-effort `log_audit` never had": a ticket acceptance commits, the audit write then fails, and there is no record that four thousand rupees moved. For the four CRITICAL_ACTIONS the inversion is a compliance failure — an Aadhaar reveal would complete with no audit row, which is the exact hole audit.py:26-29 was built to close.

**Recommendation.** Keep audit + the business tables it must commit with on the same Postgres instance. If audit must move, the only honest design is: business write to DynamoDB with a `pending_audit` marker attribute in the same TransactWriteItems, a Streams consumer that seals the event, and a sweeper that alarms on markers older than N seconds — i.e. you rebuild the outbox you already have, on top of a system that gives you weaker guarantees. Say no to this one.

**Effort.** rewrite — there is no incremental version

The finding's "four CRITICAL_ACTIONS" undercounts and drops the auditor-read case, which is if anything the strongest compliance example. (2) Line offsets are off by 1-2: `enqueue()` is audit.py:771-797 (savepoint docstring at 782-789, `async with conn.transaction():` at 791); the fail-closed re-raise is audit.py:826-833 (`if is_critical_action(action):` at 828); the web360.py savepoint rationale is 3620-3627, not 3621-3627. Everything else — the quoted enqueue docstring, the quoted web360 `_audit` "released payout that never commits" text, the module 

### `blocker` IAM cannot express "INSERT only" on a DynamoDB table, so the append-only privilege boundary has no equivalent and tamper-evidence is genuinely weakened

**Evidence.** services/api/src/audit.py:623-654 `role_bootstrap_sql()` produces the real control: `GRANT SELECT, INSERT ON audit_events_v2 TO pattadar_audit_writer`, `GRANT SELECT, UPDATE, DELETE ON audit_events_v2 TO pattadar_audit_maintainer`, `REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE, TRUNCATE ON audit_events_v2 FROM pattadar_app`. Both roles exist in the live DB (verified: `SELECT rolname FROM pg_roles WHERE rolname LIKE 'pattadar%'` → pattadar, pattadar_audit_writer, pattadar_audit_maintainer) and the trigger is installed (`audit_events_v2_guard` on audit_events_v2). The second half is the BEFORE UPDATE OR DELETE trigger at audit.py:527-585, which additionally requires the `pattadar.audit_maintenance` SET LOCAL flag (audit.py:537) — two independent gates, and audit.py:660-668 is explicit that the role is the half "the application cannot grant itself". DynamoDB's IAM has `dynamodb:PutItem`, `UpdateItem`, `DeleteItem` and `dynamodb:LeadingKeys` — but **there is no IAM condition key for ConditionExpression**. You cannot grant "PutItem, but only if attribute_not_exists(event_id)".

**Impact.** Give the API role `dynamodb:PutItem` on the audit table (which it needs to append) and it can silently overwrite any existing audit item, because PutItem replaces by default. The one control that makes the trail evidence rather than a log — the application cannot rewrite the record of what the application did — cannot be reconstructed with IAM alone. The hash chain still detects the overwrite on the next `verify_chain` walk, but only because the chain exists; you have downgraded a *prevention* control (NIST AU-9, which audit.py cites at line 19) to a *detection* control, and the code's own comment at audit.py:524 already concedes that the trigger is the weaker of the two halves.

**Recommendation.** If the audit table moves to DynamoDB, the API must get zero direct DynamoDB access to it. Appends go through a small Lambda or a dedicated ECS task with its own execution role, invoked via SQS FIFO with MessageGroupId fixed so ordering is real; the API role gets `sqs:SendMessage` and nothing else. Provision the table in Terraform and deny `CreateTable`/`DeleteTable`/`UpdateTable` to every runtime role. Budget the writer service as new code with its own failure modes. Note that today's boot-time DDL (`ensure_schema`, audit.py:1310) runs as the app login, so the equivalent DynamoDB sin — leaving CreateTable/DeleteTable on the task role — would let the app delete the entire trail in one API call with no per-item trace.

**Effort.** 4-6 weeks for the writer service + IAM/Terraform boundary

(1) The sentence 'The role is the half the application cannot grant itself' is at audit.py:517 (inside the trigger comment block), not 660-668; lines 660-668 are the role_bootstrap_sql docstring, which makes the equivalent point ('the maintainer role is created but deliberately left ungranted') — substance right, range misattributed. The 'HONEST LIMIT' concession is at 523, not 524. (2) The finding understates its own case: the app login is 'rhub', not 'pattadar_app', and the bootstrap has already been APPLIED in the live DB — rhub is a member of pattadar_audit_writer and its ACL on audit_events_v2 is INSERT/SELECT/REFERENCES/TRIGGER, i.e. UPDATE/DELETE/TRUNCATE were revoked even though rhub owns the table. So this is a live, enforced privilege boundary that DynamoDB IAM cannot reproduce, not an unrun script.

### `major` The hash chain's `FOR UPDATE` on a single row becomes an optimistic CAS on a single DynamoDB item — correct, but with different failure behaviour and a hard 1,000 WCU/s ceiling that adaptive capacity cannot lift

**Evidence.** services/api/src/audit.py:863 `SELECT seq, head_hash FROM audit_chain_head WHERE id=1 FOR UPDATE`, then audit.py:889 `UPDATE audit_chain_head SET seq=%s, head_hash=%s WHERE id=1`, both inside the single transaction opened at audit.py:850 — and audit.py:487-492 states the invariant: "Appending locks it FOR UPDATE, which serializes chain writes: without that, two workers could read the same prev_hash and both claim seq N, forking the chain into two branches that no verifier could reconcile." The table is CHECK-constrained to exactly one row (audit.py:498 `CONSTRAINT audit_chain_head_single CHECK (id = 1)`). AWS limits: a single partition sustains 1,000 WCU/s and 3,000 RCU/s; a single item is always in exactly one partition and adaptive capacity cannot split it. Conditional writes are optimistic, not blocking — a `ConditionalCheckFailedException` still consumes a WCU and the AWS SDKs do **not** auto-retry it, so the retry loop is yours to write.

**Impact.** The ceiling is not the near-term problem: 124 audit call sites (grep of `audit.record(|log_audit(|await _audit(` across services/, excluding tests) at pre-launch volume is single-digit writes/sec, far under 1,000. The real change is behaviour under contention. Today one ECS task runs one `audit.worker()` (services/api/Dockerfile:17 defaults `UVICORN_WORKERS:-1`), so `FOR UPDATE` blocks briefly and every append eventually lands in order. With N tasks doing CAS on one item, each round has one winner and N-1 losers that must re-read and retry; effective throughput on a single contended item degrades well below the partition ceiling, and unlike a row lock there is no fairness — a slow task can starve. You must also write the retry loop yourself, and a bug in it produces exactly the forked chain audit.py:489 warns about, which `verify_chain` (audit.py:1035) reports as "sequence gap inside the chain — an event was removed from history": an operator would be chasing a tamper alert caused by a retry bug.

**Recommendation.** If the chain moves, serialise it with a single-consumer queue (SQS FIFO, one MessageGroupId, Lambda reserved concurrency 1) rather than letting N writers contend on one item — you get ordering for free and the CAS becomes uncontended. Set the head item's write as a ConditionExpression on `seq = :expected` so a lost update fails loudly instead of forking. Keep `scripts/verify_audit_chain.py` and run it on a schedule; it is the only thing that would catch a bad retry loop.

**Effort.** 2-3 weeks inside the larger audit rewrite

> **Verifier correction.** Citation fixes: the transaction opens at audit.py:851 (`async with _pool.connection() as conn, conn.transaction():`), not 850 (850 is the closing `"""` of the docstring); `verify_chain` is defined at audit.py:974 and 1035 is the line carrying the quoted "sequence gap" message; the verifier lives at services/api/scripts/verify_audit_chain.py, not repo-root scripts/. The quoted comment reads "two workers (or two API replicas)". Substantive addition: the append is not a CAS on a single item. One transaction holds four statements — the outbox row locked `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` (audit.py:853-856), the event INSERT with `ON CONFLICT (event_id) DO NOTHING` (868-884), the head UPDATE guarded by `if cur.rowcount == 1` so a duplicate replay does not burn a seq (886-888), and the outbox DELETE (892). The DynamoDB equivalent is a `TransactWriteItems` over three items (event, head, outbox) with a ConditionExpression on each: 2x WCU, all-or-nothing, limits of 100 items / 4 MB per transaction, and no way to express "insert if absent, and only then advance the head" in one call — the `rowcount == 1` branch becomes a caught `TransactionCanceledException` whose per-item CancellationReasons must be 

### `major` DynamoDB Streams does not cleanly replace audit_outbox — it moves the seal to *after* the write and caps replay at 24 hours

**Evidence.** The outbox today commits with the business change: services/api/src/audit.py:771-798 `enqueue()` writes to `audit_outbox` on the caller's own connection, and `run_one()` (audit.py:839-907) drains it with two locks in a fixed order (outbox row `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` at audit.py:852, then the chain head at audit.py:863). The backlog is an observable number: audit.py:1285-1290 `SELECT count(*) AS backlog, count(*) FILTER (WHERE attempts >= 3) AS stalled, min(enqueued_at) AS oldest FROM audit_outbox WHERE state='pending'`, and audit.py:1303 refuses `healthy` while backlog ≥ 1000 or anything is stalled. AWS: DynamoDB Streams retains records for 24 hours; Lambda delivery from a stream is at-least-once; ordering is guaranteed only per partition key within a shard, never globally across a table.

**Impact.** Two regressions. (1) The event is written to the table *before* it is sealed, and the un-sealed window is invisible: there is no `audit_outbox` row to count, so audit.py:1285's backlog metric — the thing that tells you the trail is not yet tamper-protected — has no equivalent. A Lambda that dies with a poison record leaves events accumulating unsealed and nothing reports it until the 24-hour retention expires and they are unsealed *forever*, with `verify_chain` seeing a truncated tail it would report as "events were removed from the end" (audit.py:1091). (2) At-least-once delivery means the same event can be presented twice; today the PRIMARY KEY conflict at audit.py:882 (`ON CONFLICT (event_id) DO NOTHING` plus the `cur.rowcount == 1` guard at audit.py:886 that deliberately does not burn a seq on a replay) handles that, and the equivalent — conditional PutItem plus a separate head update — is two non-atomic steps unless both go in one TransactWriteItems.

**Recommendation.** If you go this way: enable Kinesis Data Streams for DynamoDB instead of DynamoDB Streams (retention configurable up to 365 days, not 24 hours), keep a `sealed: false` attribute on every audit item written by the producer, seal it in the consumer, and alarm on a GSI query for unsealed items older than 60 seconds. That is the missing backlog metric. Accept that the event exists un-sealed for the stream lag and say so in docs/compliance/soc2-controls.md.

**Effort.** 3-4 weeks

> **Verifier correction.** Keep the finding as written except for the verify_chain paragraph. Replace it with: "A Lambda that dies with a poison record leaves events accumulating unsealed and nothing reports it, because the backlog metric at audit.py:1285-1289 has no equivalent. Worse, the chain verifier does not catch it either: audit.py:1060-1075 only reports 'events were removed from the end' (audit.py:1073) when `head_seq > last_seq`, i.e. when already-sealed rows are deleted. An event that was never sealed never got a `seq`, so the head still equals the last sealed row and `verify_chain` returns ok:true while the events silently expire at the 24-hour retention boundary and are lost forever. The unsealed-item alarm in the recommendation is therefore not a nice-to-have, it is the only possible detector." Also fix the line numbers: `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` is audit.py:854 (statement starts 852), the `ON CONFLICT (event_id) DO NOTHING` replay guard on the audit_events_v2 insert is audit.py:874, the `cur.rowcount == 1` seq guard is audit.py:887, and the truncation message is audit.py:1073. Optionally add: `record()` at audit.py:798-834 fails closed for critical actions and `enqueue` uses a SAVEPOINT, so a Dy

### `major` 34 non-PK unique indexes, 16 of them PARTIAL — DynamoDB cannot express a conditional uniqueness constraint in any form

**Evidence.** Live DB counts: 34 unique indexes excluding primary keys, 16 with a WHERE clause. The 16 partial ones: account_erasure_jobs.account_erasure_active `UNIQUE (owner_user_id) WHERE status <> 'completed'`; ticket_dispatches.uq_dispatch_one_accept `UNIQUE (ticket_id) WHERE offer_state = 'accepted'` (services/api/src/associates.py:404-405); ticket_dispatches.uq_dispatch_one_per_round `UNIQUE (ticket_id, associate_id, offer_round) WHERE purpose='offer' AND associate_id <> ''` (associates.py:410-412); parcel_photos.parcel_photos_one_cover_idx and property_photos.property_photos_one_cover_idx `UNIQUE (x_id) WHERE is_cover`; family_members.idx_family_self_grp `UNIQUE (owner_user_id, group_id) WHERE is_self`; associates.uq_associates_token / uq_associates_contact, share_links.idx_share_links_token, ticket_dispatches.idx_ticket_dispatches_token, desk_tasks.uq_desk_tasks_dedupe, service_payments.uq_service_payments_idem (all `WHERE col <> ''`); plus the four `idx_*_lgd_code` geography ones. Gateway adds storage_nodes_unique_name `UNIQUE (owner_id, parent_id, lower(name)) NULLS NOT DISTINCT WHERE trashed_at IS NULL` (services/gateway/sql/init.sql:41-42) — partial *and* expression-based *and* null-distinctness-tuned. There is also a boot-time assertion that three of these exist at all: services/api/src/associates.py:998-1002 `REQUIRED_INDEXES` with the comment at associates.py:995-997 explaining that `CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS` silently no-ops on a renamed predicate.

**Impact.** Each non-PK unique needs a separate guard item written in the same TransactWriteItems as the row, and each *partial* unique additionally needs the guard **deleted** whenever the predicate stops holding — e.g. `uq_dispatch_one_accept` means the guard item `TICKET#<id>#ACCEPTED` must be created when an offer is accepted and removed if the acceptance is revoked, in the same transaction, or the ticket can never be re-offered. Miss one delete and the invariant inverts from "at most one" to "exactly zero forever". `uq_dispatch_one_accept` is the constraint that stops two associates each being told they won the same job and each being paid; it is currently one line of DDL and would become roughly 40 lines of guard-item lifecycle per site. Worst of all, `REQUIRED_INDEXES` has no DynamoDB counterpart: there is no way to assert at boot that the guard writes are still in the code path, so a refactor that drops a guard write is undetectable until two people are paid for one job.

**Recommendation.** Count on ~34 guard-item lifecycles. Write property-based tests for each (concurrent claim → exactly one winner; revoke → next claim succeeds) before migrating anything, because the constraint is now test-enforced rather than engine-enforced. For the four geography `lgd_code` ones, just make lgd_code the partition key of a lookup table. Seriously consider keeping ticket_dispatches and account_erasure_jobs relational — they are low-volume and their invariants are the sharp ones.

**Effort.** 4-6 weeks, plus permanent ongoing review cost on every write path

> **Verifier correction.** Same claim stands; two citation nits: the gateway index spans services/gateway/sql/init.sql:41-43 (not 41-42), and the boot check at services/api/src/web360.py:498 / :501-530 logs an error rather than refusing to start (web360.py:511-515 says this is deliberate for local dev). The explanatory comment spans associates.py:993-997. Everything else — 34 non-PK uniques, 16 partial, all 16 names and predicates, associates.py:404-405, :410-412, :998-1002, and the NULLS NOT DISTINCT expression index — verified exact against the live catalog and the files.

### `major` 11 `COALESCE(MAX(sort),0)+1` counters are read-then-write with no unique constraint to catch the collision

**Evidence.** services/api/src/web360.py:3118 (ticket_dispatches), 3188 (dynamic `{over['table']}`), 3231 (record_people), 7596 (purchase_lots), 7817 (dynamic `{table}`), 7868 (documents), 8029 (share_links), 8067 (record_people), 8253 (land_features), 8654 (ticket_deliverables), and services/api/src/capabilities.py:252 (ticket_deliverables). Example, web360.py:8654: `SELECT COALESCE(MAX(sort), 0) + 1 AS s FROM ticket_deliverables WHERE ticket_id=%s`, executed inside `_ticket_transaction()` (web360.py:2820-2824) which holds the ticket row lock taken at web360.py:8696. No unique index exists on any `(parent_id, sort)` pair — verified against pg_indexes; ticket_deliverables has only `ticket_deliverables_pkey` on `id`.

**Impact.** These are safe today only because the surrounding transaction holds a `FOR UPDATE` lock on the parent row, so the MAX is a repeatable read. DynamoDB has neither the lock nor the aggregate: you would Query the partition sorted descending with Limit 1, then Put — and two concurrent adds read the same MAX and both write sort=N. Because nothing is unique on sort, neither write fails. The user-visible result is two deliverables (or two people, or two purchase lots) with the same sort position, rendering in nondeterministic order — and for ticket_deliverables that is the list an acceptance iterates at web360.py:8751 to decide what gets filed onto the land record. Silent, not loud.

**Recommendation.** Stop deriving sort from MAX. Use a lexicographically sortable client-generated id (ULID or KSUID) as the sort key — it is monotonic, collision-free without coordination, and removes the read entirely. This is a change worth making in Postgres *first*, regardless of whether you migrate; it deletes 11 read-modify-write cycles today.

**Effort.** 2 weeks, and worth doing on Postgres anyway

> **Verifier correction.** Same finding, four citation fixes: (1) ticket_deliverables does NOT have "only ticket_deliverables_pkey" — it also has `idx_ticket_deliverables_ticket ON (ticket_id, sort)`. It is non-unique, so the "nothing catches the collision" conclusion stands, but the index inventory as stated is false. (2) The parent-row lock for the add-deliverable path is at web360.py:8650 (`row = await _ticket_row(conn, uid, ticket_id, lock=True)`), not 8696; line 8696 is inside `review_deliverable`, which takes no `FOR UPDATE` on the ticket at all. (3) The acceptance loop is at web360.py:8750 (8751 is the `continue` guard inside it) and it iterates a query ordered `ORDER BY sort, id` (8736), so duplicate `sort` values there tie-break deterministically by id — the "nondeterministic order" impact applies to the many bare `ORDER BY sort` readers instead (e.g. capabilities.py:159, web360.py:3087, 5620, 5630, 5780, 5824, 6164, 6688). (4) "Safe today only because the surrounding transaction holds a FOR UPDATE lock" is not true of all 11 sites: the photo-append at web360.py:7809-7819 opens a bare pool connection with no parent lock and no explicit transaction, so two concurrent photo uploads to the same record 

### `major` The idempotency middleware fails OPEN on any datastore error — under DynamoDB throttling that becomes routine, and a retried payment mutation runs twice

**Evidence.** services/api/src/main.py:6975-6977: `except Exception as exc: _log.warning("idempotency.claim_failed (running unguarded): %r", exc); verdict = "unguarded"`, then main.py:6980 `if verdict == "unguarded": return await self.app(scope, replay_receive, send)`. The claim itself is clean and maps perfectly: main.py:6934-6938 `INSERT INTO idempotency_keys (owner_user_id, key, operation, status, created_at) VALUES (%s,%s,%s,'pending',%s) ON CONFLICT (owner_user_id, key) DO NOTHING` with `cur.rowcount == 1` as the claim signal — that is exactly `PutItem` with `ConditionExpression: attribute_not_exists(pk)`, and the composite PRIMARY KEY (owner_user_id, key) is a natural partition/sort key pair. The purpose is stated at main.py:6771-6776: "Mobile clients retry mutations on flaky rural networks; without a guard a retried createPassbook or recordPayment lands twice."

**Impact.** On Postgres a claim failure means the pool is exhausted or the DB is down — rare, and when it happens the mutation behind it usually fails too. On DynamoDB the same `except Exception` catches `ProvisionedThroughputExceededException` and `RequestLimitExceeded`, which on an on-demand table during a traffic ramp (on-demand starts at 4,000 WCU/s and doubles no faster than every 30 minutes) are ordinary, transient and correlated with load. So the exact moment the platform is busiest is the moment every retried mutation runs unguarded — and the retries are themselves caused by the timeouts that the throttling produces. A user on a rural connection taps "pay" twice, both requests run unguarded, and `checkout` (payments.py:128) creates two intents... except it cannot, because `UNIQUE(owner_user_id,ticket_id)` (payments.py:42) catches it. That constraint is your real backstop, and it is one of the 34 non-PK uniques from the previous finding.

**Recommendation.** Change the fail-open to fail-closed for mutations that move money or create records — return 503 and let the client retry — before any migration. Then, if you migrate, retry the claim on throttling with jitter rather than skipping the guard. Also note `_IDEM_MAX_RESPONSE = 256 * 1024` (main.py:6789): a 256 KB memoized response fits DynamoDB's 400 KB item limit, but costs 256 WCU to write and 32 RCU to replay — drop it to 32 KB.

**Effort.** 3 days for the fail-closed fix; 1 week for the DynamoDB claim path

(b) The payments example is out of scope. `_idem_classify` (main.py ~6836-6855) only guards requests whose body is JSON containing a document matching `^\s*mutation\b`; `POST /payments/tickets/{id}/checkout` is a REST route with no GraphQL body and is therefore never guarded by this middleware at all — the `UNIQUE(owner_user_id,ticket_id)` constraint is its *only* duplicate protection today, fail-open or not. Likewise `recordPayment` appears nowhere in services/api/src except the comment at main.py:6773, so it is not a real mutation. ### `major` Two safety gates that must not be wrong read through non-PK unique columns, which become eventually-consistent GSI queries

**Evidence.** (1) services/api/src/payments.py:245: `SELECT * FROM payment_intents WHERE order_id=%s FOR UPDATE` — `order_id` is `payment_intents_order_id_key`, a UNIQUE index, not the PK (`payment_intents_pkey` is on `id`). If the lookup misses, payments.py:250 still inserts the webhook row with `intent_id=NULL`, returns `{'received': True}`, and because the insert is `ON CONFLICT(id) DO NOTHING` a Razorpay redelivery of the same `x-razorpay-event-id` is treated as a duplicate at payments.py:252-256 and dropped. (2) services/api/scripts/erase_account.py:100: `SELECT count(*) AS n FROM payment_intents WHERE owner_user_id=%s AND status NOT IN ('settled','void')` — the gate that stops an erasure destroying the only reconciliation record for in-flight money (erase_account.py:96-102, and the test at services/api/tests/test_account_data.py:133-147 asserts it raises). AWS: **global secondary indexes support eventually consistent reads only** — `ConsistentRead=true` is not available on a GSI, at any price.

**Impact.** (1) A `payment.captured` webhook that arrives before the GSI has propagated the intent's order_id is permanently orphaned: the money is captured at Razorpay, `payment_intents.payment_id` is never set, and the redelivery that would have fixed it is swallowed as a duplicate. `reconcile_checkouts` (payments.py:481) would eventually catch it — but only for intents still in `awaiting_payment`, so the window is real but recoverable. (2) is worse and not recoverable: a stale GSI read returns count 0, `require_settled_payments` passes, and `purge_api` (erase_account.py:105) deletes payment_intents/payment_operations/service_payments for an owner with a captured-but-unsettled payment. The money is gone from the provider's side and the only local record of what it was for no longer exists. The test that guards this (test_account_data.py:133) would still pass, because it runs against Postgres semantics.

**Recommendation.** Any predicate that gates money or deletion must read the base table with ConsistentRead, never a GSI. That constrains key design: pick PK = `OWNER#<uid>` / SK = `INTENT#<ticket_id>` so `_intent()` (payments.py:110, the primary access path, which already queries by `owner_user_id, ticket_id`) is a consistent GetItem, and accept that `order_id` and `payment_id` lookups go through a GSI — then make the webhook handler *retry* on a GSI miss with backoff before accepting the orphan, and never treat a redelivery of an orphaned event as a duplicate. For the erasure gate, do not use a query at all: maintain an `unsettled_intents` counter item under `OWNER#<uid>` updated in the same transaction as every intent status change, and read it consistently.

**Effort.** 2-3 weeks, and it constrains the whole key design

> **Verifier correction.** Line numbers are off by 1-9 in the current tree: the webhook lookup is payments.py:244 (not 245), the webhook insert payments.py:247-249 (not 250), the duplicate return payments.py:250-254 (not 252-256), `_intent` payments.py:111 (not 110), `reconcile_checkouts` payments.py:490 (not 481), and the erasure test is defined at test_account_data.py:131 with the raises assertion at 139-140 (not 133). Two substantive refinements: (a) the non-PK status of `order_id` should cite the DDL directly — payments.py:41 `order_id TEXT UNIQUE, payment_id TEXT UNIQUE` against payments.py:38 `id TEXT PRIMARY KEY` — rather than the Postgres constraint name; (b) gate (2) is an eventually-consistent GSI read only under a key design that does not partition by owner. ### `moderate` The `accept_ticket` transaction scales with deliverable count and has no upper bound — it will cross the 100-item TransactWriteItems cap

**Evidence.** services/api/src/web360.py:8730-8800, inside `_ticket_transaction()` (web360.py:2820). Per accepted deliverable: one write to the filed table via `_file_deliverable` (web360.py:3176 UPDATE or 3195 INSERT), one `UPDATE ticket_deliverables` (web360.py:8758-8762), one `_event` INSERT (web360.py:2923, called at 8765). Fixed cost: `_write_ledger` (web360.py:2980) writes up to 3 `service_payments` rows plus a `_event` each (web360.py:3013-3020) = 6, plus `payments.route_ledger_plan` (payments.py:265) enqueuing up to 3 `payment_operations` and updating `payment_intents`; `_move` = 2 (work_requests + ticket_events); the second `UPDATE work_requests` at 8782; `_remember_person`; `_audit` = 2 (audit_events at main.py:1395 + audit_outbox at audit.py:781); and per live dispatch, a notification_log write plus a `ticket_dispatches` update. So roughly 3D + 16 + 2X items. No LIMIT or cap on deliverable count exists — grep of web360.py for a deliverable bound returns nothing, and they are added one at a time at web360.py:8659. AWS: TransactWriteItems is capped at 100 items and 4 MB, and consumes 2× the WCU of the equivalent non-transactional writes.

**Impact.** A ticket with ~28 accepted deliverables crosses 100 items and the whole acceptance fails with ValidationException — including the ledger release. That is a survey job with a photo set, which is not exotic for this product. It also fails *loudly*, which is the good case; the bad case is that someone splits it into two transactions to get under the cap and the filing lands without the payout, or vice versa. Note this path is already illegal under finding #1 for a different reason (two work_requests writes), so the cap is the second problem, not the first.

**Recommendation.** If ticketing ever moves, model an acceptance as a saga with a durable state item rather than one transaction: mark the ticket `accepting`, file deliverables idempotently in batches, write the ledger, then flip to `accepted` — with a sweeper that finishes or reverses a stuck `accepting`. That is a real distributed-systems component you do not have. Alternatively cap deliverables per ticket at something like 20 in Postgres today, which is a one-line guard and worth having either way.

**Effort.** 6-8 weeks for the saga; 1 day for the cap

2. **`route_ledger_plan` is at payments.py:267, not 265**; the `audit_outbox` INSERT is at **audit.py:792, not 781** (781 is inside the docstring); the `audit_events` INSERT is at **main.py:1396, not 1395**; the deliverable INSERT is at **web360.py:8658, not 8659**. All trivial offsets — the code says what the finding says it says.
