### `moderate` The erasure runner discovers its own delete order by querying pg_constraint at runtime — that introspection disappears entirely

**Evidence.** services/api/scripts/erase_account.py:79-93 `foreign_children()` queries `pg_constraint` joined to `pg_attribute` to find every single-column FK, then checks whether a cascade would reach another owner's rows and raises `RuntimeError("Foreign-owned dependent records require operator review")` (erase_account.py:93). erase_account.py:148 does it again to build the delete graph: `for row in conn.execute("SELECT conrelid::regclass::text child,confrelid::regclass::text parent FROM pg_constraint WHERE contype='f'")`, feeding the topological sort at erase_account.py:150-160 which raises on a cycle. erase_account.py:111 also takes `LOCK TABLE ... IN SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE MODE` across every in-scope table, and erase_account.py:141 enumerates owner columns from `information_schema.columns`. Reality check on the FK count: the live `public` schema has only **2** foreign keys (payment_operations→payment_intents, payment_webhook_events→payment_intents, both via `psql -c "...contype='f'"`). The 40 others are in a legacy `app` schema the API does not use, and 2 in `ref`. The real cascades that matter are elsewhere: services/gateway/sql/init.sql:25 `parent_id UUID REFERENCES storage_nodes(id) ON DELETE CASCADE` — a *recursive* folder-tree delete — plus init.sql:56, 71, 94, 95, and services/assistant/src/main.py:98, 114, 128.

**Impact.** The good news is that the FK exposure in the API's own schema is tiny — 2 constraints, both within payments — so "42 FKs to hand-implement" overstates it considerably. The bad news is that the erasure runner's *entire design* is reflective: it asks the database what the shape is, every run, and refuses if it finds a cycle or a cross-owner cascade. Under DynamoDB there is nothing to ask. The runner becomes a hand-maintained table graph, which means every new table is a silent DPDP bug until someone remembers to add it — the opposite of the current property, where a new FK is picked up automatically. The storage_nodes recursive cascade (init.sql:25) is the genuinely painful one: deleting a folder currently removes an arbitrarily deep subtree in one statement, and in DynamoDB that is a recursive descent with BatchWriteItem, no atomicity, and a partially-deleted tree if it dies halfway.

**Recommendation.** If the gateway's storage metadata moves, store the full materialised path on every node so a subtree delete becomes a single `begins_with` Query rather than a recursive walk — then the delete is resumable and idempotent. Keep the erasure runner's "refuse if you find something unexpected" posture by replacing pg_constraint introspection with a checked-in manifest plus a CI test that fails when a table exists that the manifest does not name.

**Effort.** 3-4 weeks for the erasure runner; 2 weeks for the storage tree

> **Verifier correction.** Same claim, with two citations corrected: the `LOCK TABLE ... IN SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE MODE` is at services/api/scripts/erase_account.py:112 (not :111), and the `information_schema.columns` owner-column enumeration is at erase_account.py:31 inside `catalog()` (not :141). Everything else — the pg_constraint introspection at :80-84 and :148, the raises at :93 and :154, the live FK counts (public=2, app=40, ref=2, the two public FKs both payments→payment_intents), init.sql:25/56/71/94/95, and assistant main.py:98/114/128 — is verbatim correct. Worth adding: erase_account.py:146-147 already carries a hand-written `CHILD_LINKS` graph plus a hardcoded `children["invitations"] = {...}` override, and `foreign_children()` is invoked a second time at :228 guarding the storage tree ("Foreign-owned folder children require review before erasure"), so the runner is a hybrid of manifest and introspection today — which makes the recommended "checked-in manifest + CI completeness test" a smaller step than the finding implies.

### `moderate` Account erasure and audit redaction are predicate-scoped multi-row UPDATEs with no DynamoDB equivalent

**Evidence.** services/api/src/audit.py:682-714 `redact_owner()` is a single statement: `UPDATE audit_events_v2 SET actor_principal='erased', affected_owner='erased', resource_id='', request_id=%s, metadata='{}'::jsonb, redacted_at = now() WHERE affected_owner = %s AND redacted_at IS NULL`, returning `cur.rowcount` as the proof of how many events were erased. Same shape at audit.py:1184-1190 for the retention sweep (`WHERE expires_at IS NOT NULL AND expires_at < now() AND redacted_at IS NULL`) and at erase_account.py:132-136. The design reason is at audit.py:684-696: deleting the owner's rows "punches holes in the middle of the chain, and a hole is indistinguishable from tampering". The append-only trigger then additionally verifies that a redaction cleared content and did not rewrite it (audit.py:543-573, checking seq/prev_hash/row_hash/integrity_hash/occurred_at/action/outcome are all unchanged), and `verify_chain` cross-checks the shape at audit.py:1056-1061.

**Impact.** In DynamoDB this becomes: Query a GSI on `affected_owner` (eventually consistent), paginate at 1 MB per page, issue one conditional UpdateItem per item, handle throttling, and resume from a checkpoint if it dies. It is no longer atomic and no longer countable — `rowcount` as the DPDP evidence of "N events erased" becomes "N events erased so far, probably". Worse, a concurrent audit append for that owner *during* the sweep lands after the scan cursor has passed and is never redacted, so the erasure silently misses events. The trigger-enforced "a redaction may only clear content, never rewrite what it records" (audit.py:566) has no equivalent at all — see the IAM finding.

**Recommendation.** Put a TTL attribute on audit items and let DynamoDB's TTL do retention expiry (free, but delete-only, so it conflicts with the chain-hole argument at audit.py:686 — you would have to accept prefix truncation, which `verify_chain` already tolerates at audit.py:1012). For owner erasure, write a resumable job with a checkpoint item and a second pass, and require the second pass to find zero before marking the erasure complete. Record honestly in docs/compliance/gdpr-dpdp.md that erasure is eventually consistent.

**Effort.** 3 weeks

> **Verifier correction.** Claim stands, with citation fixes: the retention sweep statement is at services/api/src/audit.py:1190-1196 (function `redact_expired` at :1170), not 1184-1190; the erasure runner is at services/api/**scripts**/erase_account.py:134-139, not src/erase_account.py:132-136; the "a redaction may only clear an event's content" RAISE is at audit.py:570, not :566. Also, the trigger (audit.py:543-573) enforces that the chain and record columns are unchanged and that an already-redacted row cannot be touched again — the separate check that the content was actually cleared is `is_redaction_shape` (audit.py:909), applied by verify_chain at ~audit.py:1055-1058. Everything else, including the verbatim SQL, the `cur.rowcount` return values, the chain-hole rationale, and verify_chain's tolerance of prefix truncation (audit.py:1011), is accurate.

### `positive` No SERIAL/BIGSERIAL columns and zero sequences in the live schema — all ids are already application-generated

**Evidence.** Verified against the live DB: `SELECT sequencename FROM pg_sequences WHERE schemaname='public'` returns nothing, and `pg_get_serial_sequence()` across every public column returns NULL for all of them. Ids are application-generated throughout: `'pi_' + uuid.uuid4().hex` (payments.py:149), `'sp-' + uuid.uuid4().hex` (payments.py:335), `f"te-{_uuid.uuid4().hex[:12]}"` (web360.py:2922), `'dt-' + uuid.uuid4().hex[:12]` (payments.py:357), `str(uuid.uuid5(uuid.NAMESPACE_URL, ...))` for the deterministic operation id (payments.py:115). The only monotonic sequence anywhere is `audit_events_v2.seq`, driven by the chain head. Four composite primary keys — idempotency_keys(owner_user_id,key), parcel_fields(parcel_id,field_key), demo_stamp(record_id,table_name), service_order_intents(owner_user_id,intent_key) — map directly onto partition-key/sort-key pairs with no work.

**Impact.** This removes what is usually the single largest DynamoDB migration tax. There are no gap-free invoice numbers, no `nextval()` call sites, no ordering that depends on an id being sequential, and no id-rewriting pass across 83 tables. `uuid5` at payments.py:115 is particularly good — the operation id is a pure function of (intent_id, kind), so it is already idempotent under replay with no coordination.

**Recommendation.** Keep this property. When you add ULIDs for the sort-position fix (see the MAX(sort) finding), generate them client-side too. Do not introduce a counter table to emulate sequences under any circumstances.

**Effort.** none — already done

Ids are application-generated: `'pi_' + uuid.uuid4().hex` (payments.py:152), `'sp-' + uuid.uuid4().hex` (payments.py:328), `'dt-' + uuid.uuid4().hex[:12]` (payments.py:355), `f'te-{uuid4().hex[:12]}'` (web360.py:2922), and `uuid.uuid5(NAMESPACE_URL, f'pattadar:{intent_id}:{kind}')` for the deterministic operation id (payments.py:117). ### `positive` The compare-and-set idioms already in the code translate 1:1, and the repo's own architecture doc already says not to migrate the transactional half

**Evidence.** Several write paths are already conditional-write shaped: `ON CONFLICT (owner_user_id, key) DO NOTHING` + rowcount as the claim (main.py:6934-6939); the stamp-guarded settle that refuses to clobber a recycled claim — `WHERE owner_user_id=%s AND key=%s AND created_at=%s` (main.py:7031, 7038, 7043); `ON CONFLICT(intent_id,kind) DO NOTHING` (payments.py:116); `ON CONFLICT (idempotency_key) WHERE idempotency_key <> '' DO NOTHING` (web360.py:3004); `ON CONFLICT (scope_key,revision) DO NOTHING` (governance.py:726); the `UPDATE ... WHERE id=%s AND order_id IS NULL` guard at payments.py:437 and `WHERE id=%s AND status<>'done'` at payments.py:465. A tally of all 57 `ON CONFLICT` sites shows 14 on `(id)` and 3 on `(key)` — over a third are plain primary-key conflicts that become `attribute_not_exists`. And apps/university/ARCHITECTURE.md:128 already states the right conclusion: "Existing Pattadar relational infrastructure is appropriate for early catalog authoring, payments, workforce checks, and workflows needing multi-row transactions. High-write learner state can move independently," with ARCHITECTURE.md:280 repeating "Keep catalog authoring and financial workflows relational unless their access patterns clearly justify a move."

**Impact.** The engineer who wrote these already thought in terms of atomic claims rather than read-then-write, which is why so much of the non-money code would port cleanly. It also means the split is already documented: someone on this project reached the correct architectural conclusion and wrote it down before the founder proposed the opposite.

**Recommendation.** Adopt the document you already have. Move university learning state, tutor sessions, record/parcel/document read models and any high-write append-only data to DynamoDB — those are well-suited and the key designs at ARCHITECTURE.md:132-190 are sound. Keep audit, payments, ticketing, account erasure and the gateway's storage tree on Postgres. This is not a compromise position; it is the correct one, and it cuts the migration from roughly six months to roughly six weeks.

**Effort.** none for the decision; ~6 weeks for the read-model half vs ~6 months for everything

---

## Concrete DynamoDB table design

I designed the model and then tried to break it. The good news is real: the consumer half of the schema partitions cleanly by owner. I checked the thing that usually kills single-table designs — whether a shared record must be readable by many principals — and it does not. Sharing here is by snapshot (`shared_kits` carries `recipient_user_id` plus fully denormalized display fields, `shared_kit_items` has no `document_id` back-pointer) or by bearer token (`share_links.token_hash`, `invitations.token`), and `groups` is a sub-scope inside one owner, not a cross-owner grant — every group query is `WHERE group_id=%s AND owner_user_id=%s`. There is no group-members table holding a foreign user id. So `PK=OWNER#<uid>` is genuinely viable for 48 of 85 tables, and measured max row size is 506 bytes — the 400 KB item limit is nowhere near a problem. The bad news is equally real: the marketplace half is deliberately cross-tenant (`SELECT * FROM work_requests WHERE closed=false` at web360.py:4098, `GROUP BY area_key, kind` at 4434), Postgres is enforcing four partial-unique invariants that DynamoDB cannot express and that the code explicitly calls "THE BACKSTOP", the DPDP export/erasure is derived at runtime from `information_schema` and has no DynamoDB equivalent, and the 290 GB land corpus runs on pg_trgm fuzzy search and a pgvector HNSW index over 44M vectors that DynamoDB simply does not have. My honest split: ~45% good fit, ~35% forced-but-workable, ~20% actively wrong. And the whole reference/geography "database" is 104,970 bytes — smaller than a single DynamoDB item.

### `blocker` Four partial-UNIQUE constraints are load-bearing correctness invariants that DynamoDB cannot express — one is explicitly documented as the last line of defence

**Evidence.** services/api/src/associates.py:399-405 — "THE BACKSTOP. Even if every line of application logic were wrong, two LIVE accepted offers on one ticket cannot exist." implemented as `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_dispatch_one_accept ON ticket_dispatches (ticket_id) WHERE offer_state = 'accepted'`. Also `uq_dispatch_one_per_round ON ticket_dispatches(ticket_id, associate_id, offer_round) WHERE purpose='offer' AND associate_id<>''` (associates.py:410-412), `parcel_photos_one_cover_idx ON parcel_photos(parcel_id) WHERE is_cover`, `property_photos_one_cover_idx ON property_photos(property_id) WHERE is_cover`, `idx_family_self_grp ON family_members(owner_user_id,group_id) WHERE is_self`, `account_erasure_active ON account_erasure_jobs(owner_user_id) WHERE status<>'completed'`, and `storage_nodes_unique_name ON storage_nodes(owner_id,parent_id,lower(name)) NULLS NOT DISTINCT WHERE trashed_at IS NULL` (services/gateway/sql/init.sql:41-43). Total: 34 non-PK unique indexes across the 85 public tables, 26 of which are partial. DynamoDB enforces uniqueness on exactly one thing: the primary key of a table or index. GSIs do not enforce uniqueness at all, and GSI reads are eventually consistent only — there is no ConsistentRead on a GSI.

**Impact.** Two associates can accept the same survey job. The read-then-write guard has no backstop because the GSI you would read (`idx_dispatch_associate` -> a GSI on associate_id+offer_state) is eventually consistent, so under concurrent accepts both readers see 'no accepted row' and both write. Today Postgres raises a unique violation and rolls back the filing, ledger and trail together; the code at associates.py:406-409 says an index that did not exclude the wrong rows "would raise inside accept_ticket and roll back filing, ledger and trail" — it is relying on that. Same failure shape for two cover photos on one parcel, two `is_self` members in one group, and two concurrent erasure jobs for one account (a DPDP problem).

**Recommendation.** Replace each with an explicit lock item written in the same TransactWriteItems as the state change: `PK=TICKET#<id>, SK=LOCK#ACCEPTED` with `ConditionExpression: attribute_not_exists(SK)`. Unassign deletes the lock item in the same transaction. Same for `LOCK#COVER` on a record, `LOCK#SELF#<gid>` on an owner, `LOCK#ERASE` on an owner, and `LOCK#NAME#<lower(name)>` per folder for storage_nodes. That is 7 new lock-item lifecycles, each with a rename/trash/untrash path that must acquire and release correctly — untrashing a file whose name was reused since must now fail, which it does not have to today. Budget for the fact that a bug here is silently wrong rather than loudly wrong: the database stops telling you.

**Effort.** 3-4 weeks to implement, and permanently weaker than the index it replaces

> **Verifier correction.** Keep the finding, with three fixes. (a) Counts: the live `pattadar` public schema has 85 tables with 27 non-primary-key unique indexes, 16 of them partial (not 34/26). The 16 partial uniques are account_erasure_active, idx_districts_lgd_code, idx_family_self_grp, idx_mandals_lgd_code, idx_share_links_token, idx_states_lgd_code, idx_ticket_dispatches_token, idx_villages_lgd_code, parcel_photos_one_cover_idx, property_photos_one_cover_idx, uq_associates_contact, uq_associates_token, uq_desk_tasks_dedupe, uq_dispatch_one_accept, uq_dispatch_one_per_round, uq_service_payments_idem; plus storage_nodes_unique_name in the gateway DB. (b) The double-accept race is a future risk, not a current one: no application code writes offer_state anywhere in services/ — the accept mutation lives only in docs/specs/2026-09-13-associates-marketplace.md:505-506 (spec line 738 defines test_race_has_exactly_one_winner, unimplemented). The index and its boot-time assertion (associates.py:997-1001 REQUIRED_INDEXES) ship ahead of the feature. So the blocker is "the marketplace as designed cannot be built on DynamoDB without lock items", not "DynamoDB breaks a shipped feature". (c) The strongest live evidence

### `blocker` The 290 GB land corpus runs on pg_trgm fuzzy search and a pgvector HNSW index — DynamoDB has neither, and the replacement costs more than the Postgres it replaces

**Evidence.** `land.boundary_vectors` is partitioned bv_p0..bv_p7, each 28 GB with `CREATE INDEX bv_pN_embedding_idx USING hnsw (embedding halfvec_cosine_ops) WITH (m='16', ef_construction='64')` — 44M vectors, 224 GB. services/assistant/src/public_records/service.py:492-506 queries it with `embedding <=> %s ... ORDER BY h.dist LIMIT %(limit)s`. `land.party` (140.5M rows, 35 GB) carries `idx_party_name_trgm gin (name_norm gin_trgm_ops)` and service.py:393/403/548 uses `p.name_norm %% %(query)s` with `similarity(p.name_norm, %(query)s) AS score` and a tunable `pg_trgm.similarity_threshold` (0.35-0.6, set per-statement at service.py:413/556). `land.real_estate_records` (45.1M rows, 28 GB) has `idx_village_trgm gin (village_norm gin_trgm_ops)` plus five composite btrees, and service.py:197-212 does `WHERE village_norm %% %(n)s GROUP BY village_norm ORDER BY sim DESC`. service.py:455 and :755 compute `percentile_cont(0.5) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY market_value)`.

**Impact.** DynamoDB has no vector similarity, no trigram/fuzzy matching, no substring search, no GROUP BY and no percentile. Fuzzy party-name search is the assistant's core feature and there is no DynamoDB expression of it at any price. The replacement is OpenSearch Serverless, whose floor is 4 OCU (2 indexing + 2 search) at roughly $0.24/OCU-hour = about $700/month before you index a byte — for a pre-launch product whose entire cost model (docs/specs/2026-09-19-platform-costing-model.md, line 141) budgets ₹43,070/month at 1,000 accounts. Loading the corpus into DynamoDB, purely as a cost exercise: ~140.5M party items at 1 WRU each, ~45.1M record items at 1 WRU, ~44M 2,120-byte vector items at 3 WRU = roughly 320M write request units, about $400 one-time at us-east-1 rates, plus ~$110/month storage — and the assistant still would not work, because the vectors would be inert blobs.

**Recommendation.** Take the land schema off the table completely. It stays in PostgreSQL, full stop. A 290 GB read-mostly corpus on a small instance with gp3 storage is roughly $85/month and every query already works. If the rest of the platform moves to DynamoDB you now operate two datastores permanently — which is a legitimate outcome, but name it as a decision rather than discovering it in month four.

**Effort.** n/a — this is a scope exclusion, not a task

> **Verifier correction.** Same finding, with three details tightened: (1) the party-name similarity threshold is not a 0.35-0.6 band — service.py:556 hardcodes 0.35 for the timeline query, while service.py:413 passes a caller-supplied value floored at `_MIN_PARTY_SIMILARITY = 0.5` (service.py:33), default 0.6; (2) service.py:455 computes `percentile_cont(0.5) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY {rate})` over a dynamically chosen rate column — only service.py:755 orders by `market_value`; (3) the OpenSearch Serverless floor cited (~$700/mo at 4 OCU) is the production/redundant minimum; a non-redundant dev collection can start at 2 OCU (~$350/mo), which weakens the number but not the argument.

### `major` The marketplace half is cross-tenant by design and cannot live in an owner-partitioned table — you need at least two tables

**Evidence.** services/api/src/web360.py:4098 `SELECT * FROM work_requests WHERE closed = false AND status IN ('placed','sent') ORDER BY created_at LIMIT %s` — every owner's tickets. web360.py:4434 `SELECT area_key, kind, COUNT(*) AS n FROM work_requests WHERE closed = false AND area_key <> '' GROUP BY area_key, kind`. web360.py:6290-6297 three `COUNT(*) FILTER (...)` over all open tickets in one statement. web360.py:3733 `SELECT assignee_ref, ... FROM work_requests WHERE assignee_ref = ANY(%s) GROUP BY assignee_ref`. web360.py:3812-3819 a LEFT JOIN over a grouped COUNT to build the associate roster. `work_requests` carries 8 indexes — the most of any table — keyed on `(owner_user_id,closed)`, `(owner_user_id,entity_id,service_key)`, `area_key`, `assignee_ref`, `(owner_user_id,batch_id)`, `(dispatch_state,dispatch_at)`, `(owner_user_id,status)`. `ticket_dispatches` has 7.

**Impact.** An owner-partitioned table cannot answer "all open jobs in Shamirpet" without a Scan. Forcing the marketplace into Table 1 means either a Scan per desk page load (at 1,000 accounts that is cheap; at 100,000 it is not, and Scan reads every item including the 40+ other entity types sharing the partition) or sparse GSIs whose keys are set only on ticket items — which works, but means Table 1 carries the union of both index sets and every consumer write pays for GSIs it will never use.

**Recommendation.** Split: Table 2 `pattadar-market`, PK=`TICKET#<id>`/`ASSOC#<id>`, SK=`META`|`DISP#<id>`|`EV#<ts>`|`DISC#<discipline>`|`AREA#<level>#<nameKey>`|`CRED#<id>`. That makes `uq_assoc_discipline(associate_id,discipline)` and `uq_assoc_area(associate_id,level,name_key)` free SKs. Six GSIs: `AREA#<areaKey>#<kind>` sparse on open tickets, `ASSIGNEE#<ref>` SK=`status#created_at`, `DSTATE#<dispatch_state>` SK=`dispatch_at` sparse, `OSTATE#offered` SK=`offer_expires_at` sparse (the expiry sweeper), `STATUS#<status>` SK=`status_at` for the desk queues at 4098/4105, `OWNER#<uid>` SK=`closed#created_at` for the owner's own list. The `GROUP BY` aggregates have no DynamoDB equivalent at all — see the counters finding.

**Effort.** the split itself is a design decision; the marketplace rewrite is 2-3 months

AND status IN ('assigned','on_site','changes') AND COALESCE(NULLIF(status_at,''), created_at) < %s`. Both branches are owner-less and the docstring at 4086 states the intent explicitly ("across every owner"), so the finding's conclusion stands unchanged. Worth adding to the evidence: web360.py:3748 is a third cross-assignee aggregate (`GROUP BY assignee_ref, kind`), and web360.py:6300 aggregates `associates` the same way — so the aggregate problem is broader than the four statements cited.

### `major` 18 GROUP BY and 242 aggregate call sites have no DynamoDB equivalent — you must build and maintain counter items plus a Streams pipeline

**Evidence.** 18 `GROUP BY` and 242 aggregate-function occurrences across services/api/src/*.py. The group dashboard at services/api/src/main.py:1543-1575 fires eight separate COUNT/SUM statements per page load (`count(*) FROM family_members WHERE group_id=%s AND is_self=false`, `count(*) FROM passbooks WHERE group_id=%s AND owner_user_id=%s`, `SUM(...)` over beneficiary shares at 1560, etc.). `SELECT ... FROM documents WHERE owner_user_id=%s GROUP BY shelf` for the vault shelf counts. web360.py:4434 `GROUP BY area_key, kind`. services/assistant/src/public_records/service.py:455 and :755 use `percentile_cont(0.5) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY market_value)` — a median.

**Impact.** DynamoDB has no COUNT, SUM, GROUP BY or percentile. Every one of these becomes either (a) read all matching items and aggregate in the Lambda — at 1 MB per Query page, a vault with 2,000 documents is 3+ paginated round-trips to produce eight shelf counts, or (b) a denormalized counter item updated with ADD in the same transaction as every write, or (c) a DynamoDB Streams -> Lambda materializer. Option (b) doubles your transaction item count (TransactWriteItems caps at 100 items and 4 MB, and costs 2x a normal write). Option (c) is new infrastructure you now operate. The median in the assistant is not expressible at all under any of the three without reading the full result set.

**Recommendation.** For the group dashboard and vault shelves, maintain counter items (`PK=OWNER#<uid>, SK=COUNT#SHELF`, `SK=COUNT#GRP#<gid>`) updated with `ADD` inside the same TransactWriteItems as the entity write. Accept that a counter that drifts has no `SELECT count(*)` to reconcile against, so you also need a periodic full-scan reconciler. For the desk aggregates (4434, 6290-6297) use a Streams-triggered materialized view. For the assistant's percentiles: do not migrate that code — see the land corpus finding.

**Effort.** 6-8 weeks for counters + reconciler + Streams materializer, plus permanent operational surface

### `major` The hash-chained audit ledger requires a global gapless sequence — DynamoDB Streams guarantees ordering only per partition key, so the control has to be redesigned

**Evidence.** services/api/src/audit.py:851-891: the worker takes the outbox row `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, then `SELECT seq, head_hash FROM audit_chain_head WHERE id=1 FOR UPDATE` (line 863), computes `row_hash = chain_hash(prev_hash, integrity_hash, seq)`, inserts into `audit_events_v2` with `seq, prev_hash, row_hash`, and updates the head — all in one transaction. The comment at audit.py:488-490 states the lock exists so that two workers do not "both read the same prev_hash and both claim seq N, forking the chain into two branches". `audit_chain_head` is a one-row table with `CHECK (id = 1)`. `audit_events_v2` has a BEFORE DELETE OR UPDATE trigger (`audit_events_v2_append_only`) and a UNIQUE index on `seq`. The module cites NIST SP 800-53 AU-9 / AU-11 as its baseline.

**Impact.** In DynamoDB the head becomes a single item, so every audit event serializes through one conditional update on one partition key. That is functionally correct but it is a hot item by construction, and the retry storm under concurrency is unbounded. Worse, the natural DynamoDB pattern — Streams plus a Lambda sequencer — cannot deliver a global order: Streams guarantees ordering within a partition key only, and shards are per-partition. To get a globally gapless `seq` you need a single-threaded sequencer, which means Kinesis with exactly one shard (1,000 records/sec, 1 MB/sec ceiling) or a Lambda with reserved concurrency 1 draining a FIFO queue. Either way you have added a single point of failure to the control that the compliance docs point at.

**Recommendation.** Redesign to per-owner chains: `PK=OWNER#<affected_owner>, SK=EVT#<occurred_at>#<event_id>` with `seq` and `prev_hash` scoped to that owner. That is a legitimate design — tamper-evidence per subject is what DPDP and AU-9 actually care about — but it is a change to a reviewed control and must go back through docs/compliance/soc2-controls.md, not be slipped in as a migration detail. The one thing DynamoDB does strictly better here: `expires_at` becomes a native TTL attribute and the retention job (RETENTION_DAYS 1095/1095/365 at audit.py:65-70) deletes itself for free.

**Effort.** 3-4 weeks plus a compliance re-review

> **Verifier correction.** The hash-chained audit ledger requires a global gapless sequence, and DynamoDB Streams orders only within a partition key, so the control has to be redesigned. Evidence: services/api/src/audit.py:851-891 (outbox row FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, then `SELECT seq, head_hash FROM audit_chain_head WHERE id=1 FOR UPDATE` at :863, chain_hash at :867, head advance at :889 guarded by `cur.rowcount == 1`), rationale comment at :488-492, one-row head with `CHECK (id = 1)` at :494-499, unique index on seq at :480, append-only trigger `audit_events_v2_guard` calling function `audit_events_v2_append_only()` at :528/:583-585, NIST AU-9/AU-11 baseline at :18-20, RETENTION_DAYS 1095/1095/365 at :67-72. Per-owner chains (PK=OWNER#<affected_owner>) remain a legitimate redesign but must go back through docs/compliance/soc2-controls.md.

DROP the TTL sweetener — it is backwards. The retention job at audit.py:1190-1196 deliberately does not delete expired rows; it redacts content in place and keeps `seq`/`row_hash`, because (comment at :1184-1189) "expiry order is NOT chain order: deleting expired rows would punch holes through the middle of the chain and every later verification would report tampering that

### `major` DPDP export and erasure are derived at runtime from information_schema — DynamoDB has no catalog, so a fail-safe control becomes a hand-maintained list

**Evidence.** services/api/src/account.py:132-138 `catalog_for()` reads `SELECT table_name,column_name FROM information_schema.columns WHERE table_schema='public'`. account.py:92-130 `ownership_predicates()` then builds, per table, a predicate from a proven owner column (`owner_user_id` / `recipient_user_id`), a special case for `users`/`audit_events`/`audit_events_v2`/`audit_outbox`, and 20 declared CHILD_LINKS edges (account.py:58-88). account.py:159-171 `export_data()` iterates every table in that map. The docstring calls it "A schema-derived allowlist: only proven owner columns/known children." `OMIT_COLUMNS` plus an `_enc` suffix rule (account.py:153-156) strip secrets automatically.

**Impact.** Today, adding a new table with an `owner_user_id` column automatically puts it in the subject-access export and in the erasure sweep — the control fails safe. In DynamoDB there is no `information_schema`, no column list, and no per-table iteration: entity types are just SK prefixes with no registry. The mechanism becomes a hardcoded list of SK prefixes in Python. The first new entity someone adds and forgets to register is silently excluded from a DPDP export and survives an erasure request. That is a regulatory finding, not a bug report. The `_enc` suffix rule also stops working as a guarantee, because DynamoDB items are schemaless — an item can carry an attribute no registry knows about.

**Recommendation.** If you go ahead, the owner-partitioned design partly saves you: erasure becomes `Query(PK=OWNER#<uid>)` then BatchWriteItem the whole collection, which is far cleaner than 48 DELETE statements. But you must add a machine-checked entity registry (SK prefix -> aggregate -> export/omit rules) with a test that fails CI when a writer emits an SK prefix not in the registry. Do not rely on a hand-maintained list. Note that the cross-owner items (Table 2 marketplace, audit) still need targeted deletes and are not covered by the partition query.

**Effort.** 2-3 weeks for the registry plus the CI guard; the loss of fail-safe behaviour is permanent

> **Verifier correction.** Same claim, with corrected line anchors and one addition: `catalog_for()` is services/api/src/account.py:140-145 (the information_schema SELECT is line 141), not 132-138. `ownership_predicates()` is account.py:94-137, not 92-130. CHILD_LINKS is account.py:60-92 (comment block starts 56), not 58-88. OMIT_COLUMNS/_enc is account.py:150-156 and `export_data()` is account.py:159-171 (as cited). Additions: (a) `ownership_predicates` also carries a hand-declared `invitations` branch at account.py:121-126 mapping six (scope_type, parent) pairs — one more place that is already a manual list today and would grow in DynamoDB; (b) the erasure side is not just implied — services/api/scripts/erase_account.py:21 imports `ownership_predicates` and :29-44 re-derives the same table map from information_schema, so export and erasure share one catalog-derived control; (c) the "48 DELETE statements" figure is confirmed: 48 public tables carry `owner_user_id` or `recipient_user_id`.

### `major` Reference and geography data does not belong in DynamoDB — the whole set is 102 KB and fits inside a single DynamoDB item

**Evidence.** Row counts: states 36, districts 28, mandals 686, sro_offices 297, deed_types 115, fee_schedule 115, villages 0 (LGD all-India would be ~650k). Total raw bytes across those six tables: 104,970. Read patterns are uniformly `WHERE parent_id=%s AND active=true ORDER BY name` (districts by state_id, mandals by district_id, villages by mandal_id) plus `FROM sro_offices ORDER BY code` and `FROM fee_schedule ORDER BY reg_type_en, nature_en` — full reads of 297 and 115 rows. The one awkward shape is `SELECT ... FROM fee_schedule WHERE id=%s OR nature_en=%s OR reg_type_en=%s LIMIT 1` — an OR across three columns. `villages` carries 25 columns of sync provenance (source_id, source_url, row_hash, first_seen_at, last_seen_at, retired_at, last_sync_run_id) feeding reference_data_sources / _changes / _sync_runs.

**Impact.** Putting this in DynamoDB buys nothing and costs real complexity. The three-column OR on fee_schedule needs three GSIs or a Scan of 115 items. `ORDER BY name` requires name to be in the sort key, which means a second index for `ORDER BY code`. The reference_data sync pipeline (change detection via row_hash, retirement via active=false) is a set-difference job — natural in SQL, awkward as a DynamoDB scan-and-diff. Meanwhile the entire payload is smaller than one 400 KB item and changes a handful of times a year.

**Recommendation.** Do not migrate reference or geography to DynamoDB. Publish it as versioned JSON in S3 (`reference/v<syncRunId>/telangana.json`), load it into process memory at boot, and serve every lookup from a dict. That makes `fee_schedule WHERE id OR nature_en OR reg_type_en` a three-key dict probe instead of three GSIs, and the cascade `state -> district -> mandal -> village` a pointer walk instead of four Queries. Keep the sync-and-diff job wherever the authoritative relational store ends up. If all-India villages (~650k rows, ~50 MB) later exceed what you want in memory, shard the S3 objects by district — still not a DynamoDB case.

**Effort.** 1-2 weeks, and it removes 11 tables from the migration scope

> **Verifier correction.** Identical to the finding except: `villages` currently holds 17 rows (not 0), and the true raw total across the six/seven tables is 106,101 bytes, not 104,970 — the reviewer's sum omitted villages' 1,131 bytes. Both numbers are still ~100 KB, well under the single 400 KB DynamoDB item limit, so the recommendation is unchanged.

### `major` Honest fit rating: ~45% good, ~35% forced, ~20% wrong — and the forced 35% is where 12 months of solo work goes

**Evidence.** Good fit (roughly 40 of 85 tables): the owner record graph (passbooks, parcels, parcel_fields, parcel_owners, parcel_photos, boundary_marks, properties, property_owners, property_photos, land_features, land_expenses, capital_costs, purchase_lots, projects, notes, favourites, record_tags, record_people, waiting_items, documents, document_versions, document_links, family_members, family_notifiers, groups, share_links, shared_kits, shared_kit_items, shared_kit_checks, idempotency_keys, payment_intents, payment_operations, account_consents, notification_log) — all owner-partitioned, all key-value access, all under 512 bytes measured. Plus audit_events_v2 where native TTL replaces the retention job outright. Forced but workable (roughly 20 tables): work_requests, ticket_dispatches, ticket_events, ticket_deliverables, desk_tasks, service_requests/batches/order_intents, associates + its 6 children, inactivity_*, governance_policy_* — needs 7 lock items, counter items, a Streams materializer and a second table. Actively wrong (roughly 14 tables plus the land schema): states, districts, mandals, villages, sro_offices, deed_types, fee_schedule, market_values, village_maps, reference_data_sources/_changes/_sync_runs (102 KB total), audit_chain_head, plus all of `land` (290 GB, pgvector + pg_trgm). Scale context: docs/specs/2026-09-19-platform-costing-model.md line 141 models 1,000 accounts at ₹43,070/month, and docs/runbooks/migration.md line 5 records the actual production data as "228 objects, 1,397,852,638 bytes, single owner".

**Impact.** The rewrite surface is 522 SELECT sites, 28 JOINs, 25 IN(SELECT) subqueries, 18 GROUP BYs, 242 aggregate calls, 53 transaction blocks, 46 FOR UPDATE, 53 ON CONFLICT — spread across ~20,000 lines in main.py (7,445), web360.py (9,865), associates.py, audit.py and account.py alone. There is no ORM to swap an adapter under; it is raw psycopg SQL end to end. This is a rewrite, not a port: 6-12 months of solo work. The thing you are buying it with is DynamoDB's scale-and-no-ops story, at a scale of 1,000 accounts and 228 production objects, where a single small Postgres instance is idle. Runtime cost is not the argument either — at that volume the DynamoDB bill is a few dollars a month, and so is the RDS bill.

**Recommendation.** Do not migrate the platform to DynamoDB now. The design above is sound and I would build it that way if the constraint were 10M accounts. At 1,000 accounts pre-launch, the correct move is: (1) keep PostgreSQL as the system of record; (2) adopt the pieces of this design that are free wins today — the S3 presigned-upload path (services/gateway/src/storage.py already does SSE-KMS at line ~165), the materialized `path` column on storage_nodes, and a TTL-style retention sweep on audit; (3) if you want one DynamoDB table to prove the pattern, make it the audit event store, where the owner partition is natural, the items are append-only, and native TTL genuinely deletes the retention job. Revisit the full migration when a single table crosses ~100 GB or a single partition of load exceeds what one Postgres writer serves — neither is within two years of the current 1.4 GB / 228-object reality.

**Effort.** full migration: 6-12 months solo (rewrite). Recommended subset: 3-4 weeks.

### `moderate` Cascade deletes are hand-written multi-statement transactions that exceed the 100-item TransactWriteItems limit on realistic data

**Evidence.** services/api/src/main.py:3264-3276 deletes a property as five statements in one `conn.transaction()`: `DELETE FROM property_owners WHERE property_id=%s`, `DELETE FROM documents WHERE property_id=%s`, `DELETE FROM document_parties WHERE document_id IN (subquery)`, `DELETE FROM registered_documents WHERE property_id=%s AND owner_user_id=%s`, `DELETE FROM properties WHERE id=%s`. main.py:3081-3083 does the same for a passbook via `DELETE FROM parcel_owners WHERE parcel_id IN (SELECT id FROM parcels WHERE passbook_id=%s)`. main.py:4342-4347 deletes a group across six tables. There are 53 `conn.transaction()` blocks, 46 `FOR UPDATE`, 4 `SKIP LOCKED`, 53 `ON CONFLICT` and 25 `IN (SELECT ...)` subqueries in services/api/src. services/gateway/sql/init.sql:24 relies on `parent_id UUID REFERENCES storage_nodes(id) ON DELETE CASCADE` for recursive folder deletion.

**Impact.** DynamoDB's TransactWriteItems caps at 100 items and 4 MB, and the same item cannot appear twice in one transaction. A property with 150 documents cannot be deleted atomically — it becomes two batches, and a failure between them leaves orphaned document_parties pointing at deleted documents. Postgres gives this for free today. The storage_nodes tree has no cascade at all in DynamoDB: deleting a folder means recursively querying children level by level, N round-trips deep, with no atomicity across levels. The 46 `FOR UPDATE` pessimistic locks all become optimistic version-attribute retries, each of which is a code change with its own conflict-handling path.

**Recommendation.** Make cascade deletes explicitly two-phase: mark the aggregate root `deleted=true` in a single conditional write (immediately hiding it from every read path), then let a Streams-triggered reaper sweep children in batches with idempotent deletes. Never promise atomicity you cannot deliver. For storage_nodes, store a materialized `path` attribute on every node so a subtree delete is one Query on a `PATH#<prefix>` GSI with begins_with, rather than a recursive walk — this is worth doing regardless of whether you migrate.

**Effort.** 4-6 weeks across the ~20 cascade sites

> **Verifier correction.** Evidence stands with three detail fixes. (1) The storage_nodes cascade is services/gateway/sql/init.sql:25, not :24. (2) delete_group (main.py:4331-4348) touches eight tables, not six: it UPDATEs passbooks and properties to clear group_id, then DELETEs from family_notifiers, inactivity_capabilities, inactivity_deliveries, inactivity_escalations, family_members and groups — and it takes a SELECT ... FOR UPDATE on the group row first (4337). (3) document_parties hangs off registered_documents, not documents: main.py:3270-3273 is DELETE FROM document_parties WHERE document_id IN (SELECT id FROM registered_documents WHERE property_id=%s AND owner_user_id=%s). So the failure example should read: a property with, say, 80 registered deeds each carrying several parties, plus its filed documents and property_owners rows, exceeds 100 items in one TransactWriteItems; a split into batches can leave document_parties rows pointing at deleted registered_documents. Grep counts are marginally higher than claimed, not lower (55 conn.transaction(), 47 FOR UPDATE, 5 SKIP LOCKED).

### `moderate` GSI count is survivable only because entity types share indexes — and the 20-GSI quota leaves less headroom than it looks

**Evidence.** The schema carries 201 indexes: 119 unique (85 of them primary keys, 34 non-PK unique) and 82 non-unique secondary. Per-table worst cases: work_requests 8, ticket_dispatches 7, audit_events_v2 5, associates 5, family_members 5, documents 4, payment_intents 4, governance_policy_sets 4. Documents alone shows 8 distinct access patterns in the code (`WHERE owner_user_id`, `owner_user_id AND shelf`, `parcel_id`, `property_id`, `record_id`, `passbook_id OR parcel_id IN (...)`, `reading_id`, `order_ref`, plus `id = ANY(%s)`). DynamoDB allows 20 GSIs per table (default quota, raisable by support) and 5 LSIs (hard, and only at table creation). GSI reads are eventually consistent — there is no ConsistentRead option on a GSI.

**Impact.** In my proposed split, Table 1 needs roughly 7 GSIs (byRecord, byShelf, byToken, byGroup, byReading, byOrder, byErasureStatus) and Table 2 roughly 6-7. That fits under 20 with room, but only because a single-table design lets documents, photos, people, features and tags all share GSI1 `byRecord`. The moment an entity needs an index key that another entity already uses differently, you are adding a whole GSI for one entity type and paying for it on every write that happens to set that attribute. The eventual-consistency property is the sharper constraint: any read-then-write correctness check routed through a GSI is racy, which is exactly the failure mode in the unique-constraint finding above.

**Recommendation.** Budget 8 GSIs for Table 1 and 8 for Table 2 and hold that as a hard design budget, not a soft target. Write down, per GSI, which Postgres index it replaces and which entity types set its keys — a GSI whose keys are set by only one entity type is a signal the aggregate boundary is wrong. Keep every ownership and uniqueness check on the base table primary key with ConsistentRead, never on a GSI.

**Effort.** design discipline, ongoing

The index `idx_documents_order_ref` exists (services/api/src/ticketing.py:212) but no query in services/api/src filters documents by order_ref — the column is only written (services/api/src/web360.py:5586). Documents still shows 8 distinct read patterns without it.

(2) State that the index census understates DynamoDB GSI pressure rather than bounding it. documents has only 4 Postgres indexes; the code queries it by record_id (web360.py:5073), property_id (main.py:1670), parcel_id (main.py:2349) and passbook_id (main.py:1828) with no index behind any of them — Postgres seq-scans these today. In DynamoDB each is a GSI or a Scan, so the 201-index count is a floor on the number of access paths that must become GSIs, not a ceiling.

(3) Say "tables with 4 or more indexes" rather than "worst cases", since document_links, service_payments and document_read_jobs also sit at 4 and were omitted.

Everything else — 201 indexes (85 PK / 34 unique non-PK / 82 non-unique), the per-table counts, the 20-GSI soft quota, the 5-LSI creation-time hard li

### `positive` The design works: sharing is by snapshot and token, not by grant — so PK=OWNER#<uid> is actually possible

**Evidence.** `shared_kits` (psql \d) carries `recipient_user_id` plus denormalized `title, headline, sender_name, sender_initials, list_line, asked_price, photo_count, feature_count` — a copy, not a pointer. `shared_kit_items` has only `id, kit_id, title, shelf, note, verdict, sort` — no `document_id` back into the sender's data. `share_links` is bearer-token (`idx_share_links_token UNIQUE btree(token_hash) WHERE token_hash<>''`) with a `manifest` JSON, read by an anonymous viewer. Every group-scoped query is owner-scoped too: services/api/src/main.py:1516, 1545, 1548, 1556, 1571 all read `WHERE owner_user_id=%s AND group_id=%s`. `groups` has 7 columns and one `owner_user_id`; there is no group_members table carrying a second user id. 48 of 85 tables carry `owner_user_id` or `recipient_user_id` directly, and services/api/src/account.py:92-130 proves the remaining ones reach an owner through a declared parent chain at most 3 deep (documents -> parcels -> passbooks).

**Impact.** This is the question that decides whether a single-table design is possible at all, and the answer is yes for the consumer product. A shared record never needs to be readable from a second principal's partition, so there is no fan-out-on-write problem, no ACL item explosion, and no need for a permissions GSI. Concretely: Table 1 `pattadar-owner`, PK=`OWNER#<uid>`, SK hierarchical — `PROFILE`, `PB#<pbId>`, `PB#<pbId>#PARCEL#<pid>`, `PB#<pbId>#PARCEL#<pid>#FIELD#<key>`, `PB#<pbId>#PARCEL#<pid>#PHOTO#<id>`, `PROP#<id>`, `PROP#<id>#PHOTO#<id>`, `DOC#<id>`, `GRP#<gid>`, `GRP#<gid>#MEM#<mid>`, `PAY#<intentId>`, `PAY#<intentId>#OP#<kind>`, `IDEM#<key>`, `SHARE#<linkId>`. Three Postgres unique constraints become free: `parcel_fields_pkey(parcel_id,field_key)`, `payment_operations(intent_id,kind)`, `idempotency_keys(owner_user_id,key)` are all just SKs. The `parcels JOIN passbooks` ownership check that appears at main.py:3437, main.py:5188 and web360.py:6753/6933 disappears entirely — a parcel's owner is its partition key, so `_record_kind()` becomes a single GetItem instead of two round-trips.

**Recommendation.** Adopt PK=`OWNER#<uid>` for the consumer aggregate and keep the hierarchical SK. Add GSI1 `byRecord` (PK=`REC#<recordId>`, SK=`<TYPE>#<id>`, project ALL) to serve the six record-attached patterns I found on documents (`WHERE record_id=%s`, `WHERE property_id=%s`, `WHERE parcel_id=%s`), plus record_people, land_features and record_tags. Add GSI3 `byToken` (PK=`TOKEN#<sha256>`) to serve all five token_hash lookups (share_links, ticket_dispatches, associates, inactivity_capabilities, invitations) with one index. Do not put the marketplace in this table — see the next finding.

**Effort.** design: done; implementation of the owner aggregate alone: 2-3 months

> **Verifier correction.** PK=`OWNER#<uid>` with a hierarchical SK is viable for the consumer aggregate, and the three Postgres unique constraints (`parcel_fields(parcel_id,field_key)`, `payment_operations(intent_id,kind)`, `idempotency_keys(owner_user_id,key)`) do become free sort keys. Ownership is owner-scoped throughout (48 of 85 tables carry `owner_user_id`/`recipient_user_id` directly; the rest reach an owner through the declared CHILD_LINKS chain in services/api/src/account.py, at most 3 hops, deepest being documents/parcel_fields -> parcels -> passbooks), and `_record_kind` (services/api/src/web360.py:6748-6759, called at 20+ sites) collapses from a `parcels JOIN passbooks` round-trip to a single GetItem. ### `positive` Item size is NOT a problem — measured max row is 506 bytes, and the entire reference dataset is 102 KB

**Evidence.** Measured with `pg_column_size` against the live DB: properties max 311 / avg 308 bytes; parcels max 490 / avg 253; associates max 322 / avg 290; work_requests max 425 / avg 353; audit_events_v2 max 506 / avg 447; property_photos max 332 / avg 308; mandals max 91 / avg 77. Caveat stated plainly: the local DB is nearly empty (properties 3 rows, users 0, documents 0, groups 0), so these are shape indicators not population maxima. I checked the columns that could realistically blow up and they do not: `parcels.boundary`/`shape` hold a surveyed ring, and services/api/src/fmb_geometry.py derives it from FMB sheet corner points (typically under 60 vertices, ~1.5 KB); the large village GeoJSON goes to object storage, not a row — `village_maps` is a 12-column manifest (key, village, file_name, source_name) and services/api/src/village_map.py:64 notes the ingest route has a 12 MB cap. Reference data: states+districts+mandals+sro_offices+deed_types+fee_schedule = 104,970 bytes total.

**Impact.** The 400 KB DynamoDB item limit — normally the first thing that kills a relational-to-DynamoDB port of a 43-column table — is a non-issue here. Item collections are also far from the 10 GB LSI limit. One real consequence of the small items goes the other way: DynamoDB bills writes in 1 KB units, so a 353-byte work_requests row costs a full WCU on the base table plus one per GSI whose keys it sets. With the 7 secondary indexes work_requests carries today, one logical ticket write is ~8 WRU. At 1,000 accounts (the scale in docs/specs/2026-09-19-platform-costing-model.md) that is pennies — roughly 400k WRU/month, about $0.50 at us-east-1 on-demand rates of $1.25/M WRU; ap-south-1 runs modestly higher.

**Recommendation.** Stop worrying about item size and put the effort into the index count and the aggregate rewrites instead. Do add a guard on `parcels.boundary`/`properties.shape` write paths capping vertex count, since nothing enforces it today and a pathological client could push a ring past 400 KB where Postgres would have accepted it.

**Effort.** 1 day for the vertex guard

## The 290GB land corpus

DynamoDB is the wrong home for this corpus, but not for the reason people usually give. The money is fine: the one-time load is $30–$800 depending on path, and steady-state storage is ~$70–$100/month — affordable. The argument is entirely about query shape. Six of the eight assistant tools are built on Postgres features that have no DynamoDB expression at all: pg_trgm similarity over 140.5M party names (services/assistant/src/public_records/service.py:402-413), pgvector HNSW ANN over 44.4M embeddings (service.py:490-510), GROUP BY (service.py:196-199), percentile_cont median (service.py:454-456), POSIX regex on survey_no (service.py:327-328), and a JOIN on (rid, s_no) (service.py:406-408). Porting means reimplementing a query engine over Scans. The single most important fact in this review is that 224 GB — 77% of the corpus — is boundary_vectors, of which 112 GB is HNSW index, and it serves exactly one tool that is disabled by default (settings.py:30, .env.example:26). Turn that off and the corpus is 66 GB, at which point every hosting option gets ~10x cheaper. The corpus should live in a different store from the transactional platform data — and it already does, behind its own DSN and a 114-line read-only interface, which is the best architectural decision in this part of the codebase and means this choice is independent of any DynamoDB decision on the OLTP side. Separately: the corpus has never been deployed to AWS under any engine — terraform provisions db.t4g.micro/20 GiB.

### `blocker` Hard blocker: fuzzy name search over 140.5M parties has no DynamoDB equivalent — a full Scan costs ~2.9M RRU and 23,200 sequential pages per search

**Evidence.** services/assistant/src/public_records/service.py:392-413 — `threshold = min(1.0, max(_MIN_PARTY_SIMILARITY, float(min_similarity)))`, `where = "p.name_norm %% %(query)s"`, executed with `local_settings={"pg_trgm.similarity_threshold": threshold}`. `_MIN_PARTY_SIMILARITY = 0.5` (service.py:33). A second, looser path at service.py:546-556 sets the threshold to 0.35. Backed by a real index: `idx_party_name_trgm gin (name_norm gin_trgm_ops)`, measured at 3,784 MB over 140,527,280 rows (psql pg_class/pg_indexes). DynamoDB's only string predicates are `=`, `<`/`>`, `begins_with` on a sort key, and `contains`/`begins_with` inside a FilterExpression — and a FilterExpression is evaluated AFTER items are read and AFTER capacity is consumed. Arithmetic for the Scan fallback: party item ≈ 84 B data + 41 B attribute names (measured: sum(length(column_name)) = 41 for land.party) + ~40 B PK/SK ≈ 165 B; 140.53M × 165 B = 23.2 GB; eventually-consistent reads = 23.2 GB / 4 KB / 2 = 2.9M RRU; at $0.125 per million RRU (us-east-1 post-Nov-2024 price; ap-south-1 ≈ $0.1425 — NEEDS CONFIRMATION) = $0.36–$0.41 per search; Scan pages at 1 MB, so 23,200 sequential round trips.

**Impact.** `find_properties_by_party` and the party leg of `generate_report` stop working. These are the two tools that answer the platform's core user question — "what land is registered to this name" — in a country where the same person appears as RAMA RAO, RAMARAO, RAMA RAO S/O, and a Telugu transliteration variant. The code sets the similarity threshold as low as 0.35 precisely because exact match is useless here. DynamoDB cannot do 0.35-similarity matching at any price.

**Recommendation.** Put `land.party` in OpenSearch, which is purpose-built for exactly this (`match` with `fuzziness:AUTO`, or an edge-ngram analyzer on name_norm, with BM25 scoring replacing `similarity()`). 140.53M small docs index to roughly 15–25 GB. Managed domain, single `t3.medium.search` node pre-launch ≈ $51/month; 2 × `r6g.large.search` + 100 GB gp3 ≈ $280/month at scale (ap-south-1 rates — NEEDS CONFIRMATION). Avoid OpenSearch Serverless: it has a hard floor of 4 OCUs for a redundant collection (2 indexing + 2 search) at ~$0.24/OCU-hr, which is $700–790/month just to exist, or ~$350–395/month for a 2-OCU redundancy-disabled dev collection. That floor is disqualifying for a pre-launch solo founder.

**Effort.** 3 weeks

> **Verifier correction.** Evidence stands as written; two precision fixes. (1) `_MIN_PARTY_SIMILARITY = 0.5` (service.py:33) is a *floor* applied via `max()`, not the operating threshold — `find_properties_by_party` declares `min_similarity: float = 0.6` (service.py:386), so the effective threshold is 0.6 unless a caller passes something lower, which is then clamped up to 0.5. (2) The looser 0.35 path is `_party_report`, defined at service.py:533; its query spans 546-556 with `WHERE p.name_norm %% %(query)s` at 553, `local_settings={"pg_trgm.similarity_threshold": 0.35}` at 556, and `cap = 1000`. Additionally worth stating: the `local_settings` mechanism is genuinely applied — database.py:87-91 runs `SELECT set_config(%s, %s, true)` per entry on the pooled connection, so the threshold really is per-transaction pg_trgm state, not a no-op.

### `blocker` Hard blocker: every aggregation tool (count, SUM, count DISTINCT, percentile_cont median, six GROUP BY rollups) has to be reimplemented as full-partition reads — 254 MB and ~254 serial round trips for the largest village

**Evidence.** DynamoDB has zero server-side aggregation. The code depends on it throughout: service.py:281-283 `SELECT count(*)/COALESCE(SUM(market_value),0) ... FROM land.real_estate_records WHERE village_norm = ...`; service.py:454-456 `round(percentile_cont(0.5) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY market_value::numeric / extent_val))`; service.py:748-757 `count(DISTINCT rid)`, `count(*) FILTER (WHERE market_value > 0)`, and a median in one statement; service.py:761-786 six separate GROUP BY rollups (by txn_class, land_class, extent_unit, village, year) inside a single `generate_report` call. Measured skew: `select count(distinct village_norm) from land.real_estate_records` = 140,757; the largest village VIJAYAWADA(URBAN) has 333,797 records, GUNTUR (U) 229,527, and 681,917 records have a NULL village_norm. Arithmetic with pk=village_norm: rer item ≈ 340 B data + 393 B attribute names (measured exactly: sum(length(column_name)) = 393 across 40 columns) + ~40 B PK/SK ≈ 760 B; 333,797 × 760 B = 254 MB per village read; 254 MB / 4 KB / 2 = ~31,000 RRU; DynamoDB Query returns at most 1 MB per page, so ~254 sequential round trips; a single partition key is capped at 3,000 RCU/s, so 31,000 RRU against that key is ≥10 s of throughput time before network latency. `generate_report` does six of these.

**Impact.** p50 on `get_analytical_stats`, `get_price_rates` and `generate_report` goes from ~200 ms to tens of seconds, inside an assistant turn where the user is waiting. The standard DynamoDB answer — precompute rollups via Streams — fails here on two counts: `get_analytical_stats` (service.py:215-224) accepts any combination of 5 optional filters (year, transaction_type, transaction_class, land_class, sro), which is 2^5 = 32 filter shapes × 140,757 villages of counters to maintain; and medians do not compose from counters at all, so `percentile_cont` (service.py:455, 755-757) cannot be precomputed for arbitrary filter combinations no matter how many counters you keep.

**Recommendation.** Move `land.real_estate_records` to S3 + Parquet + Athena. This shape compresses well — 45.14M rows × 372 B measured average = 21 GB raw, and with ZSTD Parquet over these low-cardinality text columns (village, sro_name, land_class, txn_class, extent_unit) realistically 4–8x to 3–5 GB (ESTIMATE). Partition by dist_cd/year and sort by village_norm so row-group min/max pruning handles the village predicate; do not create 140,757 Hive partitions. Athena is $5.00 per TB scanned with a 10 MB minimum per query (NEEDS CONFIRMATION for ap-south-1, but us-east-1 and ap-south-1 are both $5/TB as far as I know). A village-scoped `generate_report` scans maybe 20–50 MB = $0.0001–$0.00025 per report; storage is 5 GB S3 Standard at ~$0.025/GB-month ap-south-1 = ~$0.13/month. That is roughly four orders of magnitude cheaper than any always-on database for these six tools. Accept the tradeoff: Athena p50 is 1–3 s and p99 is 10 s+ from planning and Glue metadata, and there is a soft default quota of ~20–25 concurrent DML queries per account per region. Fine for assistant tool calls; not fine for keystroke-latency UI.

**Effort.** 1 month

> **Verifier correction.** Same claim, with the query count corrected: generate_report issues SIX statements total — one ungrouped multi-aggregate overview (service.py:747-760) plus FIVE GROUP BY rollups (service.py:761-788: by txn_class, land_class, extent_unit, village, year), and two of those five are conditional (`top_villages = [] if village else ...` line 778, `by_year = [] if year else ...` line 784), so a village-scoped report issues four. The aggregation surface is actually wider than claimed: add the `sum_extent` branch (service.py:265-273, `SELECT extent_unit, COALESCE(SUM(extent_val),0), count(*) ... GROUP BY extent_unit`) and `resolve_village` (service.py:208-213, `max(similarity(village_norm, %(n)s))` + `count(*) GROUP BY village_norm` over a pg_trgm `%%` match), the latter having no DynamoDB equivalent at all. All other evidence — line numbers, SQL text, and the measured 40 cols/393 B names, 140,757 villages, 333,797 / 229,527 / 681,917 skew figures, 45,143,701 rows — verified exact.

### `major` The corpus has never been deployed to AWS under any engine — terraform provisions db.t4g.micro with 20 GiB for a 290 GB dataset

**Evidence.** infra/terraform/modules/runtime/variables.tf:104-113 — `variable "db_instance_class" { default = "db.t4g.micro" }` and `variable "db_allocated_storage" { default = 20 }`. infra/terraform/modules/runtime/rds.tf:17-35 uses those directly (`engine = "postgres"`, `storage_type = "gp3"`, `multi_az = false # TODO(Phase 2)`). Measured corpus: 290 GB in the `land` schema against 21 MB in `public` (psql, sum of pg_total_relation_size grouped by namespace). db.t4g.micro has 1 GiB of RAM; the HNSW indexes alone are 112 GB and the party trigram GIN is 3,784 MB. Separately, docs/specs/2026-09-19-platform-costing-model.md contains no line item for the corpus — grep for "corpus", "land", "140M", "assistant" returns nothing; its only infrastructure line is "₹15/account-month | Shared ECS, RDS, edge, monitoring, background work" (line 45), and §10 "Initial 100,000-file ingestion" budgets $85–$115 for user documents, not for a 290 GB public corpus.

**Impact.** This is not a DynamoDB migration — it is a first-ever deployment decision being framed as a migration. Nothing in prod holds this data, the runtime layer is destroyed anyway, and the approved cost model has no tier that absorbs $170–$1,290/month of always-on database. Whichever engine wins, the costing model needs a new line and the plan needs a number, or the first month of real hosting produces a surprise that is 10–100x the modelled infrastructure spend at 1,000 accounts.

**Recommendation.** Before choosing an engine, write the corpus into docs/specs/2026-09-19-platform-costing-model.md as its own line item with the chosen target and monthly figure. Then, as the cheapest correct interim move that requires zero code change: one RDS Postgres db.r6g.large (16 GiB) with 100 GiB gp3 holding party + real_estate_records only — 66 GB without the vectors, ~$155/month instance + ~$12/month storage ≈ $167/month ap-south-1 (NEEDS CONFIRMATION; us-east-1 r6g.large is ~$0.19/hr and Mumbai runs ~10% higher), or ~$92/month on db.t4g.large if pre-launch traffic allows. The code already points at a DSN, so this is an env-var change. Migrate to the Athena + OpenSearch split when query volume justifies the extra moving parts. Bump db_allocated_storage's default off 20 GiB in the same change so nobody applies the current terraform and gets a disk that cannot hold 3% of the data.

**Effort.** 1 week

> **Verifier correction.** Claim stands with two numeric corrections. The HNSW indexes total 108 GB, not 112 GB — the eight bv_p*_embedding_idx indexes each render as "14 GB" under pg_size_pretty, but the exact sum of pg_relation_size grouped by access method is 108 GB (btree 22 GB, gin 4530 MB). The party + real_estate_records subset is 63 GB, not 66 GB (sum of pg_total_relation_size for both). Everything else verifies verbatim, and the "never deployed" element is stronger than stated: infra/terraform contains no Athena, OpenSearch, or DynamoDB resource, and no file under infra/ or docs/ references land.party, real_estate_records, or boundary_vectors at all. Also worth adding to the evidence: no tfvars or script anywhere in the repo overrides db_instance_class or db_allocated_storage, so the t4g.micro/20 GiB defaults are what an apply would actually create.

### `major` Four more query patterns with no DynamoDB expression: fuzzy village resolution (on the hot path of 5 of 8 tools), OFFSET pagination in a public response contract, POSIX regex on survey_no, and the party⋈records join

**Evidence.** (a) Fuzzy village resolution, service.py:196-199 and 209-212: `SELECT village_norm, max(similarity(village_norm, %(n)s)) AS sim FROM land.real_estate_records WHERE village_norm %% %(n)s GROUP BY village_norm ORDER BY sim DESC LIMIT 1` — fuzzy AND aggregate over 140,757 distinct values, and it runs as a prerequisite inside `get_analytical_stats` (service.py:224), `get_property_history` (:316), `get_price_rates` (:419) and `search_semantic_boundaries` (:486). (b) OFFSET pagination, service.py:336-352: `ORDER BY reg_date ASC NULLS LAST, rid, s_no LIMIT %(page_size)s OFFSET %(page_offset)s`, returning a public contract of `{"limit", "offset", "has_more", "next_offset"}` (:344-351). DynamoDB has no OFFSET — only opaque LastEvaluatedKey cursors — and cannot jump to offset 500 without reading 500 items. (c) POSIX regex, service.py:327-328: `clauses.append("survey_no ~ %(survey_no)s")` with the param built as `r"\m" + re.escape(survey_no.strip()) + r"\M"` — word-boundary regex, which DynamoDB has no operator for. (d) Joins, service.py:406-408 and 551-553: `FROM land.party p JOIN land.real_estate_records r USING (rid, s_no)`. Measured fan-out: 5.24 parties per rid on average, max 1,995. (e) Leading-wildcard substring: service.py:253-254, 262-263, 727-728 use `transaction_type ILIKE %term%` and `sro_name ILIKE %term%`.

**Impact.** (a) breaks 5 of 8 tools at the entry point, not at the edge. (b) is an API break: `next_offset` cannot be produced, so every caller of `get_property_history` changes. (c) has no DynamoDB form at all. (d) forces a choice between denormalizing 12 record columns onto all 140.53M party items — which grows party from 23 GB to ~100 GB and makes every record correction fan out to an average of 5.24 and up to 1,995 items — or a BatchGetItem per page of hits, doubling latency. (e) is expressible via `contains` but bills for everything scanned before the filter applies.

**Recommendation.** Note honestly which of these are Postgres-specific versus genuinely hard: the survey_no regex (c) is already unindexable in Postgres too — `idx_spatial btree(village_norm, survey_no)` only serves the village_norm equality prefix and then filters — so it is already a per-village scan; it is just a cheap one there because the village lives on one node and tuples are 372 B. Athena/Trino gives you `regexp_like` and `levenshtein_distance`, so (c) and (e) port cleanly to the Athena target and (a) survives as a Levenshtein or a small dedicated village-dimension lookup table (140,757 rows fits comfortably anywhere, including DynamoDB or even memory). Fix (b) by changing the contract to cursor-based pagination regardless of engine — it is the right shape for a 45M-row table anyway. Do not attempt (d) by denormalization.

**Effort.** 2 weeks alongside the store migration

> **Verifier correction.** Evidence stands as written, with three corrections. (1) Line numbers: resolve_village_norm's fuzzy query is service.py:197-199 (not 196-199) and resolve_village's is :210-213; the callers are get_analytical_stats:229, get_property_history:314, get_price_rates:423, search_semantic_boundaries:479, and generate_report via _market_report:722. The `find_properties_by_party` join is :406-407 and _party_report's is :551-552; there is also a third, uncited join at :501 in search_semantic_boundaries (`FROM hits h JOIN {records_fqn} r USING (rid, s_no)`). (2) The join key is (rid, s_no), so the denormalization fan-out should be quoted per (rid, s_no): a 0.05% system sample of land.party gives 73,988 rows over 24,279 distinct (rid,s_no) ≈ 3.0 items per record version (≈4.2 per rid), not 5.24; the argument is unchanged, the multiplier is smaller. (3) "5 of 8 tools" is correct but conservative — the `resolve_village` tool itself (:204-214) issues the same fuzzy+GROUP BY query, making it 6 of the 8 registered tools (adapters/mcp/records.py:280-289).

### `moderate` Aurora Serverless v2 is more expensive than provisioned RDS here, not less — 64 ACU for the vector working set is ~$6,450/month

**Evidence.** Aurora PostgreSQL does support both extensions this code needs — pg_trgm, and pgvector 0.5.1+ with HNSW on Aurora PG 15.5+/16.1+ — so it is a genuine drop-in for services/assistant/src/public_records/*, unlike DynamoDB. The cost arithmetic is the problem. An ACU is 2 GiB of RAM. Keeping the 112 GB of HNSW index warm (measured: 14 GB × 8 partitions) needs ~128 GiB = 64 ACU. At $0.12/ACU-hr us-east-1, ap-south-1 ≈ $0.14 (NEEDS CONFIRMATION): 64 × $0.14 × 730 = $6,540/month sustained. Without the vectors, the 66 GB corpus with a ~16 GiB working set is 8 ACU = 8 × $0.14 × 730 = $817/month sustained. Aurora storage is $0.10/GB-month us-east-1 (~$0.114 Mumbai) = ~$33/month for 290 GB. Aurora Standard additionally bills I/O at $0.20 per million requests, and a trigram GIN scan over a 3,784 MB index generates a lot of them; Aurora I/O-Optimized removes that charge at +30% on both ACU and storage.

**Impact.** Aurora Serverless v2 is the option that sounds cheapest and prices out worst for this workload, because serverless billing rewards idle and this corpus rewards a large, constantly-warm buffer cache. Choosing it on the strength of the word "serverless" would cost ~5x a provisioned r6g.large for the same query behaviour.

**Recommendation.** Use Aurora Serverless v2 only if the deciding factor is scale-to-zero — min capacity of 0 ACU has been available since Nov 2024, with a ~15 s cold resume. For a pre-launch assistant that gets a handful of queries a day, genuinely idling at 0 ACU and paying only ~$33/month of storage is a real argument and beats a $167/month always-on RDS. But that only works with the vectors excluded, and the 15 s resume lands on the first user of the day inside an assistant turn. If assistant traffic is continuous rather than bursty, provisioned RDS wins outright.

**Effort.** 2 weeks

> **Verifier correction.** Aurora PostgreSQL is a genuine drop-in for services/assistant/src/public_records/* — but the version floor is pgvector 0.7.0+, not 0.5.1. The boundary index is `hnsw (embedding halfvec_cosine_ops)` on a `halfvec(1024)` column (\d+ land.bv_p0; service.py:493), and halfvec/halfvec_cosine_ops did not exist before pgvector 0.7.0; Aurora PG 15.5/16.1 ship 0.5.1 and would reject this schema outright. Target an Aurora minor carrying pgvector 0.7.x/0.8.x (local is 0.8.2). Also confirm the corpus-build tooling never uses `vectorscale` (0.9.0 is installed locally; no Aurora/RDS equivalent exists) — the live indexes are all plain hnsw, so this is a build-path check, not a current break. Everything else in the finding is verified against the live DB: 8 × 14 GB HNSW = 112 GB, idx_party_name_trgm = 3,784 MB GIN, pattadar = 290 GB, and pg_trgm is required by four distinct query paths. The cost arithmetic (64 ACU ≈ $6.5k/mo, 8 ACU ≈ $817/mo) depends on an unverified ap-south-1 ACU rate and on a working-set estimate that counts only the HNSW indexes — it omits the 12 GB party_pkey and 3.7 GB trigram GIN the same queries hit, so it errs low, not high.

### `moderate` Ongoing reparse passes would cost ~$140 each in DynamoDB versus effectively free in Postgres, because UpdateItem bills the full item plus every affected GSI

**Evidence.** The corpus is not static: `land.real_estate_records` carries `last_fetch_date`, `ingest_ts timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()` and `parse_status integer NOT NULL DEFAULT 0`, with a dedicated partial index `idx_parse_status btree (parse_status) WHERE (parse_status <> 0)` — a re-fetch/reparse pipeline. `parse_status` is also surfaced to callers in `_RECORD_COLUMNS` (service.py:36-43). In Postgres, correcting one parsed field across the corpus is an UPDATE that touches one tuple each and only the indexes whose keys changed. In DynamoDB, UpdateItem is billed at the full item size rounded up to the next KB regardless of how few attributes changed, plus a full write to every GSI whose projected attributes changed. Arithmetic for one reparse pass over all 45.14M records: 49.7M WRU base + ~149M WRU across 3 ALL-projection GSIs ≈ 199M WRU × $0.71/M (ap-south-1 estimate, NEEDS CONFIRMATION) ≈ $141 per pass.

**Impact.** Expensive rather than blocking, but it changes behaviour: a reparse becomes a budgeted event rather than something you run casually when the parser improves. For a corpus assembled from scraped public registry data — where the parser will improve repeatedly — that is a real drag on iteration speed, and it is exactly the kind of cost that does not appear in a migration estimate because the estimate only models the initial load.

**Recommendation.** If any part of this corpus does go to DynamoDB, keep the mutable parse outputs out of the ALL-projection GSIs so a reparse only rewrites the base item (49.7M WRU ≈ $35/pass instead of $141). Better: on the Parquet/Athena target a reparse is a rewrite of the affected partitions' Parquet files — bounded by the partitions touched, and you get the old version for free if you keep S3 versioning on. That is another point in favour of the Athena home for real_estate_records specifically.

**Effort.** n/a (design constraint, not a task)

> **Verifier correction.** Keep the claim, with two tightenings: (a) `parse_status`, `last_fetch_date` and `ingest_ts` exist in the schema and `parse_status` is demonstrably populated in production data (only 283,312 of 45,143,701 rows at 0; ≥10 distinct bitmask values), but no ingest/reparse code lives in this repo — `parse_status` appears exactly once (services/assistant/src/public_records/service.py:42, a read-path SELECT list) and the other two columns appear nowhere in the tree, so the reparse pipeline is inferred from schema+data, not cited from code; (b) the 49.7M-WRU base assumes ~1.1 KB/item, while measured Postgres heap is 495 B/row (21 GB / 45.14M), so the base pass is $32-$35 depending on encoded item size. Also note the GSI count is conservative: the Postgres table carries nine secondary indexes, so a faithful port would likely exceed three GSIs and cost more than $141/pass, not less.

### `positive` The initial load is NOT the problem — $30 via ImportTable, $383–$766 via BatchWriteItem. Do not reject DynamoDB on load cost.

**Evidence.** Measured item counts: party 140,527,280; real_estate_records 45,144,496; boundary_vectors 44,360,232 = 230.03M items. Measured DynamoDB item sizes (data + exact attribute-name bytes 41/393/25 + ~40 B PK/SK): party ~165 B = 1 WRU; rer ~760 B = 1 WRU for most, 2 WRU for the ~10% with long boundary_* free text, call it 49.7M WRU; boundary_vectors 2,048 B halfvec as binary + 65 B = 2,113 B = ceil(2113/1024) = 3 WRU each = 133.1M WRU. Base total = 140.53 + 49.7 + 133.1 = 323.3M WRU. GSI writes are billed separately at the same rate: 3 ALL-projection GSIs on rer (+149M) and 1 on party (+140.5M) brings it to 612.8M WRU. Pricing: AWS cut on-demand DynamoDB prices ~50% in Nov 2024, so us-east-1 is $0.625 per million WRU, not the $1.25 in the brief. ap-south-1 typically runs ~10–15% above us-east-1, so I used $0.71/M WRU — NEEDS CONFIRMATION. 612.8M × $0.625 = $383; × $0.71 = $435; at the brief's $1.25 = $766. Cheaper path: DynamoDB ImportTable from S3 charges no WCU, only $0.15/GB of uncompressed source (us-east-1; ap-south-1 ≈ $0.17, NEEDS CONFIRMATION). 151 GB of items becomes ~200 GB of DynamoDB-JSON after type wrappers → ~$30.

**Impact.** None — this is the part of the proposal that is financially sound. A reviewer who kills the DynamoDB idea on migration cost is arguing the wrong case and will lose credibility on the parts that actually matter. Storage is likewise affordable: base 151 GB (party 23.2 + rer 34.3 + vectors 93.7) plus GSIs brings it to ~250–357 GB; at ap-south-1 standard-class $0.285/GB-month (NEEDS CONFIRMATION; us-east-1 is $0.25) that is $71–$102/month.

**Recommendation.** If you do migrate any table to DynamoDB, use ImportTable, not BatchWriteItem. Two constraints to design around up front: ImportTable cannot import into an existing table (new table only), and GSIs created during the import are built free but you cannot add one afterwards without a full backfill billed at normal WRU. With no ORM and 33k lines of raw SQL there is no migration tooling to lean on, so a wrong key design means re-importing from scratch.

**Effort.** n/a

> **Verifier correction.** Directionally correct and the conclusion stands; three numbers should be restated. (1) Measured rer item size is ~885 B avg (p50 867, p90 1,004, p99 1,507, max 3,611), not ~760 B, and 7.9% of rows exceed 1 KB, not ~10% — giving 1.080 WRU/item and 48.8M WRU, so the base total is 322.4M WRU (140.53 + 48.8 + 133.1), not 323.3M. (2) The party item is ~150 B (measured data 69.4 B + 41 B attribute names + ~40 B PK/SK), not 165 B; still 1 WRU, so nothing downstream changes. (3) Base storage recomputes to ~155 GB (party 21.1 + rer 40.0 + vectors 93.7), not 151 GB. Additionally, the ImportTable source-size estimate is optimistic: DynamoDB-JSON base64-encodes Binary attributes, inflating each 2,048 B halfvec to 2,731 B, so the export is ~215-235 GB rather than ~200 GB — roughly $33-$40 at $0.15/GB, not ~$30. All AWS list prices cited (the $0.625/M WRU post-Nov-2024 rate, the ap-south-1 uplift, $0.15/GB import, $0.285/GB-month storage) remain unverified from the codebase and should be price-checked before being quoted.

### `positive` The corpus is already correctly isolated behind its own DSN and an 8-tool read-only interface — this decision is independent of any OLTP DynamoDB decision

**Evidence.** services/assistant/src/public_records/settings.py:21 `database_dsn: str = ""` with schema/table names as configurable identifiers validated against `^[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*$` (settings.py:7-14); services/assistant/.env.example:12-21 wires `PUBLIC_RECORDS_DATABASE_URL` / `PUBLIC_RECORDS_PG_*` separately from the platform DB; services/assistant/README.md:64-66 specifies "a dedicated SELECT-only PostgreSQL role." The entire data-access surface is 114 lines (database.py) exposing one method, `fetch_all`, which forces `SET TRANSACTION READ ONLY` (database.py:82), a statement_timeout (database.py:83-86), and an allowlist of exactly two tunable GUCs (`_ALLOWED_LOCAL_SETTINGS` at database.py:16-19, enforced at :74-76). Above it sit 8 named tools (`PUBLIC_RECORD_TOOL_NAMES`, service.py:17-26). Compare `apps/university/ARCHITECTURE.md:280`, which already states the house position: "Move learning progress and tutor sessions to DynamoDB when measured throughput or cost supports the migration. Keep catalog authoring and financial workflows relational unless their access patterns clearly justify a move."

**Impact.** Positive: you can re-home the corpus without touching the transactional platform, and you can decide DynamoDB-for-OLTP without touching the corpus. The read-only role, the statement timeout and the GUC allowlist also mean the corpus cannot be corrupted by the assistant and cannot be used to pin a connection — real safety properties that a hand-rolled DynamoDB access layer would have to re-earn.

**Recommendation.** Keep this seam and make it the explicit migration boundary. Answering the dimension's question directly: no, the corpus must not share a store with the transactional platform data, and today it already doesn't — preserve that. Whatever you decide for properties/users/documents, the 8 corpus tools should be re-pointed independently, and the `fetch_all`-shaped interface is the right place to swap in an Athena client and an OpenSearch client behind the same 8 method signatures.

**Effort.** n/a
