## Cognito + 5-subdomain auth

Two of your five subdomains are already built and one of them is already live in production: `auth.pattadar.com` is a working Cognito custom domain for pool `ap-south-1_XfgAF21Z3` (the pool's own OIDC discovery document names `https://auth.pattadar.com/oauth2/authorize`), and `university.pattadar.com` has a repo, a README and an ARCHITECTURE.md that already specify the shared-pool design. So item 3 of the proposal is done, not pending. The genuinely load-bearing problems are elsewhere. First, "university login as a separate login" contradicts your own written architecture and, if built as a second user pool, permanently forks identity for anyone who is both a landowner and a student — do not do it; one pool, second app client, server-side roles. Second, there is no CORS configuration anywhere in this codebase (zero `CORSMiddleware`, zero S3 `cors_rule`): the entire platform is built on the assumption that the browser and `/api/*` are the same origin, and CloudFront's `spa_router` function says so explicitly ("keeps Cognito callbacks single-origin"). Moving the app from the apex to `platform.pattadar.com` breaks every API call in the product unless you carry a same-origin `/api/*` behavior onto the new distribution. Third — and this is the real blocker — `assistant.pattadar.com` as a publicly-reachable origin would expose a service that has no authentication of its own: `services/assistant/src/main.py:172` takes identity straight from the `x-user-id` header, and its only guard fails OPEN because `INTERNAL_PROXY_SECRET` is set nowhere in the ECS task definitions. Authorization overall is sound at the gateway (identity headers are stripped in ASGI middleware before any handler runs, and principals derive from `iss`+`sub`) but there are no roles, groups or scopes anywhere, which University needs and does not have.

### `blocker` Live callback allowlist accepts exactly two redirect URIs; platform.* and university.* fail with redirect_mismatch today

**Evidence.** Measured against the live pool, not inferred. `GET https://auth.pattadar.com/oauth2/authorize?client_id=10okivmth1rv58ed8f2k7eq4mm&...&redirect_uri=<X>` returns:
  https://pattadar.com/auth/callback        → 302 to /login  (ACCEPTED)
  http://localhost:5173/auth/callback       → 302 to /login  (ACCEPTED)
  https://platform.pattadar.com/auth/callback   → 302 to /error?error=redirect_mismatch
  https://university.pattadar.com/auth/callback → 302 to /error?error=redirect_mismatch
  http://localhost:5180/auth/callback       → 302 to /error?error=redirect_mismatch
  http://localhost:5181/auth/callback       → 302 to /error?error=redirect_mismatch
Cognito matches `redirect_uri` byte-for-byte (documented in infra/terraform/modules/persistent/variables.tf:139-142: "localhost and 127.0.0.1 are DIFFERENT callbacks"). apps/university/README.md:45-46 already lists the four University URLs that are not registered. apps/web/vite.config.ts:23 defaults the web dev port to 5180, which is not registered — scripts/start-local.sh:400-402 prints a warning about exactly this.

**Impact.** Nothing on platform.pattadar.com or university.pattadar.com can sign a user in on day one. Every social login dies on Cognito's own error page, which names neither the cause nor the fix. localhost:5181 (University's dev port, apps/university/README.md:41) is also dead, so you cannot even exercise University sign-in locally against the real pool.

**Recommendation.** Register, before any subdomain cutover: SPA/platform client — https://platform.pattadar.com/auth/callback, https://platform.pattadar.com/ (logout), plus http://localhost:5180 and http://127.0.0.1:5180 spellings, and keep https://pattadar.com/auth/callback during the transition so existing sessions do not strand. University client (new, same pool) — https://university.pattadar.com/auth/callback, https://university.pattadar.com/, http://localhost:5181/auth/callback, http://127.0.0.1:5181/auth/callback. Native client 44gv48ihjlgub7h0lnvjbdmj89 — unchanged (pattadar://auth, verified live: 302s to /login, not to /error). assistant.pattadar.com needs NO redirect URI if it stays a route inside the platform SPA, which is what the code does today (apps/web/src/assistant/AssistantPanel.tsx). The quota is 100 callback URLs per app client, so headroom is not the issue — registration is.

**Effort.** 1 day, but it is a hard gate on the cutover

### `blocker` Terraform will DELETE the localhost:5173 callback on the next prod apply, locking the founder out of local sign-in

**Evidence.** infra/terraform/modules/persistent/variables.tf:113-117 — `variable "spa_callback_urls" { default = ["https://pattadar.com/auth/callback"] }` (a one-element list). infra/terraform/envs/prod/persistent/main.tf:30-47 does NOT override `spa_callback_urls`, `spa_logout_urls`, or `enable_local_dev_client` (whose default is `false`, variables.tf:191-195). So Terraform believes client 10okivmth1rv58ed8f2k7eq4mm has exactly one callback URL and that the `local_dev` client does not exist. The live probe above proves http://localhost:5173/auth/callback IS registered on that client — console drift. Because `enable_local_dev_client` is false, scripts/start-local.sh:73 points local dev at the PROD client id (`COGNITO_CLIENT_ID="10okivmth1rv58ed8f2k7eq4mm,44gv..."`), which is the only reason the loop works.

**Impact.** A single `terraform apply` on envs/prod/persistent — including one run for an unrelated reason, e.g. flipping SES or `enforce_documents_sse_kms_headers` (prod/persistent/main.tf:46) — silently removes the localhost callback. You are Google-federated, so email/password is not a fallback; you lose real-pool local sign-in entirely and the failure surfaces as Cognito's generic error page. Adding the 5-subdomain callbacks in the console instead of in Terraform makes this worse, not better: more drift to be deleted.

**Recommendation.** Before touching anything else: set `spa_callback_urls` / `spa_logout_urls` explicitly in envs/prod/persistent/main.tf to the full live list, and set `enable_local_dev_client = true` so loopback URLs live on their own client (which is what cognito.tf:194-198 was designed for) and the production client can go https-only. Then `terraform plan` must be clean before you add subdomain URLs. Same apply should `terraform import 'module.persistent.aws_cognito_user_pool_client.mobile' ap-south-1_XfgAF21Z3/44gv48ihjlgub7h0lnvjbdmj89` — the import command is already written out at cognito.tf:252-255, and cognito.tf:263-268 warns that a Terraform-created twin gets a NEW client id, which bricks every installed iOS build and revokes every refresh token.

**Effort.** 1 day including a verified clean plan

> **Verifier correction.** Terraform will remove the http://localhost:5173 callback/logout URLs from the prod SPA client (10okivmth1rv58ed8f2k7eq4mm) on the next `terraform apply` in envs/prod/persistent. ### `blocker` assistant.pattadar.com as a public origin exposes a service with no authentication whose only guard fails OPEN

**Evidence.** services/assistant/src/main.py:172-175 — `def _user_id(x_user_id: str = Header(default="", alias="x-user-id")) -> str:` … `raise HTTPException(401, "Missing x-user-id header")`. That is the entire identity check on ~14 endpoints (main.py:304, 317, 335, 364, 377, 389, 402, 648, 674) plus services/assistant/src/account_export.py:15. The file header states it plainly: "The assistant has no auth of its own: it trusts the ``x-user-id`` the gateway injects" (services/assistant/src/internal_auth.py:3-4). The only lock is `InternalProxyAuthMiddleware`, and internal_auth.py:28-31 is `secret = os.getenv("INTERNAL_PROXY_SECRET", "").strip(); if not secret: return True` — fails open by design (line 7). `grep -rn INTERNAL_PROXY_SECRET infra/terraform` returns ZERO hits: it is set in no ECS task definition (infra/terraform/modules/runtime/ecs.tf environment blocks, lines 217-241, 291-319, 475-489). services/api/src/main.py:1205-1217 has the identical fail-open shape for the API's /internal/ routes, and services/api/src/main.py:1195-1199 (`_uid_from_request`) takes owner identity from the same raw header. Today the only thing protecting both is network placement: the ALB's sole rules are /cron/inactivity-check → api and /api/* → gateway (infra/terraform/modules/runtime/alb.tf:184-219), so the assistant has no listener rule at all.

**Impact.** Publishing assistant.pattadar.com to any origin other than the gateway converts a header into a complete authentication bypass: `curl -H 'x-user-id: shankarreddy.t' https://assistant.pattadar.com/...` reads and writes any user's assistant history, attachments and account export. For a platform holding Aadhaar (encrypted) and land-title documents under DPDP, that is a reportable breach, not a bug. This is the single highest-severity item in the whole proposal and it is created purely by the subdomain topology — the current code is safe only because the assistant is unroutable from the internet.

**Recommendation.** Make assistant.pattadar.com a FRONTEND origin only: a static bundle that calls the gateway at the same `/api/gateway/assistant/*` path the SPA uses today (services/gateway/src/routes/proxy.py:1-9 already streams SSE through it, and proxy.py:36-57 strips `x-user-id` and re-injects the validated one). The assistant container must never get a DNS name or an ALB rule. Independently, and before anything else ships: set `INTERNAL_PROXY_SECRET` in all three ECS task definitions so both fail-open guards close, and change both `if not secret: return True` sites to fail CLOSED once `APP_ENV != local`. The fail-open default was reasonable for a half-rolled deploy in 2026; it is not reasonable once a fourth and fifth public hostname exist.

**Effort.** 2 days to wire the secret; the topology decision costs nothing if made now

> **Verifier correction.** Identical finding, with the count fixed: the header check governs 10 identity-bearing routes (services/assistant/src/main.py:304, 317, 335, 364, 377, 389, 402, 648, 674 plus account_export.py:15) out of 14 total routes on the service; the other four (/health, /internal/metrics, /api/capabilities, /docs-info) take no user identity. Worth adding as reinforcement: infra/terraform/modules/runtime/ecs.tf:498 comments that "The assistant sits behind no target group", and services/api/src/main.py:1206 notes the gateway refuses to proxy any path containing an `internal` segment — which is precisely why /internal/account/export is currently unreachable and would become reachable the moment assistant.pattadar.com resolves to the container.

### `major` There is no CORS configuration anywhere in the platform — splitting the app onto platform.pattadar.com breaks every API call

**Evidence.** `grep -rn "CORSMiddleware|allow_origins|Access-Control" services/ --include=*.py` (excluding tests) returns NOTHING. services/assistant/src/main.py:176-177 says why: "No CORS: the browser never reaches this service directly, only through the gateway proxy, which owns the origin policy" — but services/gateway/src/main.py:112-221 installs no CORS middleware either. `grep -n cors infra/terraform/modules/persistent/s3.tf` returns nothing: the documents bucket has no CORS rule, which also blocks the presigned-direct-upload part of your plan. The same-origin assumption is explicit in the infrastructure: infra/terraform/modules/runtime/cloudfront.tf:231-233 — the www→apex 301 exists to keep "Cognito callbacks single-origin" — and docs/specs/2026-07-25-standalone-platform-design.md:66: "Browser app traffic is same-origin `/api/*` via CloudFront → ALB origin, so no CORS." The client relies on it: packages/core/src/api/client.ts:4-5 and apps/web/vite.config.ts:49-68 use relative paths (`/api/gateway/...`), never an absolute API host.

**Impact.** If platform.pattadar.com is an Amplify app whose API calls go to api.pattadar.com (or to pattadar.com/api), every non-GET request triggers a CORS preflight that nothing answers. The whole product returns network errors in the browser with a clean 200 in the server logs — the worst failure mode to debug. Adding CORS is not a one-line fix: `allow_credentials` plus an origin allowlist now has to be correct across 3 app origins × dev/prod, and a mistake (`allow_origins=['*']` or a regex matching `platform.pattadar.com.evil.com`) hands your Bearer-carrying API to any site.

**Recommendation.** Do not solve this with CORS. Keep every browser origin serving its own same-origin `/api/*`: give each CloudFront distribution (or each Amplify app, via a 200-rewrite of `/api/<*>` to the ALB) the same `/api/*` behavior the current distribution has at cloudfront.tf:308-320, using the `Managed-AllViewerExceptHostHeader` origin request policy already referenced at cloudfront.tf:216-219 so `Authorization` survives and the ALB still sees the api vhost. That keeps `packages/core` unchanged, keeps cookies/preflights out of the picture, and is what the codebase was built for. Note for the S3 part of your proposal: presigned direct uploads DO need a bucket CORS rule listing each origin explicitly (`AllowedOrigins`, `AllowedHeaders: ["*"]`, `ExposeHeaders: ["ETag"]`) — that one is unavoidable and currently absent.

**Effort.** 1 week to add the /api behavior per distribution; 3+ weeks and ongoing risk if you go the CORS route

> **Verifier correction.** Claim stands; fix three line numbers. The assistant's "No CORS: the browser never reaches this service directly, only through the gateway proxy, which owns the origin policy" is at services/assistant/src/main.py:166-167 (not 176-177). The www->apex "keeps Cognito callbacks single-origin" comment is at infra/terraform/modules/runtime/cloudfront.tf:226-227 (not 231-233), with the same rule restated at :262 and at :3 ("so no CORS anywhere"). The /api/* ordered_cache_behavior to copy is at cloudfront.tf:348-359 (not 308-320); the Managed-AllViewerExceptHostHeader origin-request policy at 216-219 is cited correctly and is already wired into that behavior at :358 and into the default behavior at :340. Also strengthen the S3 note: services/gateway/src/storage.py has no generate_presigned_* call anywhere — bytes currently flow through the gateway with SSE-KMS applied server-side at storage.py:165-166 — so presigned direct upload is net-new code plus a net-new bucket CORS rule, not a toggle on an existing path.

### `major` "University login" as a SEPARATE login forks identity permanently; your own ARCHITECTURE.md already says one pool

**Evidence.** apps/university/ARCHITECTURE.md:41 — "Browser -> auth.pattadar.com -> shared Cognito user pool"; :55-56 — "Use the existing Cognito user pool and a dedicated University public app client. Use authorization code flow with PKCE"; :57 — "Use the Cognito `sub` as `learnerId`"; :5 — "preserving one account and one trust boundary for users". apps/university/README.md:41-47 repeats it. apps/university/src/auth/AuthProvider.tsx:6-7,30-42 is already wired for it (`VITE_COGNITO_AUTHORITY` + `VITE_COGNITO_CLIENT_ID`, `response_type: 'code'`, oidc-client-ts PKCE) — identical construction to apps/web/src/auth/AuthProvider.tsx:85-97. Quotas make the shared-pool choice free: 1,000 app clients per user pool, and Cognito's hosted-UI session cookie lives on the auth domain, so a second client on the SAME pool gets SSO automatically. Two pools would need two custom domains against a hard limit of 4 per account per region.

**Impact.** Two pools means: two `sub` values for one human, so `subject_<sha256(iss,sub)>` (services/gateway/src/auth.py:218-226) produces two unrelated principals and University progress can never be joined to a Pattadar account; no SSO (separate hosted-UI cookies) — a user signing in on platform. is signed out on university.; duplicate TOTP MFA enrolment (cognito.tf:82-86); two sets of Google/Facebook/Apple IdP configs to maintain (cognito.tf:353-413) plus two more sets of console redirect URIs; and double MAU billing on the ESSENTIALS tier (cognito.tf:66) for anyone active in both. Reversing it later means a full identity migration — the runbook for that already exists and is 6 steps of operator-reviewed evidence (docs/runbooks/identity-migration.md), and services/gateway/src/auth.py:249-258 hard-fails startup without `IDENTITY_LEGACY_BINDINGS`. Realistically a rewrite-class task once real learners exist.

**Recommendation.** One user pool (`ap-south-1_XfgAF21Z3`), a new public app client `pattadar-university` beside `spa`/`local_dev`/`mobile` in cognito.tf, University's own callback URLs, and roles as SERVER grants. "University login" then means "a login page styled for University that lands you in the learning app" — the same identity, a different landing. The one-pool choice is cheap to keep and expensive to undo; the two-pool choice is the reverse. If you genuinely need separately-branded sign-in pages, `aws_cognito_managed_login_branding` is per app client (cognito.tf:321-335) and already gives you that on one pool.

**Effort.** 3 days for the client + branding; vs. a rewrite to undo a second pool later

> **Verifier correction.** The finding stands as written, with two precision fixes. (1) "services/gateway/src/auth.py:249-258 hard-fails startup without IDENTITY_LEGACY_BINDINGS" is true only when the gateway is running against a real Cognito pool — under LocalTrust (auth.py:252-254) it silently accepts a default "{}" and returns; the hard failure is the production path (and it is genuinely wired into startup at services/gateway/src/main.py:93). (2) The finding should cite cognito.tf:339-341, which already warns that even within ONE pool, "a federated login with the same email creates a SEPARATE Cognito user (different sub)". This reinforces the recommendation (sub-forking is a known, documented hazard here) but means one pool alone does not guarantee one sub per human — IdP account linking still has to be handled. Also, the README citation is more precisely :43 (the sentence) within the :41-48 block.

### `major` "Authentication routing" as a service you own is not worth building — the hosted UI already is it

**Evidence.** The proposal is ambiguous between "auth.pattadar.com is the Cognito hosted UI" (which is what it already is, proven above) and "auth.pattadar.com is a router I write." The codebase never asks for the second: apps/web/src/auth/AuthProvider.tsx:1-22 documents a deliberate two-path design — native SRP on your own pages for email/password ("customers never see a non-pattadar.com URL") and oidc-client-ts redirect ONLY for social — and apps/web/src/auth/AuthProvider.tsx:92 comments `response_type: 'code', // PKCE — oidc-client-ts generates the code challenge`.

**Impact.** A hand-written auth router must correctly implement: PKCE `code_verifier`/`code_challenge` generation and storage, `state` generation and verification (CSRF on the authorization response), `nonce` binding for the id token, the token-exchange call (which needs the client secret if you make it confidential, or PKCE if not), refresh-token rotation and reuse detection, and logout propagation to the hosted-UI cookie. Every one of those is a documented CVE class. You have a solo founder, pre-launch, and a 9,865-line web360.py that needs the attention more.

**Recommendation.** auth.pattadar.com = Cognito Managed Login v2, nothing else. It already is. If what you actually want is "one URL that knows whether to land you on platform. or university. after sign-in", that is a 10-line `returnTo` in the OAuth `state` parameter — both AuthProviders already do it (apps/web/src/auth/AuthProvider.tsx:188-199, apps/university/src/auth/AuthProvider.tsx:65-71, which correctly rejects `//` open-redirects at line 70). Do not own a token endpoint.

**Effort.** 0 if you keep the hosted UI; ~2 months plus permanent security ownership if you build a router

> **Verifier correction.** Keep the finding, with the impact list trimmed. Accurate version: "A hand-written auth router must correctly implement PKCE code_verifier/code_challenge generation and storage, `state` generation and verification (CSRF on the authorization response), `nonce` binding for the id token, and the code-for-token exchange (client secret if confidential, PKCE if public) — all of which Cognito managed login plus oidc-client-ts already do (apps/web/src/auth/AuthProvider.tsx:92)." Drop refresh-token rotation and logout from that list: rotation is an app-client setting (`RefreshTokenRotation`, Essentials/Plus only, ~60s grace on the rotated-out token, GA April 2025) that works the same whichever UI you use and is currently unset on all three clients (cognito.tf:182/235/296 — turning it on is a two-line Terraform change worth making independently), and logout is a single `/logout` URL the apps already build themselves (AuthProvider.tsx:171-179). Also worth stating, since it is the real fragility: auth.pattadar.com is conditional on `manage_dns` (cognito.tf:422) and requires an us-east-1 ACM cert plus a resolving apex A record, and a user pool supports only one custom domain — so university shar

### `major` Tokens live in localStorage with no CSP and no security-headers policy; the assistant is the XSS-to-token-theft path you asked about

**Evidence.** apps/web/src/auth/AuthProvider.tsx:95 — `userStore: new WebStorageStateStore({ store: window.localStorage })`; apps/university/src/auth/AuthProvider.tsx:40 — identical. The native SRP path uses amazon-cognito-identity-js, which also defaults to localStorage. `grep -rn "Content-Security-Policy|response_headers_policy|X-Frame-Options" infra/terraform/modules/runtime services/` returns only two hits, both on downloaded-file responses (services/gateway/src/routes/storage.py:138, services/gateway/src/routes/capabilities.py:93) — the application HTML gets NO CSP, and no `aws_cloudfront_response_headers_policy` exists anywhere. Refresh tokens are 30 days (cognito.tf:182). The assistant today is a panel inside the platform SPA (apps/web/src/assistant/AssistantPanel.tsx) rendering LLM output as React text, which is safe; but apps/web/src/w360/MapCanvas.tsx:1501 does use `dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: tip as string }}` on record-derived content — mitigated by apps/web/src/components/escapeHtml.ts, which escapes at the concatenation point, i.e. correctness depends on every call site remembering.

**Impact.** One XSS anywhere in the platform origin reads a 30-day refresh token out of localStorage and exfiltrates it; token revocation is enabled (cognito.tf:190) but you will not know to revoke. Moving the assistant to its own origin actually HELPS here — a separate origin cannot read platform.pattadar.com's localStorage — but only if you resist the instinct to fix the resulting inconvenience with a `Domain=.pattadar.com` cookie. A cookie scoped to `.pattadar.com` is readable by EVERY sibling subdomain including any future marketing or status page, which converts one compromised subdomain into a full account takeover, and it reintroduces CSRF that the current Bearer-header design does not have.

**Recommendation.** Keep Bearer-in-header + per-origin storage; do NOT introduce a `Domain=.pattadar.com` cookie for cross-subdomain session. Cross-subdomain SSO comes free from the Cognito hosted-UI cookie on auth.pattadar.com — a user already signed in gets a silent 302 through /oauth2/authorize with no password prompt, which is the SSO you want and costs nothing. Add an `aws_cloudfront_response_headers_policy` to every distribution with a real CSP (`script-src 'self'`, `connect-src 'self' https://cognito-idp.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com https://auth.pattadar.com`, `frame-ancestors 'none'`), HSTS, and `X-Content-Type-Options`. If the assistant does get its own origin, give it the tightest CSP of the three since it is the one rendering model output.

**Effort.** 1 week for the headers policy; the cookie decision is free if made now

> **Verifier correction.** Same finding, with the XSS-sink mitigation sentence corrected: apps/web/src/w360/MapCanvas.tsx:1501 does `dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: tip as string }}` on record-derived content. That `tip` is built by its only caller, apps/web/src/w360/pages/RecordBoundary.tsx:1038, from `sideTip`/`cornerTip` — and those do NOT use the shared apps/web/src/components/escapeHtml.ts. RecordBoundary re-declares the escaper inline twice (RecordBoundary.tsx:484-485 and :549-550), and both local copies escape only `[&<>"]` while the shared helper also escapes `'`. escapeHtml.ts is imported by five other Leaflet popup sites instead (FmbMapViewer, GeoMap, LocationDialog, ParcelDetailPage, PropertyDetailPage). So the "correctness depends on every call site remembering" point is understated: the platform has two divergent escapers and the one guarding the single dangerouslySetInnerHTML sink is the weaker, duplicated copy. ### `moderate` Moving the app off the apex to platform.pattadar.com signs every existing user out and needs a redirect plan for pattadar.com/app/*

**Evidence.** Today the SPA is served at the apex with the app under /app/* (docs/specs/2026-07-25-standalone-platform-design.md:66; cloudfront.tf:296-298 `aliases = [var.web_domain, local.www_domain]`). Sessions are per-origin localStorage (AuthProvider.tsx:95) and the hosted-UI cookie is 1 hour. apps/web/src/pages/landing/LandingPage.tsx:108 already hardcodes the cross-app link `https://university.pattadar.com`, and apps/web/src/auth/AuthProvider.tsx:177 builds `logout_uri` from `window.location.origin`, so logout URLs must be registered for the new origin too or sign-out 400s.

**Impact.** Every signed-in user loses their session on cutover. Google-federated users get one silent redirect back IF their hosted-UI cookie is still warm (≤1 hour); everyone else re-authenticates, and email/password users — who by design never touch the hosted UI (AuthProvider.tsx:5-7) — must retype credentials. Any bookmarked or emailed `https://pattadar.com/app/...` link 404s against the new portal. Ticket/invitation links minted with `APP_PUBLIC_URL` (ecs.tf:298, `https://${var.web_domain}`) already in the wild point at the old origin.

**Recommendation.** Ship platform.pattadar.com alongside the apex, not instead of it: keep `https://pattadar.com/auth/callback` registered, add a CloudFront function 301 from `pattadar.com/app/*` → `platform.pattadar.com/app/*` (same shape as the existing www→apex function at cloudfront.tf:232-256), and bump `APP_PUBLIC_URL` only after the redirect is live. Announce the sign-out. Retire the apex callback URL one refresh-token lifetime (30 days) later, not on cutover day.

**Effort.** 1 week

> **Verifier correction.** Evidence stands as written, with three precision fixes: (1) the aliases line is infra/terraform/modules/runtime/cloudfront.tf:298, not 296-298; (2) AuthProvider.tsx:95 is the oidc-client-ts (social) store specifically — the native email/password session also lives in localStorage per apps/web/src/auth/cognitoNative.ts:10, so the per-origin session loss applies to both auth paths; (3) LandingPage.tsx:108's `https://university.pattadar.com` is the production fallback behind `VITE_UNIVERSITY_URL` (line 107), so it is env-overridable rather than an unconditional hardcode. ### `moderate` Authorization has no roles, groups or scopes — University's six roles have nothing to attach to

**Evidence.** `grep -rn "cognito:groups|aws_cognito_user_group|aws_cognito_resource_server"` across the repo returns ZERO. Every app client requests only `allowed_oauth_scopes = ["openid", "email", "profile"]` (cognito.tf:155, 210, 272). Authorization is entirely row-scoping on `owner_user_id` in raw SQL (e.g. services/api/src/main.py:1247-1254, the `_INVITATION_OWNED` predicate) plus two hand-maintained allowlists: `ADMIN_SUBJECT_IDS` as a comma-separated env var (services/gateway/src/auth.py:305-307, regex-validated to `subject_[0-9a-f]{64}`, fails closed at :310-318) and a `platform_settings('admin.uids')` DB row (services/api/src/web360.py:3395-3423). Meanwhile apps/university/ARCHITECTURE.md:59 requires six server-granted roles: learner, mentor, instructor, reviewer, content-editor, employer, university-admin.

**Impact.** University cannot express "this user is a mentor" or "this user may review submissions" with anything that exists. The likely shortcut — putting the role in the JWT via the pre-token-generation Lambda (infra/terraform/modules/persistent/lambda/pre_token_gen.py, already wired at cognito.tf:131-136) — bakes a 60-minute-stale grant into a token that cannot be revoked mid-life, which is wrong for a revocation-sensitive role like reviewer.

**Recommendation.** Keep roles in the database as server grants, checked per request, exactly as ARCHITECTURE.md:59 says — not in the token. Use the JWT only for identity (`sub`). If you want coarse product separation, a Cognito group per product (`platform`, `university`) surfaced as `cognito:groups` is acceptable because product membership changes rarely; fine-grained roles are not. Separately, promote `ADMIN_SUBJECT_IDS` out of an env var before there is a second admin — an env-var allowlist means a Terraform apply and an ECS redeploy to grant or revoke admin.

**Effort.** 2 weeks for a role table + middleware; do it before University has real mentors

Terraform already codifies it: infra/terraform/modules/persistent/cognito.tf:464-486 (`aws_cognito_user_pool_domain.custom`, `managed_login_version = 2`) with the ACM cert created under `provider = aws.us_east_1` (cognito.tf:425-437, 456-462) — confirming your note that a Cognito custom domain's cert MUST be in us-east-1 even though the pool is in ap-south-1. infra/terraform/envs/prod/persistent/main.tf:39 sets `custom_auth_domain = "auth.pattadar.com"`. The parent-A-record precondition is met: `dig pattadar.com` → 18.238.192.x (CloudFront), and cognito.tf:418-419 documents that precondition verbatim. Google federation is live on it: authorize with `identity_provider=Google` 302s to `accounts.google.com/o/oauth2/v2/auth?client_id=813618968893-...`.

That property is exactly why the hosted UI should stay on its own subdomain. It is also already baked into shipped native clients — apps/ios/Pattadar/Sources/CognitoAuth.swift:27 (`private static let domain = "auth.pattadar.com"`) and apps/mobile/src/auth/cognitoConfig.ts:9 — so the domain can now never move without an App Store release.

Do not attempt to point auth.pattadar.com at anything you wrote yourself.

**Effort.** 0 — already done

### `positive` The gateway's identity handling is genuinely sound and should be the blueprint for the new subdomains

**Evidence.** services/gateway/src/main.py:43-49 defines `_IDENTITY_HEADERS` and :116-132 strips them in raw-ASGI middleware BEFORE any handler runs, added outermost at :204. services/gateway/src/routes/proxy.py:36-57 drops them again (defense in depth) and :78-88 re-injects `x-user-id` only from validated claims. services/gateway/src/auth.py:272-278 — "SECURITY: Never trust client-supplied identity headers" — and :218-226 derives `subject_<sha256([iss,sub])>` so subjects from different issuers can never collide. services/gateway/src/cognito_jwt.py:228-235 fails CLOSED on an empty client allowlist and requires `token_use == "access"`. The dev door cannot reach production: services/gateway/src/local_issuer.py:11-21 explains that activation needs `LOCAL_AUTH_KEY_FILE` (absent from every ECS task definition) and that a local token fails both issuer and signature against the real pool. The Vite `x-user-id` injection (apps/web/vite.config.ts:63) only ever reaches the API on localhost:8080 — it is stripped at the gateway edge in AWS.

**Impact.** Answering your question 6 directly: no production path trusts a client-supplied identity header today. `owner_user_id` traces back to the immutable `iss`+`sub` of a signature-verified Cognito access token, every time. This is the part of the architecture that is already right.

**Recommendation.** Preserve the invariant as you add subdomains: every browser origin must reach the API through the gateway, and no service other than the gateway may ever receive traffic that did not pass through it. Concretely, that means no ALB listener rule and no DNS name for the api or assistant containers — which is exactly what breaks if assistant.pattadar.com is wired as a backend origin.

**Effort.** 0 — protect it, do not extend it

## S3 presigned uploads + SSE

There is no presigned URL anywhere in this codebase today — `grep -rn "generate_presigned"` across services/, apps/ and infra/ returns zero hits. Every byte of every upload and every download is proxied through the gateway process: `services/gateway/src/routes/storage.py:422` does `data = await file.read()` into RAM, and `services/gateway/src/storage.py:320-327` `read_object()` does `body.read()` of the whole object into RAM before the response is built. So the proposal is not "tune the presign", it is "build it". The good news is that the S3 side is already unusually well-prepared for it: `infra/terraform/modules/persistent/s3.tf:173-183` sets bucket default SSE-KMS with the app CMK and `bucket_key_enabled = true`, and s3.tf:276-318 already contains the exact two deny statements (`DenyUploadsWithoutKms`, `DenyUploadsWithWrongKmsKey`) that a presigned design needs — they are just switched off by default (`enforce_documents_sse_kms_headers` default = false, variables.tf:25-44). The bad news is concentrated in three places. First, the gateway is not merely a byte pipe: the download route is where Content-Disposition/nosniff/CSP-sandbox hardening (routes/storage.py:130-155), HEIC→JPEG transcoding, `?thumb=` downscaling, share-revocation re-checks (`_access`, storage.py:618-633) and the `download_document` DPDP/SOC2 audit record all live — a presigned GET deletes all five at once. Second, the S3 object key is `f"{owner}/{node_id}/{version_id}"` (storage.py:181-182), so the key embeds a *derived, mutable* identity; the live "subject_ hash" bug is that identity derivation changing, and I found it in the real database with the same deed PDF stored twice under two different `subject_` owners. Third, the bucket has no CORS configuration and no `abort_incomplete_multipart_upload` lifecycle rule at all — both are hard requirements for browser direct upload. My verdict: presigned PUT for upload is correct and should be built; presigned GET as a *replacement* for the content route is wrong and should be scoped to full-size originals only, with the transform/thumbnail path staying on the gateway.

### `blocker` Presigned PUT + the repo's own SSE-KMS deny policy is the #1 way this will break: both headers must be signed AND sent byte-identical, and SSEKMSKeyId must be the full ARN

**Evidence.** The deny statements already exist at infra/terraform/modules/persistent/s3.tf:276-318:
```
sid       = "DenyUploadsWithWrongKmsKey"
effect    = "Deny"
actions   = ["s3:PutObject"]
condition { test = "StringNotEquals"
            variable = "s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption-aws-kms-key-id"
            values   = [aws_kms_key.main.arn] }
```
and the sibling `DenyUploadsWithoutKms` on `s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption != aws:kms`. Both are gated on `var.enforce_documents_sse_kms_headers`, default **false** (variables.tf:25-44). The server today supplies these as boto3 params, not as client headers: storage.py:161-166 `{"ServerSideEncryption": "aws:kms", "SSEKMSKeyId": self._kms_key_arn, "BucketKeyEnabled": True}`, where `_kms_key_arn` comes from `STORAGE_KMS_KEY_ARN` (routes/storage.py:93).

**Impact.** With an IAM Deny, a policy condition on a **missing** key evaluates StringNotEquals as true, so the Deny fires. Consequences: (a) if you presign with those Params, boto3 signs them as `x-amz-server-side-encryption` and `x-amz-server-side-encryption-aws-kms-key-id`, and the browser must send both, byte-for-byte, or SigV4 fails with 403 SignatureDoesNotMatch before S3 even evaluates the policy; (b) if you presign *without* them and later flip `enforce_documents_sse_kms_headers = true`, every browser upload 403s with AccessDenied and the bucket default encryption does not save you, because the policy checks the *request header*, not the resulting encryption; (c) if the client sends the key **alias** or bare key-id while the policy compares against `aws_kms_key.main.arn`, StringNotEquals is true and the Deny fires — a 403 that looks identical to a signature failure and will cost a day to diagnose. Note also that `BucketKeyEnabled` is a signed header too (`x-amz-server-side-encryption-bucket-key-enabled`) — if you sign it and the client omits it, 403.

**Recommendation.** Do NOT sign the SSE headers. Presign the PUT with only `Bucket`, `Key`, and `ContentType`, rely on bucket default encryption (s3.tf:173-183) which already applies the app CMK with bucket keys to every object regardless of headers, and keep `enforce_documents_sse_kms_headers = false`. If you want the policy as defence-in-depth, change the deny to an **S3 bucket policy that denies PutObject when `s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption` is present and not `aws:kms`** (i.e. add `Null` guards) rather than the current "missing header also denied" shape — otherwise default encryption and the deny policy are mutually exclusive designs. Whichever you pick, write an integration test that does a real presign + real `fetch` PUT against the real bucket; a unit test cannot catch a signature mismatch.

**Effort.** 3 days (policy fix + presign endpoint + one live integration test)

> **Verifier correction.** Claim stands; three detail fixes. (a) These denies are already an **S3 bucket policy**, not an IAM policy: the document is `data.aws_iam_policy_document.documents_bucket` consumed by `resource "aws_s3_bucket_policy" "documents"` at s3.tf:321-326, and each statement has `principals = ["*"]`. So the IMPACT line "With an IAM Deny" and the RECOMMENDATION's "change the deny to an S3 bucket policy that denies PutObject when the header is present and not aws:kms" should read "add `Null` guards to the existing bucket-policy denies" — the shape to change, not the policy type. (b) Line drift: the boto3 params are storage.py:163-168 (dict keys at :165-167), not 161-166; the env read is routes/storage.py:94, not :93. (c) Worth adding, because it strengthens the case rather than weakening it: variables.tf:38-41 already documents that GuardDuty's header-less validation object depends on bucket default encryption, which is the repo's own acknowledgement that the current "missing header is also denied" shape is incompatible with header-less writers — exactly the browser-presign case.

### `blocker` The bucket has no CORS configuration — browser direct upload is impossible until one exists

**Evidence.** `grep -rn "cors" infra/terraform/` → **zero hits**. There is no `aws_s3_bucket_cors_configuration` for `aws_s3_bucket.documents` in infra/terraform/modules/persistent/s3.tf (326 lines, reviewed in full). There is also no CORS middleware in the services: `grep -n "CORSMiddleware\|allow_origins" services/gateway/src/main.py services/api/src/main.py` → zero hits, because today everything is same-origin behind one CloudFront distribution (infra/terraform/modules/runtime/cloudfront.tf:348-360, "Same-origin API: browser calls https://pattadar.com/api/* — no CORS").

**Impact.** A browser PUT to `<bucket>.s3.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com` from `platform.pattadar.com` is cross-origin. Chrome sends a preflight `OPTIONS` because the request carries `Content-Type` and (if you sign them) `x-amz-*` headers. With no bucket CORS config S3 answers the OPTIONS with 403 and the PUT never fires. This is a silent-in-testing failure: `curl` works perfectly, the browser does not. It is compounded by the founder's 5-subdomain topology — platform., university., auth., assistant., and the apex all become distinct origins that must each be listed. The zero-CORS property the platform enjoys today is a direct consequence of the single-origin CloudFront design and the subdomain split gives it up.

**Recommendation.** Add `aws_s3_bucket_cors_configuration` to the documents bucket with `allowed_methods = ["PUT", "GET", "HEAD"]`, `allowed_origins` listing the exact 5 https origins (never `*` — a wildcard on a presigned-PUT bucket lets any page on the internet drive a leaked URL), `allowed_headers = ["content-type"]` plus any `x-amz-*` header you actually sign, `expose_headers = ["ETag"]` (the confirm step needs it), and `max_age_seconds = 3000` so the preflight is not re-paid per file. Native iOS (`apps/ios/.../WriteQueue.swift:568`) is unaffected — URLSession does not preflight — so CORS is a web-only blocker, which means it will be discovered late if the iOS path is built first.

**Effort.** 1 day

> **Verifier correction.** The finding stands. Two mechanism/framing details should be fixed:

(a) The preflight rationale is wrong in its reasoning, right in its conclusion. A cross-origin `PUT` is NOT a CORS-simple request regardless of headers — `PUT` is not in the safelisted method set {GET, HEAD, POST}, so the browser sends an `OPTIONS` preflight unconditionally. It is not "because the request carries Content-Type and x-amz-* headers." (A non-safelisted `Content-Type` such as `application/pdf` or `image/jpeg` would independently force a preflight even on POST, but that is a second reason, not the reason.) S3 answers `OPTIONS` on a bucket with no CORS configuration with 403 `AccessDenied` / "CORSResponse: CORS is not enabled for this bucket," so the PUT never fires. Conclusion unchanged.

(b) Severity should be stated as "hard blocker for the proposed browser-direct-upload path," not a defect in shipped code. `grep -rn "generate_presigned"` across the repo returns zero hits: presigned URLs are not generated anywhere today, server-side or client-side, and all uploads (web and iOS) go through the gateway at `api/gateway/storage/files` (services/gateway/src/storage.py:156-169, apps/ios/.../WriteQueue.swift:

### `blocker` The object key already embeds a derived identity, and the live subject_ bug is that derivation changing — Amplify/Cognito rebuild makes this strictly worse

**Evidence.** services/gateway/src/storage.py:181-182:
```
def _key(owner: str, node_id: str, version_id: str) -> str:
    return f"{owner}/{node_id}/{version_id}"
```
`owner` is `extract_user_id(request)` → `user_id_from_claims` (services/gateway/src/auth.py:261-269), which returns the normalized `sub` **only** under the local issuer with LocalTrust, and otherwise `principal_id_from_claims` = `"subject_" + sha256(json.dumps([iss, sub]))` (auth.py:217-226), unless remapped by `IDENTITY_LEGACY_BINDINGS`. I confirmed the split in the live local data. In `pattadar_hub.storage_nodes`:
```
 owner_id                     | count | max size
 w360-demo                    |  121  |     56766
 sankara.telukutla            |   90  |  14439064
 shankarreddy.t               |   67  |  11661206
 subject_f087e81d…ed777       |    1  |   5330712
 subject_f3fc1a5b…dff83       |    1  |   5330712
```
Those two `subject_` rows are **the same file** — `SaleDeed_2019_Katragunta_KanderaNagaiah-to-SankaraReddy.pdf`, byte-identical at 5,330,712 — stored at two different keys, `subject_f3fc…/fad9b271…/523670cf…` and `subject_f087…/1e316701…/fb1ef4a9…`, because the human signed in through two different issuer/sub pairs. Meanwhile in the api DB `pattadar.documents`: `w360-demo` 62 rows (62 with empty file_ref), `sankara.telukutla` 5 rows (5 empty), `shankarreddy.t` 2 rows (both with real UUID file_refs that resolve in `pattadar_hub` under owner `shankarreddy.t`). And the second half of the reported bug is trivial: the router prefix is `/api/gateway/storage` (routes/storage.py:44), so `/storage/files/...` matches nothing and 404s by construction.

**Impact.** Because the owner string is in the S3 key, an identity change is not a metadata fix — it is a **CopyObject of every object the user holds**, and with SSE-KMS every copy is a KMS Decrypt + GenerateDataKey. Every query in the service filters on `owner_id` (`storage.py:145-160` list_children, :234 get_node, :618-633 `_access`), so the user simply sees an empty drive. Creating a new Cognito user pool — which is exactly what `amplify add auth` / Amplify Gen 2 `defineAuth` does — changes `iss` for every user, which changes every `subject_<sha256>`, which orphans every object key in the bucket. The `IDENTITY_LEGACY_BINDINGS` escape hatch (auth.py:228-246) remaps the *lookup* but not the *key*, so bindings paper over the DB and leave S3 inconsistent. Amplify's own Storage convention (`private/{identity_id}/…`) is the same mistake one level worse: it embeds the **Cognito Identity Pool** identity id, which is regenerated if the identity pool is ever recreated.

**Recommendation.** Two things, in this order. (1) Before any presigned work, change `_key()` to `f"{node_id}/{version_id}"` — drop the owner segment entirely. `node_id` is already a server-generated uuid4 unique across the table, authorization is already done in SQL via `_access` (storage.py:618-633) and never by key prefix, and this makes the key immutable under any identity change forever. Migrate the existing ~280 objects with a CopyObject sweep and an `UPDATE storage_versions SET object_key = …`; at 280 objects this is a 10-minute script, and it will never be this cheap again. (2) Fix the 62 + 5 empty `file_ref` rows and add a NOT-NULL-ish check, because half the reported "404" is a document row that was never linked to a storage node at all. Do NOT adopt Amplify Storage's `private/{identity_id}/` path convention.

**Effort.** 1 week for (1) including the migration; the alternative later is a full-bucket re-key

Current live counts in pattadar_hub.storage_nodes are w360-demo 121, sankara.telukutla 96 (not 90), shankarreddy.t 67, u01 2, plus the two subject_ rows. The api-side table column is documents.owner_user_id (not owner_id), with w360-demo 62 rows/62 empty file_ref, sankara.telukutla 5/5 empty, shankarreddy.t 2 with resolvable refs, and system 8 rows with refs. The two subject_ rows are same-name and same-size (5,330,712 bytes) duplicates; byte-identity was not hash-verified but is the obvious reading.

### `blocker` Presigned GET would delete the XSS sandbox, HEIC transcode, thumbnailing, share revocation and the download_document audit record in one move

**Evidence.** All five live in the byte path you would be removing. (a) XSS hardening — routes/storage.py:130-155:
```
_SANDBOX = {"X-Content-Type-Options": "nosniff",
            "Content-Security-Policy": "sandbox; default-src 'none'"}
_INLINE_TYPES = frozenset({image/jpeg, png, gif, webp, bmp, heic, heif, application/pdf})
def _disposition(mime): return "inline" if … in _INLINE_TYPES else "attachment"
```
with the comment at :127-129: "A stored file's type is whatever the uploader claimed — and an anonymous work-token holder can put a file in an owner's drive." (b) HEIC→JPEG at routes/storage.py:165-177 and `?format=web` at :336-341; `image/heic` is a real stored type in the corpus. (c) `?thumb=` Pillow downscale at :185-196 and :329-335. (d) Share revocation — `content_identity` calls `self._access(caller, node_id)` (storage.py:277-278) on **every** read, and `_access` (storage.py:618-633) re-evaluates unexpired shares on the node and its ancestors. (e) The DPDP/SOC2 record — routes/storage.py:355-363 `BackgroundTask(record_audit, "download_document", …)`, registered in services/api/src/audit.py:154 and named as an incident-response evidence source in docs/runbooks/incident-response.md:68. Callers that depend on the transforms: apps/web/src/components/holdingCards.tsx:106 `?format=web&thumb=${thumb}`, apps/web/src/w360/paper/scan.tsx:58 `?format=web`, apps/mobile/src/api/storage.ts:179 `?format=web`.

**Impact.** (a) S3 serves whatever `ContentType` was stored, with no nosniff and no CSP. An uploaded `deed.svg` or `receipt.html` becomes stored XSS on `*.s3.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com` — and with the founder's `auth.pattadar.com` topology, if you ever put the bucket behind a `*.pattadar.com` CNAME it becomes stored XSS in the auth cookie's domain. (b) Safari renders HEIC; Chrome and Firefox do not. A presigned GET of the one stored `image/heic` object shows a broken image in the web app. (c) Every property grid card would download the full 5 MB original instead of a ~40 KB thumbnail — on the costing model's own 100 GB/month figure this is the fastest way to multiply India egress. (d) A presigned URL is an unrevocable bearer token for its whole TTL. Revoking a share stops the *next* presign, not the outstanding one. (e) You lose per-user download attribution: S3 server access logging (s3.tf:194-199) records the **requester**, which for a presigned URL is the signing task role, not the person — and CloudTrail data events are WriteOnly here (cloudtrail.tf:186-198), so reads are not in the trail either.

**Recommendation.** Split the download path, do not replace it. Keep the gateway route for `?thumb=`, `?format=web`, and anything not in `_INLINE_TYPES`. Add presigned GET **only** for full-size originals of already-safe types, and when you do: set `ResponseContentDisposition` and `ResponseContentType` as signed response-header overrides on the presign so S3 emits the same `attachment`/`inline` decision `_disposition()` makes today (this is the one S3 feature that recovers control (a)); keep the TTL at 60-120 s so (d) is bounded to roughly a click; and write the `download_document` audit row at **presign time** rather than at byte time, accepting that it now records "a URL was issued" not "bytes left" — say so explicitly in docs/compliance/gdpr-dpdp.md rather than letting the control quietly weaken. Range support, which the gateway has never had (`grep -n "Accept-Ranges\|Range" routes/storage.py` → nothing) and which docs/specs/2026-08-14-ios-offline-architecture.md:518-524 already flags, comes free with presigned GET — that is the real prize for the iOS app.

**Effort.** 2 weeks

### `major` No presigned URLs exist anywhere — the platform is 100% proxy today, and the proxy reads whole objects into RAM

**Evidence.** `grep -rn "generate_presigned\|presign" services/ apps/ infra/` → zero code hits (only prose in docs/specs/2026-08-14-ios-offline-architecture.md:522). Upload: services/gateway/src/routes/storage.py:422 `data = await file.read()` — unbounded — then services/gateway/src/storage.py:433,454 `self._s3.put_object(**self._put_args(key=key, data=data, mime=mime))` with `"Body": data` (storage.py:156-160), i.e. the full byte string in memory. Download: storage.py:320-327 `read_object()` → `body.read()` returns all bytes; routes/storage.py:346 `data = await run_in_threadpool(svc.read_object, key)` then `Response(content=data,...)` at :345-363. Client-side proof that this already hurts: apps/web/src/api/client.ts:54 gives any FormData POST and any `/content` GET a **600,000 ms (10 minute)** timeout, vs 20,000 ms for /graphql.

**Impact.** Every 5 MB deed (the costing model's stated average — docs/specs/2026-09-19-platform-costing-model.md:230 "100,000 files × 5 MB") is buffered twice in the ECS task's heap on upload and once on download. With the documented 100 GB/month download delivery (costing-model.md:31) every one of those bytes crosses CloudFront → ALB → ECS → S3 and back, so the founder is paying CloudFront India egress *plus* ALB LCU *plus* ECS CPU/RAM *plus* S3 GET for bytes S3 could have served alone. It also means concurrency is bounded by task memory, not by S3: 20 simultaneous 14 MB uploads (14,439,064 bytes is the real max in the local corpus) is ~290 MB of transient heap in one process.

**Recommendation.** Build presigned PUT for the upload direction. Keep the gateway in the *download* path for transformed reads (see the transform finding) but add presigned GET for full-size originals. This is the single highest-value piece of the founder's proposal and it should be done regardless of whether DynamoDB or Amplify ever happen — it is orthogonal to both.

**Effort.** 2 weeks

> **Verifier correction.** No presigned URLs exist anywhere in the platform — every byte is proxied through the gateway process, and the gateway materializes whole objects in RAM in both directions. Upload: services/gateway/src/routes/storage.py:422 `data = await file.read()` reads the whole body into one bytes object (bounded, not unbounded: services/gateway/src/main.py:152-205 `LimitRequestBodyMiddleware` rejects bodies over MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES + 8 MiB = 108 MiB before or during read, and routes/storage.py:423 rejects >100 MB after), which is then passed straight to S3 as `"Body": data` (services/gateway/src/storage.py:156-160, called at storage.py:434 and :456). Download: storage.py:320-325 `read_object()` does `resp["Body"].read()` and returns all bytes; routes/storage.py:323 `data = await run_in_threadpool(svc.read_object, key)` and the handler returns `Response(content=data, ...)` at :345ff — there is no StreamingResponse in the file. Starlette spools the upload to a temp file above 1 MiB, so it is one heap copy per in-flight request, not two; 20 concurrent uploads at the real corpus maximum of 14,439,064 bytes (pattadar_hub.storage_versions: 299 rows, max 14,439,064, avg 2,080,974) is still ~290 MB of tr

### `major` A presigned PUT cannot enforce size — and there is no storage quota code anywhere in the platform

**Evidence.** Today the bound exists in code: `MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES = int(os.getenv("STORAGE_MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES", str(100 * 1024 * 1024)))` (routes/storage.py:46), checked at :423. `grep -rn "quota\|storage_bytes\|reserve" services/gateway/src services/api/src` returns nothing about storage quotas — the only "reserve" hits are payments.py. Yet the costing model asserts as design assumption #9: "Upload bytes are reserved before S3 writes to stop concurrent quota overshoot" (docs/specs/2026-09-19-platform-costing-model.md:290), and the pricing tiers sell 1 GB / 10 GB / 40 GB / 100 GB caps (costing-model.md:57-60). AWS limits: a single PutObject can write up to **5 GB**; only multipart reaches the 5 TB object ceiling.

**Impact.** A presigned PUT carries no size condition — SigV4 query-string presigning signs the method, key, expiry and any headers you name, and nothing else. A signed-in Free-tier user (1 GB cap, ₹0 revenue) can request a presign for a 2 KB thumbnail and PUT 5 GB. Repeated, this is an uncapped storage + KMS + GuardDuty-scan bill against a credit balance, with zero server code in the way. The current 100 MB limit is not enforced by anything except the proxy you are removing. Note the existing proxy already has its own version of this bug: routes/storage.py:422 does `await file.read()` (unbounded) and checks the length *after*, while the capability route does it correctly — `await file.read(MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES + 1)` at routes/capabilities.py:119.

**Recommendation.** Use **POST policy** (`generate_presigned_post`) rather than presigned PUT for browser uploads. It is the only presign form that supports `["content-length-range", 1, MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES]`, and it also lets you pin `Content-Type` with `starts-with` and force `success_action_status`. S3 rejects an over-size body at the edge, before the bytes are billed. If you must keep PUT (iOS `UploadClient` is simpler with it), then on confirm do a `head_object` and compare `ContentLength` to the declared size, delete on mismatch, and add a per-owner reserved-bytes row so assumption #9 stops being fiction. Also fix routes/storage.py:422 to `await file.read(MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES + 1)` today, independent of all of this.

**Effort.** 1 week

> **Verifier correction.** Keep the finding, with two edits. (a) Drop "(unbounded)" for routes/storage.py:422: the gateway already bounds every request body via `LimitRequestBodyMiddleware` (services/gateway/src/main.py:160-199, registered at main.py:205) against `MAX_REQUEST_BYTES = MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES + 8 MiB` (main.py:54-56), which checks declared Content-Length and stream-counts received bytes, returning 413. So `file.read()` there is capped at ~108 MB; tightening it to `read(MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES + 1)` is hygiene, not a fix for a live DoS. Correspondingly, today's 100 MB bound is enforced twice (route check + ASGI middleware), not once — but both live in the proxy path the proposal removes, so the consequence is unchanged. (b) Soften "a presigned PUT carries no size condition" to: a presigned PUT can only pin an *exact* Content-Length (and only if that header is included in SignedHeaders); it cannot express a range, and the common implementation signs no size at all — which is what lets a presign minted for a 2 KB thumbnail accept a 5 GB PutObject. Everything else — the missing quota code, costing-model.md:290 assumption 9 being unimplemented, the 1/10/40/100 GB tiers at :57-60, SSE-KMS per-object cost at stora

### `major` No orphan/abandoned-upload reaping: the bucket has no abort_incomplete_multipart_upload rule and there is no unconfirmed-upload lifecycle

**Evidence.** `grep -rn "abort_incomplete\|AbortIncomplete" infra/ services/` → **zero hits**. The documents bucket's lifecycle (infra/terraform/modules/persistent/s3.tf:201-226) has exactly two rules: `current-to-standard-ia` at 90 days and `expire-noncurrent-versions` at `var.noncurrent_version_expiration_days` (default 180, variables.tf:19-23). Neither reaps an object with no DB row. Today this cannot happen: `create_file` (storage.py:450-478) PUTs first and, on a `UniqueViolation` from the node insert, calls `self._safe_remove(key)` — a synchronous compensating delete inside one request. Versioning is on (s3.tf:165-171), so even a deleted orphan leaves a delete marker plus a noncurrent version.

**Impact.** A presigned flow is init → client PUT → confirm, and the server is absent for the middle step. Two failure modes appear that do not exist today: (1) client uploads and the app is backgrounded / the tab closes before confirm → a fully-paid, KMS-encrypted, GuardDuty-scanned object that no row points at and nothing will ever delete, because the expire-noncurrent rule only touches *noncurrent* versions and an orphan is current; (2) client confirms without uploading → a `storage_nodes` row whose `current_version_id` points at a `storage_versions.object_key` that 404s in S3, which is a second, self-inflicted copy of the exact 404 bug this review was asked to explain. There is a third, subtler one: `create_file`'s `on_conflict="duplicate"` path allocates a name via `next_free_name` (storage.py:112-131) against the UNIQUE index `storage_nodes_unique_name` (sql/init.sql:41-43), so an abandoned init permanently burns "deed (2).pdf".

**Recommendation.** Three additions, all cheap. (1) Add `abort_incomplete_multipart_upload { days_after_initiation = 7 }` to the documents lifecycle now — it costs nothing and prevents invisible partial-upload storage. (2) Add a lifecycle rule with `filter { prefix = "pending/" }` + `expiration { days = 1 }`, presign into `pending/` and have the confirm step CopyObject into the final key; an unconfirmed upload then reaps itself in 24 h and a confirmed one is verified by the server (this also gives you the `head_object` size check for free). (3) Make confirm idempotent and make the DB row creation happen at confirm, not at init, so mode (2) is impossible — and keep the `_safe_remove` compensating-delete instinct that the current code already shows.

**Effort.** 1 week

> **Verifier correction.** Claim stands. Two citation nits: `next_free_name` is defined at services/gateway/src/storage.py:114 (not 112), and `create_file` begins at storage.py:382 — the quoted PUT-then-compensating-delete logic is at :456 and :477 within it. Also worth adding: the lifecycle has no `expiration { expired_object_delete_marker = true }`, so the delete markers left by `_safe_remove` (storage.py:760-764, which deletes without a VersionId) are never reaped either.

### `moderate` A presigned URL signed by an ECS task role dies when the role's session rotates, not at the TTL you asked for

**Evidence.** The gateway resolves credentials via the default chain with no static keys — routes/storage.py:66-72: "boto3 resolves credentials via the default chain — on ECS/EKS that is the task-role. NO static keys are read here, by design." SigV4 presigned URLs have a documented maximum expiry of 7 days (604,800 s), but a URL signed with temporary credentials (any AssumeRole-derived session, which is what an ECS task role is) stops working the moment those credentials expire, regardless of the `ExpiresIn` you passed. ECS task role credentials are refreshed by the agent on a rolling basis, typically on the order of hours.

**Impact.** If you presign a GET with `ExpiresIn=86400` for an offline-capable mobile cache, the URL will 403 unpredictably — sometimes in 6 hours, sometimes in 20 minutes if the task recycled — and it will look like an intermittent auth bug rather than a credential-lifetime bug. This will burn debugging time on the iOS offline path specifically, because apps/ios/.../WriteQueue.swift:600-608 already classifies 401/403 as retryable "weather" and will silently retry a permanently-dead URL.

**Recommendation.** Keep upload TTLs at 5 minutes and download TTLs at 60-120 seconds. At those numbers the credential-rotation issue is invisible and the bearer-capability exposure is bounded to roughly one user action, which is also what mitigates the unrevocable-share problem. Never presign for hours or days from a task role. Document the rule next to the presign helper so nobody "helpfully" raises it to a day later. If long-lived document links are ever a product requirement, that is a CloudFront signed-cookie problem, not a presigned-URL problem.

**Effort.** 1 day (a constant and a comment, if it is decided up front)

> **Verifier correction.** Evidence is accurate: services/gateway/src/routes/storage.py:68-69 does say "boto3 resolves credentials via the default chain — on ECS/EKS that is the task-role. NO static keys are read here, by design." and apps/ios/PattadarKit/Sources/PattadarKit/Storage/WriteQueue.swift:600-613 does classify 401/403 as retryable "weather" with no attempt cap (only other 4xx park, line 614), so a permanently-dead URL is retried forever at up to 1h intervals. Fix two details: (1) WriteQueue is the UPLOAD path — it POSTs multipart to the gateway storage route (lines 585-593) — so it would only see a presigned-URL 403 if presigned PUTs are adopted; it never handles a cached presigned GET, so the "iOS offline download cache" framing does not follow from that citation. (2) Task recycling does not kill the URL: an ECS task-role session stays valid until its own expiry (~6h) whether or not the task still runs. The real problem is that the signer cannot know how much of that session remains, so an ExpiresIn longer than the residual credential lifetime silently truncates. Also note this is a constraint on the PROPOSAL, not an existing bug: routes/storage.py:7 states the current design is "bytes in AWS S3 

### `moderate` Direct upload does NOT bypass malware scanning — GuardDuty already covers it — but the deny policy has a documented gap and DEEP_ARCHIVE parking breaks it

**Evidence.** GuardDuty Malware Protection for S3 is provisioned on the documents bucket (infra/terraform/modules/persistent/guardduty.tf:1-8, 138+, with the validation object allowance at :101-103), and the bucket policy denies reads of infected objects — s3.tf:255-274:
```
sid    = "DenyReadOfInfectedDocuments"
effect = "Deny"
actions = ["s3:GetObject", "s3:GetObjectVersion"]
condition { test = "StringEquals"
            variable = "s3:ExistingObjectTag/GuardDutyMalwareScanStatus"
            values   = ["THREATS_FOUND"] }
```
with the comment at :253-254: "Only THREATS_FOUND is denied: an untagged object is one the scan has not reached yet, and denying those would stall every fresh upload." Separately, scripts/platform-down.sh transitions objects to `var.parking_storage_class` = DEEP_ARCHIVE (variables.tf:46-60). GuardDuty Malware Protection for S3 does not scan objects in archived storage classes, and has a 5 GB per-object scan ceiling.

**Impact.** Mostly good news: the scan is bucket-triggered, so it fires on a presigned PUT exactly as it does on a proxied one, and the founder's proposal does not weaken it. Three real gaps remain. (1) The race the comment acknowledges: between the PUT and the tag, the object is readable. With a proxy that window is invisible because the server holds the bytes; with direct upload the client can PUT and immediately presign a GET of its own upload, so the window is now reachable by an attacker on purpose. (2) An object over 5 GB — reachable via the unbounded-PUT finding above — is never scanned and stays untagged and therefore readable forever. (3) If the environment is ever parked to DEEP_ARCHIVE and un-parked, freshly restored objects are in a class the scanner will not have covered.

**Recommendation.** Gate the *confirm* step on the tag rather than the read: on confirm, `head_object` and if `GuardDutyMalwareScanStatus` is absent, mark the node `scan_pending` and do not surface it in the drive until a tag arrives (an S3 Event Notification or a short poll). That closes the race without stalling uploads, which is what the current comment was worried about. Separately, wire an EventBridge rule on GuardDuty's `Object Scan Result` so a THREATS_FOUND verdict deletes the object and the row instead of leaving a permanently unreadable node behind — today an infected upload becomes a row that 404s forever, which is the same user-visible symptom as the bug under investigation.

**Effort.** 1 week

> **Verifier correction.** One qualification: the GuardDuty detector, IAM role and malware-protection plan are all gated on `var.manage_org_security` (`count = var.manage_org_security ? 1 : 0` at guardduty.tf:9, 18, 43, 123, 131, 141). It defaults to true (infra/terraform/README.md:166), but README.md:151 tells non-prod envs to set it to false, so in a dev/second env the scan may not exist at all while the `DenyReadOfInfectedDocuments` statement — which is applied unconditionally via aws_s3_bucket_policy "documents" (s3.tf:321-326) — still sits there matching nothing. In that configuration the "untagged means not yet scanned" comment is not merely a race window; nothing will ever tag, so every object is permanently readable with no scan at all. Otherwise the finding stands as written.

### `moderate` The client would start telling the server what it uploaded, which inverts an invariant the code states explicitly

**Evidence.** services/api/src/capabilities.py:232-233, the docstring on the worker deliverable endpoint:
```
"""Gateway supplies uploaded storage IDs, never IDs from a public JSON body."""
```
and the gateway side that makes it true — services/gateway/src/routes/capabilities.py:118-133 uploads the bytes itself via `svc.create_file(grant["owner"], …)` and then constructs `payload.update({"fileRef": node["id"], "fileName": node["name"], "mimeType": …, "sizeBytes": len(data)})` from the node it just created — none of it from the request body. The same-side invariant appears again at capabilities.py:181-183, which re-reads `file_ref` from the DB and compares it to the client's claimed value before honouring it. Note this route is reachable by an **anonymous capability-token holder** (`_check("work", token)` at routes/capabilities.py:111), not a signed-in user.

**Impact.** In a presign → PUT → confirm flow, `fileRef`, `fileName`, `mimeType` and `sizeBytes` all arrive in the confirm body, i.e. from the client. If confirm is implemented naively, an anonymous work-token holder can confirm an arbitrary node id and attach *someone else's document* to a ticket as their deliverable — a cross-tenant read via a write endpoint. The current architecture is immune by construction; the proposed one is not, unless the confirm step is designed against this.

**Recommendation.** Make the presign step, not the confirm step, the authority. Issue a short-lived server-signed upload ticket (HMAC over owner, node_id, version_id, key, max_bytes, expiry) at presign time, require it back at confirm, and derive every field from the ticket plus a `head_object` — never from the confirm body. Concretely: `fileRef` comes from the ticket, `sizeBytes` and `ETag` come from `head_object`, `mimeType` comes from the signed Content-Type. That preserves the invariant the docstring states. This is a small amount of work if designed in from the start and a security incident if retrofitted.

**Effort.** 3 days if designed in up front

> **Verifier correction.** Two citation fixes, neither of which changes the conclusion:

(a) The docstring is at services/api/src/capabilities.py:234, not 232-233 (232 is the route decorator). Stronger supporting evidence the finding omitted: the handler body at capabilities.py:248-256 INSERTs `file_ref`, `str(body.get("fileName") or "")`, `str(body.get("mimeType") or "")` and `int(body.get("sizeBytes") or 0)` into `ticket_deliverables` with zero validation — no ownership check on the node id, no size ceiling, no server-side lookup. That unvalidated INSERT is what the docstring's invariant is protecting.

(b) capabilities.py:181-183 is NOT a check of a client-claimed value. It sits in the `file_grant` download route (173-185) and compares the *server-stored* manifest's `file_ref` (read from the capability row's `manifest` JSON at capabilities.py:128-131) against the current DB row. Its inline comment states its purpose: "A deleted or replaced source cannot continue exposing the old bytes" — i.e. revocation, not anti-forgery. It is consistent with the theme (file identity is always derived server-side) but should not be cited as a second instance of the same invariant.

Confirmed exactly as claimed: the gatew

### `moderate` Presigned upload breaks the iOS write queue's single-call idempotency contract

**Evidence.** apps/ios/PattadarKit/Sources/PattadarKit/Storage/WriteQueue.swift:565-598 — one `uploader.upload(fileURL:to:headers:mimeType:fileName:)` call to `api/gateway/storage/files`, which either returns a node id or throws; the surrounding mutations carry `headers: ["x-idempotency-key": "\(entry.id.uuidString)-photo"]` (:541,:546). The failure classifier at :600-630 assumes a single HTTP outcome per queue entry: "409 is the idempotency layer saying 'in flight'; 410 is it saying 'landed once, answer gone' — the one code that must reach a human," with a `[2, 8, 30, 120, 600, 3600]` backoff. Name uniqueness is also protected on the server side by `storage_nodes_unique_name` (sql/init.sql:41-43) and `next_free_name` (storage.py:112-131), and the client compensates with an entry-id suffix (WriteQueue.swift:584-586).

**Impact.** A three-step upload has 2^3 partial-failure states, and the queue's classifier was written for one. Specific breakages: an entry that succeeded at presign and PUT but failed at confirm will, on retry, re-presign and re-PUT — a second full 14 MB upload over a phone's cellular connection, then possibly a second node. The 409/410 idempotency semantics do not extend across three calls. And the `[2, 8, 30, …]` backoff applied to "which of three steps failed" produces retry storms on the cheap step (presign) and starvation on the expensive one (PUT).

**Recommendation.** Make the presign step allocate and persist the node_id and version_id server-side and return them, then make confirm strictly idempotent on that version_id — confirming twice returns the same node, and re-presigning for an entry that already has a node_id returns the *same* key so a re-PUT overwrites rather than duplicates. Store the presign response in the queue entry so a crash resumes at PUT rather than restarting at presign. Then extend `classify()` with a per-step notion. Budget this properly: the offline write queue is the most subtle code in the iOS app and this is not a mechanical change.

**Effort.** 2-3 weeks for the iOS side alone

> **Verifier correction.** The claim stands, but one detail of the impact/recommendation is overstated. The write queue is NOT purely single-outcome-per-entry: it already carries a persisted per-step checkpoint. `Entry.uploadedNodeID` (WriteQueue.swift:84) is documented as "Set the moment the storage upload succeeds, so a crash between upload and record creation re-uses the node instead of re-uploading," and the drain path honors it at :371-377 (`var nodeID = entry.uploadedNodeID; if nodeID == nil { ... entries[i].uploadedNodeID = nodeID; save() }`). What is genuinely unchanged: `classify()` (:607-625) still resolves exactly one HTTP status into one retry/park decision with one shared `[2, 8, 30, 120, 600, 3600]` backoff and one `attempts` counter per entry, so a three-call presign/PUT/confirm sequence does need a per-step notion of which call failed, and the 409/4

### `positive` Multipart upload is not needed and should not be built — the real corpus tops out at 14 MB

**Evidence.** Live data from `pattadar_hub.storage_nodes` (kind='file', 227 files): largest object 14,439,064 bytes (13.8 MB, application/pdf); per-owner averages 4.5 MB (shankarreddy.t), 2.6 MB (sankara.telukutla), 4.3 KB (w360-demo). Mime distribution: 140 pdf, 63 mp4, 33 png, 33 jpeg, 8 webp, 3 gif, 1 heic, 1 text. The costing model independently assumes 5 MB average (docs/specs/2026-09-19-platform-costing-model.md:230). The current cap is 100 MB (routes/storage.py:46). AWS single-PutObject limit is 5 GB; the practical multipart threshold is ~100 MB.

**Impact.** None — this is the part of the proposal that is comfortably sound. A single presigned PUT (or POST policy) covers 100% of the real corpus with 7x headroom to the current 100 MB cap and 350x headroom to the 5 GB single-PUT limit.

**Recommendation.** Do not presign multipart. It would mean presigning CreateMultipartUpload, N × UploadPart (max 10,000 parts, min 5 MB each except the last) and CompleteMultipartUpload, plus client-side part orchestration and abort handling on three platforms — weeks of work for a file size nobody uploads. Revisit only if you start accepting video: the 63 stored mp4 files are currently tiny (max 7,429 bytes) but real deed videography would change this. Set `STORAGE_MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES` explicitly rather than relying on the default, and make the presign/POST-policy enforce it.

**Effort.** none — this is a decision not to build
