## Amplify as build/host

Two different products are hiding behind the word "Amplify" and the answer splits cleanly. Amplify Gen2 (TypeScript AppSync + DynamoDB backend) is not viable here: it means rewriting 36,845 lines of Python across three FastAPI services exposing 270 GraphQL operations and 69 REST routes over 83 Postgres tables — a 12-18 month solo rewrite with no product delivered. Amplify Hosting is a genuine option for the static frontends, but it collides head-on with three of this platform's load-bearing behaviours: Amplify's reverse-proxy rewrite has an unconfigurable ~30s response ceiling and does not stream, while apps/web/src/api/client.ts:55 budgets 600,000 ms for uploads and SSE, services/gateway/src/routes/proxy.py:218 proxies assistant SSE with a 200s timeout, and services/gateway/src/routes/storage.py:46 accepts 100 MB multipart uploads. The repo's existing infra/terraform/modules/runtime/cloudfront.tf already solves all of this with a tunable origin_read_timeout and an explicit zero-CORS same-origin design. Two parts of the proposal are already built or nearly free: auth.pattadar.com exists as a Cognito custom domain (persistent/cognito.tf:464-484) and needs no Amplify, and apps/university genuinely has no deployment path today — it appears nowhere in .github/workflows or scripts/deploy-release.py — which is the one real gap the subdomain plan addresses. Amplify's one honest advantage is atomic deploys and per-branch previews, which would retire ~45 lines of homegrown sync/backup/rollback machinery in scripts/deploy-release.py. That is not worth giving up a tunable origin timeout, an existing WAF, Terraform-native IaC and CloudFront's perpetual 1 TB/month free tier.

### `blocker` Amplify Gen2 backend means rewriting 36,845 lines of Python, 270 GraphQL operations and 69 REST routes over 83 Postgres tables

**Evidence.** `find services -name "*.py" -not -path "*/tests/*" | xargs wc -l` → 36,845 total. GraphQL surface: 160 `@strawberry.mutation` + 110 `@strawberry.field` = 270 operations, in services/api/src/main.py (159, Query at main.py:1594, Mutation at main.py:3021) and services/api/src/web360.py (111), mounted at main.py:7140 `graphql_app = GraphQLRouter(schema, path="/graphql", context_getter=_graphql_context)`. REST: 24 route decorators in services/api/src, 31 in services/gateway/src, 14 in services/assistant/src. Datastore: 83 public-schema tables, 42 FK constraints, schema applied at boot by idempotent DDL under an advisory lock. Gateway owns 9 more tables in services/gateway/sql/init.sql. AppSync has a hard 30-second maximum request execution time that is not adjustable — which recreates the previous finding at the API layer too.

**Impact.** A solo founder, pre-launch, would spend an estimated 12-18 months reproducing behaviour that already works locally, and would ship nothing in the meantime. The idempotency machinery alone (main.py:6773-6895, the /graphql-scoped replay guard for retried createPassbook/recordPayment) has no AppSync equivalent and would be hand-rebuilt. The 42 FK constraints and the multi-row transactional workflows (payments, governance, ticketing) have no DynamoDB analogue without redesigning every write path.

**Recommendation.** Reject Amplify Gen2 as the backend outright. apps/university/ARCHITECTURE.md already states the correct posture — "Keep catalog authoring and financial workflows relational unless their access patterns clearly justify a move" and DynamoDB only at step 5 of the delivery sequence. Keep FastAPI. If the goal is cheaper always-on compute, the middle path is Lambda (Mangum adapter over the existing ASGI app) or App Runner, not a rewrite — but note Lambda's 15-minute ceiling and 6 MB synchronous payload limit collide with the 100 MB upload path unless presigned S3 uploads land first.

**Effort.** rewrite (12-18 months solo) if pursued; 0 if rejected

> **Verifier correction.** Every cited number is exact. One definitional refinement: of the 270 strawberry resolvers, 12 are field resolvers on output types (ParcelType 5, RegisteredDocumentType 4, PassbookType 2, PropertyType 1), so the client-callable operation surface is 258 — Mutation 90 + Query 57 (services/api/src/main.py) and WebMutation 70 + WebQuery 41 (services/api/src/web360.py), reached via the namespace pattern at main.py:3021-3023 where `Mutation.web` returns `web360.WebMutation()`. Also, the boot DDL under the advisory lock spans main.py:5576 (`SELECT pg_advisory_lock(918273645)`) through main.py:6768 (unlock), wider than the 5470-6100 range given in the background facts. Neither change affects the severity or the recommendation.

### `major` auth.pattadar.com is already built as a Cognito custom domain and cannot be an Amplify app

**Evidence.** infra/terraform/modules/persistent/cognito.tf:464-471 `resource "aws_cognito_user_pool_domain" "custom" { domain = var.custom_auth_domain ... managed_login_version = 2 }`, with the A/AAAA alias at cognito.tf:474-484 pointing at `aws_cognito_user_pool_domain.custom[0].cloudfront_distribution`. It is wired in prod: infra/terraform/envs/prod/persistent/main.tf sets `custom_auth_domain = "auth.pattadar.com"`. The header comment (cognito.tf:414-417) states the purpose and a precondition: "the parent apex already resolves via an A record (CloudFront), which Cognito requires before accepting the domain."

**Impact.** Item 4 of the proposal ('auth.pattadar.com -> authentication routing') is done. A Cognito custom domain is served by a CloudFront distribution AWS owns; you cannot host an Amplify app on that hostname, and there is nothing to route there beyond Cognito Managed Login v2. Separately, the documented precondition is a sequencing hazard: if pattadar.com's apex A record is torn down while re-pointing the domain topology, recreating the Cognito custom domain will be refused.

**Recommendation.** Leave auth.pattadar.com entirely alone and remove it from the Amplify scope. When moving the apex to a new origin, use `allow_overwrite = true` alias swaps (as runtime/route53.tf already does at lines 12-13 and 26-27) so the apex A record never ceases to exist, rather than destroy-then-create.

**Effort.** 0 (already shipped); 1 day of sequencing care during any apex move

> **Verifier correction.** Same claim, with exact line spans: the Cognito custom domain resource is cognito.tf:464-472, the A/AAAA alias is cognito.tf:474-486 (alias block 481-485), and the precondition comment is cognito.tf:416-419. The apex `allow_overwrite = true` precedent is runtime/route53.tf:12 and :28. Also note `aws_route53_record.auth_alias` itself lacks `allow_overwrite`, while the cert-validation record at cognito.tf:448 has it.

### `major` The five-subdomain split destroys the repo's explicit zero-CORS same-origin design and invalidates every registered Cognito callback

**Evidence.** infra/terraform/modules/runtime/cloudfront.tf:1-4 header: "/api/* proxied to the ALB (SAME-ORIGIN API — the browser never leaves https://pattadar.com, so no CORS anywhere)", and cloudfront.tf ordered_cache_behavior "/api/*" comment "Same-origin API: browser calls https://pattadar.com/api/* — no CORS." The distribution's `aliases = [var.web_domain, local.www_domain]` — apex and www only, no subdomains. infra/terraform/modules/persistent/variables.tf:113-123: `spa_callback_urls` default `["https://pattadar.com/auth/callback"]`, `spa_logout_urls` default `["https://pattadar.com/"]`. .github/workflows/deploy.yml bakes `VITE_COGNITO_REDIRECT_URI=https://pattadar.com/auth/callback` into the build. apps/web/src/api/client.ts:112 `const GRAPHQL_PATH = '/api/gateway/pattadar/graphql'` — every call is a relative path assuming same-origin.

**Impact.** Moving the app from pattadar.com to platform.pattadar.com changes the browsing origin, so: (a) Cognito matches redirect_uri byte-for-byte and every existing callback breaks until `spa_callback_urls` is updated and applied; (b) tokens in browser storage are origin-scoped and every signed-in session is dropped; (c) if each subdomain calls api.pattadar.com cross-origin, the gateway must grow a per-origin CORS allowlist and a preflight path, on a service whose design rule (per the superseded Next.js plan's invariants) is that it strips client identity headers and injects x-user-id from validated claims — CORS preflight and credentialed cross-origin fetch are new attack surface on exactly that boundary.

**Recommendation.** If you want platform.pattadar.com, keep the same-origin property: give each subdomain its own CloudFront distribution (or one distribution with all five aliases) that carries its own /api/* behaviour to the same ALB origin, so every browser still calls a relative /api path. Budget the Cognito work explicitly: add the new callback/logout URLs to spa_callback_urls BEFORE the DNS flip, keep the old ones registered through the transition, and update VITE_COGNITO_REDIRECT_URI in deploy.yml in the same commit. Note the memory-recorded prior incident: a port change alone already caused redirect_mismatch failures.

**Effort.** 1-2 weeks including Cognito app-client work and a staged callback cutover

> **Verifier correction.** Same claim, with three adjustments: (1) the Cognito callback/logout defaults are at infra/terraform/modules/persistent/variables.tf:112-122 (not 113-123); (2) impact (b) — dropped sessions — applies to local/dev and any future live sessions, not to prod today, since the runtime layer is destroyed and /api/* already returns 502, so there are no prod sessions to lose; (3) add a fourth cost the finding omits: infra/terraform/modules/runtime/cloudfront.tf:90 sets subject_alternative_names = [local.www_domain], so the ACM cert covers pattadar.com + www.pattadar.com only, not *.pattadar.com — every new subdomain alias requires an expanded or wildcard us-east-1 cert with DNS validation before the distribution will accept it. Strengthening evidence for the core claim: a grep of apps/web/src for VITE_API_BASE/API_BASE/baseUrl/BASE_URL returns zero hits, so there is no configurable API origin anywhere in the SPA — every call is relative (client.ts:112, AuthProvider.tsx:137) and the same-origin property is unconditional, not a default.

### `major` apps/university has no deployment path at all today — this, not Amplify, is the real gap the subdomain plan is reaching for

**Evidence.** `grep -rn "university" .github/workflows/ scripts/deploy-release.py` returns nothing. .github/workflows/deploy.yml builds only `bun run --filter @pattadar/web build` and scripts/deploy-release.py:239-240 syncs only `apps/web/dist` to the SPA bucket. Root `bun run build` (package.json) does run `--filter '@pattadar/*' build`, so university is typechecked and built in CI — and then thrown away. apps/university/ARCHITECTURE.md already specifies the intended topology: "Route53 university.pattadar.com -> CloudFront + WAF -> S3 static University bundle".

**Impact.** 7,087 lines of university TypeScript plus a full SEO prerender pipeline are unreachable in production. This is a genuine missing capability and it is what makes the subdomain proposal feel necessary. But it is a hosting gap, not an Amplify-shaped gap — ARCHITECTURE.md already names CloudFront+S3 as the answer.

**Recommendation.** Add `university.pattadar.com` as a second CloudFront alias/distribution with its own S3 bucket, extend deploy-release.py with a `--university-bucket`, and add `bun run --filter @pattadar/university build` to deploy.yml. Critical detail: apps/university/scripts/generateSeo.ts:170,184 writes `states/index.html` and `states/{slug}/index.html` — the existing `spa_router` CloudFront function (cloudfront.tf) rewrites any extension-less URI to /index.html, which would swallow all 36 prerendered state pages and silently destroy the SEO work. The function needs a branch that appends `/index.html` and falls back, exactly as ARCHITECTURE.md says ("CloudFront should prefer the exact route object (or append /index.html) before applying the SPA fallback").

**Effort.** 3-5 days

### `major` Amplify Hosting is more expensive than the CloudFront+S3 already in the repo, at every volume this platform will see pre-launch

**Evidence.** AWS Amplify pricing (checked 2026-09-21): hosting data transfer $0.15/GB after a 15 GB/month free allowance that is part of the 12-month free tier; storage $0.023/GB-month after 5 GB; build $0.01/minute after 1,000 minutes; WAF integration $15/month per Amplify app on top of WAF's own charges. CloudFront's free tier is perpetual, not 12-month: 1 TB data transfer out and 10 million HTTPS requests per month, plus 2 million CloudFront Functions invocations. The built bundle is 9.3 MB: `du -sh apps/web/dist` → 9.3M, of which assets 5.6M (xlsx 416K, jspdf 392K, leaflet 148K, html2canvas 196K), vm/ geojson 2.5M, service-visuals 1.3M.

**Impact.** 15 GB/month of Amplify allowance is roughly 1,600 uncached full loads of a 9.3 MB bundle. CloudFront's 1 TB is roughly 110,000 — a ~68x difference, and CloudFront's stays free forever while Amplify's expires after 12 months. Five subdomains as five Amplify apps with WAF = $75/month in Amplify WAF surcharge alone, versus $0 surcharge for attaching a WAF ACL to a CloudFront distribution (cloudfront.tf already builds `aws_wafv2_web_acl.web` with three managed rule groups). Credits cover this today, but the memory note is explicit that credit expiry is the thing to watch.

**Recommendation.** Stay on CloudFront+S3. If the bundle size matters to you regardless of host, the cheap win is lazy-loading xlsx (416K) and jspdf (392K) behind the export actions and moving the 2.5 MB of village-map geojson out of the bundle to fetched assets.

**Effort.** 0 to keep CloudFront; ~2 days for the bundle-splitting side note

### `major` Amplify does not help the 502: the minimum path back to a serving API is a runtime-layer terraform apply, and hosting work delays it

**Evidence.** pattadar.com serves the SPA but /api/* is 502 and api.pattadar.com does not resolve — because the runtime layer (infra/terraform/modules/runtime: alb.tf, ecs.tf, rds.tf, vpc.tf, cloudfront.tf, route53.tf) has been destroyed. runtime/route53.tf's `api` A-record aliases `aws_lb.main.dns_name`, so the hostname disappears with the ALB. The path back already exists as code: .github/workflows/platform-up.yml, plus deploy.yml which resolves `ecs_cluster_name`, `spa_bucket_name`, `cloudfront_distribution_id` from terraform outputs, builds gateway/api/assistant images for linux/arm64, and calls scripts/deploy-release.py.

**Impact.** The API is down because there is no compute and no load balancer, not because of anything to do with static hosting. Every hour spent on Amplify is an hour not spent restoring the ALB/ECS/RDS layer, and Amplify Hosting would still return 502 from the same absent origin.

**Recommendation.** Sequence it plainly: (1) `terraform -chdir=infra/terraform/envs/prod/runtime plan` and review what the destroy left behind, especially RDS — confirm whether a snapshot exists before applying, because a fresh RDS with the boot-time DDL gives you an empty schema, not your data; (2) apply the runtime layer with enable_cdn/enable_waf as they were; (3) let CI's deploy.yml roll images and sync the SPA; (4) verify /api/* and api.pattadar.com resolve. Only then consider hosting topology. Note the prerequisite from memory: the terraform digest-revert issue was fixed in the 2026-09-19 audit, so a terraform apply should no longer stomp a digest deploy — verify that before applying.

**Effort.** 1-3 days to restore, assuming RDS data is recoverable; unknown if it is not

### `moderate` Amplify contradicts the repo's Terraform + GitHub-OIDC conventions and cannot be fully provisioned as code

**Evidence.** The repo runs a strict two-layer Terraform split (infra/terraform/modules/persistent + modules/runtime, envs/prod/{persistent,runtime}) with everything gated on flags like `var.enable_cdn` and `var.enable_waf`. .github/workflows/deploy.yml uses `aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials` with `role-to-assume: ${{ vars.AWS_DEPLOY_ROLE_ARN }}` and `permissions: id-token: write` — OIDC, deliberately no long-lived credentials (there is a `github_oidc.tf` in modules/persistent). `aws_amplify_app` requires either `access_token`/`oauth_token` (a long-lived GitHub PAT that lands in Terraform state) or a GitHub App connection that must be completed by hand in the Amplify console and cannot be created by the provider. Amplify quota: Domains per app defaults to 5 per region (adjustable); Apps defaults to 25; concurrent jobs 5.

**Impact.** Either a GitHub PAT enters Terraform state (a credential the repo has specifically avoided), or the repo acquires a console-managed resource that Terraform can never fully reconcile — permanent drift in a codebase whose whole infra story is 'terraform plan is the truth'. Also note the Terraform state backend already has no DynamoDB lock table, so drift is already the weak spot; adding a partially-console-managed resource makes it worse.

**Recommendation.** If Amplify Hosting is adopted anyway, confine it to a single app and accept the console GitHub App connection as a documented manual step in a runbook, with the app itself imported into Terraform afterwards. Better: don't adopt it, and spend the same effort adding the DynamoDB state lock table the backend is missing.

**Effort.** 1 week if adopted; the state-lock fix is ~2 hours

### `moderate` assistant.pattadar.com has nothing to host — the assistant is a backend SSE service with no frontend of its own

**Evidence.** services/assistant has no package.json and no frontend; it is a FastAPI service returning `StreamingResponse(..., media_type="text/event-stream")` (services/assistant/src/main.py:183-186). The browser reaches it only through the gateway: services/gateway/src/routes/proxy.py mounts the streaming passthrough, apps/web/vite.config.ts proxies `/api/gateway/assistant` to the gateway, and apps/web/src/api/client.test.ts:17 asserts `/api/gateway/assistant/chat/stream` gets the 600s budget. The apps/web bundle is the assistant's only UI.

**Impact.** Giving the assistant its own subdomain means one of two things, and neither is an Amplify job: either you build a new assistant SPA (new product surface, new Cognito client, new callbacks), or you expose a raw SSE API on a public hostname — which would move the chat stream cross-origin and, on Amplify Hosting, hit the 30s buffered-response ceiling head-on.

**Recommendation.** Drop assistant.pattadar.com from the topology unless a standalone assistant product is actually being built. Today the assistant is a feature of the app, and it should stay at `platform.pattadar.com/api/gateway/assistant/*` same-origin. If a standalone surface is wanted later, it is a new SPA decision, not a hosting decision.

**Effort.** 0 to drop; 4-6 weeks if a standalone assistant SPA is actually wanted

### `minor` Bun workspaces are not in Amplify's monorepo auto-detection list

**Evidence.** Root package.json: `"packageManager": "bun@1.3.14"`, `"workspaces": ["apps/*", "packages/*", "tests/*"]`, with `"@pattadar/core": "workspace:*"` dependencies in apps/web/package.json and a 214 KB bun.lock. Every CI step uses `oven-sh/setup-bun` + `bun install --frozen-lockfile` (.github/workflows/ci.yml:19-23, deploy.yml). AWS documents Amplify monorepo auto-detection for npm workspaces, pnpm workspaces, Yarn workspaces, Nx and Turborepo — bun is not on that list, and bun is not in the default Amplify build image.

**Impact.** You would hand-write amplify.yml with AMPLIFY_MONOREPO_APP_ROOT plus a preBuild step that curls the bun installer on every build, or maintain a custom build Docker image. Build reproducibility depends on that install pinning 1.3.14 exactly; a floating bun install would silently diverge from the lockfile CI validates against. apps/university also needs bun specifically — its build is `bun run seo:assets && vite build && bun run seo:prerender` and generateSeo.ts is run with `bun`.

**Recommendation.** Annoyance, not a blocker — but it is real per-app maintenance for every one of the five subdomains. The existing GitHub Actions path already has bun pinned correctly and costs nothing.

**Effort.** 1-2 days per app to set up, ongoing maintenance thereafter

> **Verifier correction.** Evidence stands, with tightened citations: root package.json declares `"packageManager": "bun@1.3.14"` and `"workspaces": ["apps/*","packages/*","tests/*"]`; bun.lock (214 KB) is the ONLY lockfile in the repo — no package-lock.json, yarn.lock or pnpm-lock.yaml exist; apps/web/package.json:23-24 and apps/mobile/package.json:15-16 use the `workspace:*` protocol; bun 1.3.14 is pinned via oven-sh/setup-bun in every JS-touching job (ci.yml:19-22, 60-63, 118-121; nightly.yml:21-24; deploy.yml:36, 54) — the cited range is 19-22, not 19-23, and ci.yml:17-18 runs a python step first so "every CI step" should read "every JS-touching job". apps/university/package.json build is verbatim `bun run seo:assets && vite build && bun run seo:prerender`, with both seo scripts executing apps/university/scripts/generateSeo.ts (8.4 KB TS, no build step) directly under bun — so university needs bun as a runtime, not merely as a package manager. Add: deploy.yml:55 currently builds only `@pattadar/web` into an S3/CloudFront SPA bucket, so apps/university has never had a hosted build config — Amplify would be adding per-app build config for four new surfaces, not porting existing ones. The AWS-side claim (bu

### `positive` SSR is genuinely off the table — both frontends are pure Vite SPAs, which removes Amplify's strongest technical argument

**Evidence.** apps/web/package.json build is `vite build`; apps/web/vite.config.ts has no SSR config and all proxies are explicitly marked DEV-ONLY ("never in a build"). apps/university/package.json build is `bun run seo:assets && vite build && bun run seo:prerender` — build-time static generation, not server rendering. docs/plans/2026-07-26-web-nextjs-minimals-migration.md:3-9 carries a superseded banner: "`apps/web-next` was removed from the repo: it duplicated `apps/web` closely enough that every defect had to be fixed twice, and it never reached deployable parity. This document is kept as the record of what was decided and why, not as work to pick up. The browser client is `apps/web`." deploy.yml confirms it operationally: "There is no web container: the Next.js app that would have produced one was removed."

**Impact.** Amplify Hosting's differentiator is managed SSR (Next.js/Nuxt compute, $0.30/M requests + $0.20/GB-hour). With no SSR anywhere in the repo, that entire capability is dead weight. Hosting here is a static-file problem, and CloudFront+S3 already solves it in code that exists.

**Recommendation.** Keep it that way. If SEO for university needs to grow, extend generateSeo.ts (build-time prerender) rather than reintroducing SSR — the repo already paid for and abandoned the SSR path once.

**Effort.** n/a

> **Verifier correction.** Two refinements, both strengthening rather than weakening the finding:

(a) university is not merely "build-time static generation" of metadata — it is true content prerendering. apps/university/scripts/generateSeo.ts:107 substitutes a rendered snapshot for `<div id="root"></div>` in the built index.html, gated by `isDistribution` (line 14). So the SEO story is already solved at build time for the routes listed in sitemapEntries (lines 115-124).

(b) A hosting consequence worth carrying into the Amplify section: because those prerendered routes are written as discrete files (e.g. `states/index.html`), the host must serve them as real static paths and NOT blanket-rewrite every 404 to `/index.html` the way a naive SPA rewrite does — otherwise the prerendered HTML is never served to crawlers. This is a config detail on any host (CloudFront function / Amplify rewrite rules), not a differentiator for either.

### `positive` Amplify's real advantage is atomic deploys and branch previews, which would retire fragile homegrown release machinery

**Evidence.** scripts/deploy-release.py:212-254 hand-rolls the release: it copies the live bucket to `s3://<bucket>/releases/<id>/previous/` as a backup (line 220), syncs `apps/web/dist` excluding index.html with `--cache-control no-cache` (line 239), then copies index.html last (line 240) — the comment at line 237-238 explains this ordering exists precisely to avoid serving old public files mid-sync — then runs a full `aws cloudfront create-invalidation --paths '/*'` and blocks on `wait invalidation-completed` (lines 205-207), with a rollback that re-syncs from the backup prefix and invalidates again (lines 251-254). Amplify Hosting does atomic deploys natively and gives per-branch preview URLs for free.

**Impact.** The current path has a genuine visible window during the sync where hashed assets and index.html can disagree, and a `/*` invalidation plus wait adds 1-3 minutes to every deploy. It also has no preview environments at all, which for a solo founder reviewing UI changes is a real cost.

**Recommendation.** Acknowledge this honestly: it is the one thing Amplify does better here. But it is not worth the 30s proxy ceiling, the WAF surcharge, the Terraform drift and the loss of the perpetual CloudFront free tier. The cheaper fix for the same problem is versioned asset prefixes (deploy to `/v/<sha>/` and flip only index.html) so the deploy becomes atomic by construction and the `/*` invalidation shrinks to `/index.html`. That is ~half a day of work on a script that already exists.

**Effort.** 0.5 days for the versioned-prefix fix vs 2-4 weeks to migrate hosting

> **Verifier correction.** Amplify's one real advantage here is per-branch preview URLs — confirmed absent: no preview infrastructure exists anywhere in infra/terraform or .github. Atomicity is NOT an advantage: scripts/deploy-release.py:239-240 already syncs hashed assets first with no `--delete` (old assets survive for open tabs and rollback, per the comment at 218-219) and flips index.html last with a single `cp`, which is atomic by construction. The comment cited as explaining that ordering is actually at 236-237 and explains something else — the `spa_changed=True` flag placed before the first mutation so a partial upload still triggers restore. The remaining genuine cost is scripts/deploy-release.py:205-207, a full `/*` CloudFront invalidation followed by a blocking `wait invalidation-completed` on every deploy — and that is likely dead weight, since every object is uploaded with `--cache-control no-cache` (lines 239 and 240), so the cheap fix is to drop or narrow the invalidation, not to re-architect the asset layout.

#### Refuted and dropped

- ~~Amplify Hosting's ~30s rewrite-proxy ceiling breaks uploads, document extraction polling and assistant SSE — all three have explicit >30s budgets in the code~~ — Overstated, and three of its AWS facts are wrong or misapplied. (1) CloudFront numbers are wrong: the origin **Response timeout** default is **30s, not 60s**, and **180s is the plain maximum settable value — no service-quota request is needed** to go from 30 to 180 (a quota request is only needed to exceed 180). The "60" the finding calls the AWS default is this repo's own `variable "cloudfront_origin_read_timeout" { default = 60 }`. Source: docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonCloudFront/latest/DeveloperG

---

## Cost model

The migration cannot save meaningful money because there is almost no money being spent. The entire transactional dataset is 21 MB (measured: `public` schema = 0.0209 GB across 85 tables), and the whole always-on prod footprint prices out at roughly $100/month — which independently confirms the founder's own "$80–$100/month at small scale" line (pricing-model.md:234). Right now it is closer to $5–8/month because `scripts/platform-down.sh` has already destroyed the runtime layer, so the migration's savings at this stage are approximately zero. Against that ceiling sits a rewrite of 1,033 SQL statements and 1,541 execute/fetch call sites across 45,428 lines of Python with 83 tables and 42 FK constraints — 4 to 8 months solo, with a payback period past 100 months. Two parts of the proposal are genuinely right and should be done regardless of the database: S3 presigned uploads (the gateway currently does `data = await file.read()` on a 100 MB cap inside a 1 GiB Fargate task) and Amplify Hosting for the two pure-static Vite SPAs. The real cost story is elsewhere entirely: the 289.75 GB `land` corpus — 112 GB of it HNSW vector index — is not in the RDS plan (`db.t4g.micro`, 20 GiB) and is explicitly excluded from the migration runbook, yet hosting it costs $290–$1,170/month, which is 2–7x the entire ₹15,000/month infrastructure baseline the costing model assumes covers everything.

### `blocker` The 289.75 GB land corpus is the single largest cost item on the platform and appears in neither the costing model nor the deployed infrastructure

**Evidence.** Measured: `psql -d pattadar -c "select schemaname, sum(pg_total_relation_size(...))/1024^3 ..."` → land = 289.75 GB (11 tables), public = 0.0209 GB (85 tables), ref = 0.0009 GB. Top tables: land.party 35 GB (19 GB indexes), land.bv_p0..bv_p7 28 GB each (14 GB indexes each = 112 GB of HNSW), land.real_estate_records 28 GB. Indexes confirmed: `CREATE INDEX bv_p0_embedding_idx ON land.bv_p0 USING hnsw (embedding halfvec_cosine_ops) WITH (m='16', ef_construction='64')` ×8. Deployed RDS is infra/terraform/modules/runtime/variables.tf:107 `db_instance_class default = "db.t4g.micro"` (2 GiB RAM) and variables.tf:113 `db_allocated_storage default = 20` GiB. docs/runbooks/migration.md:11 excludes it outright: `pg_dump -h localhost -U rhub -N land ...` ("exclude the legacy 'land' schema"). docs/specs/2026-09-19-platform-costing-model.md §2.3 allocates ₹15/account-month for "Shared ECS, RDS, edge, monitoring" — the corpus is nowhere in the model.

**Impact.** HNSW must be resident in RAM to serve ANN at interactive latency; 112 GB of index plus 19 GB of pg_trgm indexes on land.party needs a db.r6g.xlarge (32 GiB, ~$292/mo) at the low end and a db.r6g.4xlarge (128 GiB, ~$1,170/mo) to hold the vector indexes properly — plus ~320 GiB gp3 at ~$0.1145/GB-mo = $36.60/mo and post-deletion snapshot storage at $0.095/GB-mo. At 1,000 accounts the costing model's whole infrastructure envelope is ₹15,000/mo ≈ $176; the corpus alone is 1.7x to 6.6x that, user-independent, from day zero. Instance-class prices are ap-south-1 estimates extrapolated from us-east-1 on-demand — treat the range, not the endpoints, as the finding. DynamoDB cannot absorb any of this: service.py:403 uses `similarity(p.name_norm, %(query)s)` with `pg_trgm.similarity_threshold` 0.35–0.6, service.py:492-496 uses `embedding <=> CAST(... AS halfvec(1024))` ORDER BY with `hnsw.ef_search`, service.py:407 `JOIN {records_fqn} r USING (rid, s_no)`, service.py:750 `count(DISTINCT village_norm)`. None of fuzzy match, vector ANN, join, or distinct-count exists in DynamoDB.

**Recommendation.** Cost the corpus explicitly as its own line before deciding anything about DynamoDB. Then split it out of the OLTP path: export land.real_estate_records and land.party to district/year-partitioned Parquet in S3 (~40–80 GB after dropping the 131 GB of indexes) and query with Athena at $5/TB scanned — a partitioned 2 GB scan costs $0.01, and S3 storage is ~$1.50–2.00/mo. Keep only the vector search on Postgres, on an instance started on demand, or replace the bge-m3 + HNSW path with a precomputed candidate shortlist. If neither is funded, ship without the public-records assistant tools and say so, rather than carrying a $300–1,200/mo line the pricing model does not fund.

**Effort.** 3-6 weeks for the Parquet/Athena path; the vector-search replacement is a separate 3-4 week question

> **Verifier correction.** Keep the finding, with four evidence fixes: (1) land.party's 19.4 GB is its four indexes combined, of which the pg_trgm GIN index (idx_party_name_trgm) is 3.70 GB and party_pkey is 11.70 GB — do not attribute 19 GB to pg_trgm; (2) pure HNSW index footprint is ~108.3 GB (bv_p0_embedding_idx = 13.54 GB x 8), not 112 GB, and total index footprint across the corpus is ~131 GB; (3) the RDS is not deployed — the runtime layer is destroyed, so db.t4g.micro / 20 GiB is the class the module would create on the next platform-up, and rds.tf has no max_allocated_storage so there is not even storage autoscaling, making 290 GB vs a fixed 20 GiB volume a hard mismatch rather than merely an undersized one; (4) the migration.md `pg_dump -N land` exclusion is at line 13 (repeated at 122), not line 11.

### `blocker` The migration's total addressable saving is bounded at ~$100/month, and today it is ~$0 because the runtime layer is already destroyed

**Evidence.** Per-line ap-south-1 model, 730 hr/mo, from the actual terraform: 3 ARM64 Fargate tasks (ecs.tf:191-198, 267-274, 451-458; `task_cpu` 512 / `task_memory` 1024, variables.tf:60-70; prod.auto.tfvars `desired_count = 1`) → 0.5 vCPU × $0.04048 + 1 GB × $0.004445, ×0.8 for Graviton = $0.019748/hr = $14.42/task-mo × 3 = $43.25. 3 public IPv4 @ $0.005/hr (vpc.tf:1-7 documents this) = $10.95. ALB $0.0225/hr = $16.43 + ~1 LCU @ $0.008/hr = $5.84 → $22.27. RDS db.t4g.micro ~$0.018/hr = $13.14 + 20 GiB gp3 @ $0.1145 = $2.29 → $15.43. WAF (cloudfront.tf:138-160, 3 managed rule groups) $5 ACL + 3 × $1 = $8.00. KMS 2 CMKs (kms.tf:84, kms.tf:112) = $2.00. Route53 $0.50. ECR ~$0.30. CloudWatch ≈ $0 (9 alarms vs 10 free; Container Insights already disabled at ecs.tf:16-19 after being measured at ~$18/mo). Total ≈ $102/mo — which matches pricing-model.md:234 "$80–$100/month at small scale". Current actual: scripts/platform-down.sh destroys the entire runtime layer, so the live bill is persistent-only ≈ $5–8/mo. Rewrite scope, measured: 1,033 SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE statements and 1,541 `.execute(`/`.fetch` call sites across 45,428 lines of Python in services/, with 83 public tables, 42 FK constraints, and boot-time idempotent DDL in main.py ~5470-6100.

**Impact.** A serverless rebuild at idle costs roughly $9/mo (DynamoDB 0.021 GB × ~$0.285/GB-mo = $0.006; PITR at $0.20/GB-mo = $0.004; Lambda and API Gateway inside permanent free tier; Amplify 5 apps × ~100 build-min × $0.01 = $5; Route53 + KMS + S3 unchanged). Versus today's $5–8/mo actual, the saving is negative. Versus the $102/mo all-on figure, the ceiling is ~$93/mo ≈ ₹7,900. A 4–8 month solo rewrite at even ₹1.5L/month of founder opportunity cost is ₹6–12 lakh to capture ₹7,900/month: payback 75–150 months, before counting the bugs a 1,541-site datastore swap introduces into a pre-revenue product. Unit prices for ap-south-1 Fargate/ALB/RDS/DynamoDB are extrapolated from published us-east-1 rates and should be confirmed against the calculator before any decision rests on the exact number; the order of magnitude is not in doubt.

**Recommendation.** Do not migrate the transactional core. It is 21 MB — it fits in the free tier of literally any datastore, so the database is not what you are paying for. Keep Postgres, keep the raw-SQL layer, and keep using platform-down.sh as your scale-to-zero. Revisit only if a measured RDS or Fargate line actually exceeds ₹5,000/month, which on current data will not happen before 50k accounts.

**Effort.** the migration itself is 4-8 months; not doing it is 0

> **Verifier correction.** Same finding, with three factual tweaks: (1) the local public-schema footprint is 27 MB, not 21 MB (`pg_total_relation_size` sum; the 290 GB database total is the `land` corpus, which is not part of the migration argument); (2) there are four ECS services in the module, not three — `web` (ecs.tf:580/630) exists but runs at `web_desired_count = 0` by default (variables.tf:78-82), so three running tasks is the correct cost basis; (3) the all-on estimate omits the ALB's two chargeable public IPv4 addresses (~$7/mo), so the ceiling is ~$109/mo rather than ~$102/mo — which moves the payback from 75-150 months to roughly 70-140 months and changes nothing about the recommendation.

### `major` Lambda/AppSync bills the assistant's streaming wall-clock; the crossover against one Fargate task is ~120 streaming-hours/month

**Evidence.** services/assistant/src/main.py:183-186 returns `StreamingResponse(..., media_type="text/event-stream")`; services/gateway/src/routes/proxy.py:229 proxies it. API Gateway HTTP API has a hard 30-second maximum integration timeout (REST APIs 29s, raisable only by quota request), so SSE past 30s requires Lambda Function URLs in RESPONSE_STREAM mode. Lambda bills $0.0000166667 per GB-second of wall clock, including time spent blocked on the LLM. Break-even against one $14.42/mo Fargate task: $14.42 / $0.0000166667 = 865,200 GB-s; at 2 GB that is 432,600 seconds = 120 hours of streaming per month.

**Impact.** 120 streaming-hours is about 7,200 one-minute assistant conversations per month, roughly 240/day. A single 0.5 vCPU Fargate task multiplexes far more concurrent idle SSE streams than that, because an asyncio task waiting on a socket costs nothing. At 50k MAU with 5 assistant calls each (250,000 calls × 60s × 2 GB = 30M GB-s) Lambda costs ~$500/month for work that costs ~$14–43/month on Fargate. The assistant is exactly the workload where serverless billing is worst: long-lived, I/O-blocked, low-CPU.

**Recommendation.** Keep the assistant on a container no matter what happens to the rest. If you want serverless elsewhere, draw the boundary at assistant.pattadar.com and leave it on ECS/Fargate or App Runner. Do not put SSE behind API Gateway at all — the 30s ceiling is a functional break before it is a cost problem.

**Effort.** n/a — this is a decision not to build something

> **Verifier correction.** Claim stands, with two detail fixes:

(a) "API Gateway HTTP API has a hard 30-second maximum integration timeout" is slightly overstated. Since June 2024 AWS allows the 29s (REST) / 30s (HTTP API) integration timeout to be raised via a Service Quotas request for *both* API types, up to 900s. So it is not a hard functional wall — but it still requires a quota increase per account/region, and even at 900s it does nothing about the billing problem, because Lambda charges wall-clock regardless. The functional argument is better made from Lambda's own 15-minute max and from the fact that buffered (non-RESPONSE_STREAM) integrations defeat SSE's purpose entirely.

(b) The $14.42/mo Fargate baseline is a bit high for the "0.5 vCPU" the impact paragraph names. In ap-south-1, 0.25 vCPU + 1 GB is ~$0.25*730*$0.04656 + 1*730*$0.00511 = $8.50 + $3.73 = ~$12.2/mo; 0.5 vCPU + 1 GB is ~$20.7/mo. Using ~$12/mo moves the crossover to ~100 streaming-hours/month rather than 120 — which makes the finding's case marginally stronger, not weaker.

Supporting evidence the finding should cite: services/gateway/src/routes/proxy.py:221 sets `httpx.Timeout(200.0, connect=10.0)` on the assistant proxy, proving 

### `major` GuardDuty Malware Protection for S3 is costed ~4.5x low in the costing model, and it is the largest per-GB variable cost on the platform

**Evidence.** docs/specs/2026-09-19-platform-costing-model.md §10 estimates "GuardDuty malware scan of 500 GB and 100,000 objects — approximately $65–$70 using published example rates" and an initial non-AI document cost of $85–$115. Published GuardDuty Malware Protection for S3 rates are $0.60/GB for the first 500 GB/month scanned (then $0.35/GB, then $0.25/GB) plus $0.187 per 1,000 objects scanned. That gives 500 × $0.60 = $300 plus 100 × $0.187 = $18.70 ≈ $319. The plan is live in terraform: guardduty.tf:140-147 `aws_guardduty_malware_protection_plan "documents"`. The model's own §12 rule 8 makes scanning mandatory and folds it into the ₹8/GB-month storage reserve.

**Impact.** I am not fully certain of the current ap-south-1 per-GB tier and flag it as the number to verify first — but the founder's figure implies ~$0.13/GB, which is below every published GuardDuty S3 tier I am aware of. If $0.60/GB is right, the 500 GB initial ingestion costs ₹27,000 one-time, not ₹7,500, and consumes about 6.4 months of the ₹8/GB-month storage reserve in a single scan. Scaling the model's own 25 GB/month of new files per 1,000 accounts to 50k MAU gives 1.25 TB/month scanned = 500×$0.60 + 750×$0.35 = $562/month — larger than every compute line combined and completely unaffected by the database choice.

**Recommendation.** Verify the ap-south-1 GuardDuty S3 malware rate against the calculator this week and re-run §10 and the ₹8/GB reserve. If it confirms at $0.60/GB, either raise the storage reserve to cover ingest-time scanning as a separate one-time line, or scan selectively (e.g. only user-uploaded originals, not thumbnails or derived versions) — thumbnails and previews are generated by your own code and do not need a malware gate.

**Effort.** 1 day to verify and re-model

> **Verifier correction.** Claim stands as written on the evidence lens, with three refinements: (1) the terraform resource block is guardduty.tf:140-168, not 140-147, and is conditional on `count = var.manage_org_security ? 1 : 0` (line 141), a variable that defaults to true (infra/terraform/README.md:166) — so it is live where that toggle is on, not unconditionally; (2) the $85–$115 total sits at line 238 and the scan line at line 235, both under §10 which begins at line 228; (3) §14 "Evidence status" (lines 308-321) already classes "Public AWS/Anthropic list-price inputs" as unvalidated planning references and states "Do not call the model validated until at least three months of tagged production or pilot usage and accounting review exist", so the error is an unflagged wrong number inside an already-caveated document rather than an assertion of verified cost. The AWS per-GB tier itself ($0.60/$0.35/$0.25 plus $0.187 per 1,000 objects) is not verifiable from the repo and remains the item to confirm against the ap-south-1 calculator before re-running §10 and the ₹8/GB reserve.

### `major` The one cost lever with the largest effect that is not 'switch databases': keep the 290 GB corpus out of the hot transactional database

**Evidence.** land = 289.75 GB of which 131 GB is index (112 GB HNSW across bv_p0..bv_p7 at 14 GB each, 19 GB pg_trgm/btree on land.party). public = 0.0209 GB. Ratio: the read-only corpus is 13,870x the size of the entire transactional dataset. docs/runbooks/migration.md:11 already excludes it from the cloud migration (`-N land`), and infra/terraform/modules/runtime/variables.tf:113 provisions 20 GiB — so today the corpus exists only on the founder's laptop and the assistant's public-records tools cannot work in production at all.

**Impact.** This single decision swings $290–$1,170/month, every month, at zero users — more than the entire rest of the platform combined at every scale modelled. By comparison the database migration's ceiling is ~$93/month, presigned uploads are worth ~$50-150/month at 50k MAU, and the malware-scan repricing is ~$560/month at 50k MAU but $0 pre-launch. The corpus lever is the only one that is both large and active today, and it is entirely orthogonal to DynamoDB.

**Recommendation.** Three options, cheapest first. (1) Parquet + Athena for the scan/aggregate/timeline queries (service.py:253-264, 727-792) — ~$2/month storage, $5/TB scanned, partitioned by district and year. (2) For the fuzzy party search (service.py:403-413), precompute normalised name keys and n-gram buckets into a small searchable index rather than running pg_trgm over 140M rows live. (3) For the HNSW vector path (service.py:492-501), accept that it needs RAM and either run it on a scheduled/on-demand instance or defer the feature — and note the assistant also configures a local bge-m3 embedder (public_records/settings.py:26-29, ~568M params ≈ 2.3 GB fp32) which cannot load in a 1 GiB Fargate task, so productionising it needs a 4 vCPU / 8 GB task (~$115/month ARM) or Bedrock embeddings per call. Decide and price this before spending a single week on DynamoDB.

**Effort.** 4-8 weeks total across the three paths; option (1) alone is 3 weeks and captures most of the saving

> **Verifier correction.** Same claim with three details fixed: (1) land's index footprint is 135 GB, not 131 GB (112 GB HNSW + ~19.9 GB on land.party + ~3 GB on real_estate_records secondaries); (2) the `-N land` exclusion is at docs/runbooks/migration.md:13 (repeated at :122), not :11; (3) infra/terraform/modules/runtime/variables.tf declares db_allocated_storage at line 110 with `default = 20` at line 112, consumed at modules/runtime/rds.tf:27 — and there is no `max_allocated_storage` anywhere in infra/terraform, so it is a fixed 20 GiB with no storage autoscaling, which makes the "corpus cannot exist in production" point harder than stated. Also note services/assistant/src/public_records/settings.py:30 sets `embeddings_enabled = False` by default, so the bge-m3 / 8 GB-task cost is incurred only when the vector path is turned on.

### `moderate` DynamoDB attribute-name overhead is measurable on this schema and pushes items over the 1 KB write boundary; GSI writes then multiply it

**Evidence.** Measured against the live schema: `properties` 43 columns / 405 bytes of column names; `family_members` 41 cols / 427 bytes; `parcels` 41 / 408; `registered_documents` 39 / 392; `documents` 32 / 273. DynamoDB counts attribute names in item size for both storage and capacity, on every item. The schema has 42 FK constraints, which a single-table design resolves with GSIs; each GSI write is billed as an additional write request unit.

**Impact.** 405–427 bytes of pure attribute-name overhead per item is ~40% of the 1 KB WRU boundary, so a property or family-member item with ordinary field values crosses into 2 WRU instead of 1 — a silent doubling of write cost, PITR cost ($0.20/GB-mo) and storage cost. A 4-GSI single-table design on a 42-FK schema means each logical write bills 5 WRU, and with the 2x size effect, 10. In absolute terms this is still small money here (5M writes/month × 10 WRU × ~$0.7125/million ≈ $36/month at 50k MAU), so it is not a blocker — but it means the naive 'DynamoDB is cheaper' arithmetic is off by ~10x, and the ap-south-1 WRU/RRU rates I used are extrapolated from us-east-1 ($0.625/$0.125 per million) and should be confirmed.

**Recommendation.** If any table does move to DynamoDB, shorten attribute names to 2-3 characters with a mapping layer, and count GSI writes explicitly in the model. But given the transactional dataset is 21 MB total, the honest move is not to model this at all — model the Cognito, S3, egress and malware lines instead, which are 50-100x larger.

**Effort.** n/a unless migrating

> **Verifier correction.** Keep the finding as written, with two fixes:

1. Replace "The schema has 42 FOREIGN KEY constraints, which a single-table design resolves with GSIs" with: "The live `public` schema declares only 2 FK constraints (services/api/src/payments.py:46, :54) — referential integrity is enforced in application SQL, not by the database. (The 42-FK figure circulating in this review counts 40 constraints in the unused, empty legacy `app` schema, which no API query targets.) That makes the GSI argument stronger, not weaker: relationships are implicit, so a single-table design must reproduce every one of them as a GSI or a duplicated item, with no schema-level list to enumerate them from."

2. Keep "a 4-GSI design bills 5 WRU per logical write, 10 with the 2x size effect" as an assumption about access patterns, explicitly not derived from FK count.

All measured evidence stands: properties 43 cols/405 bytes, family_members 41/427, parcels 41/408, registered_documents 39/392, documents 32/273 (all public schema, verified live); public-schema total size 21 MB.

### `moderate` At 50k MAU the bill is Cognito + S3 + CloudFront egress + malware scanning; the database line is ~2% at every scale

**Evidence.** Scaling the costing model's own per-account ratios (§2.1: 0.65 GB retained per account, 0.1 GB/month downloaded per account) to 50,000 accounts: S3 32.5 TB × ~$0.025/GB-mo = $812/mo; CloudFront India egress 5 TB × ~$0.1090/GB = $545/mo; GuardDuty malware 1.25 TB/mo ≈ $562/mo; Cognito Essentials (cognito.tf:66 `user_pool_tier = "ESSENTIALS"`) at $0.015/MAU beyond the 10,000 free tier = 40,000 × $0.015 = $600/mo. Total ≈ $2,519/mo before any compute. DynamoDB for 21 MB scaled 50x is ~1 GB = $0.29/mo storage plus ~$30-60/mo of request units; RDS db.t4g.micro→db.r6g.large is ~$130/mo. Either way the datastore is 1-5% of the bill.

**Impact.** The migration optimises the smallest line on the invoice. The four lines above are identical under both architectures — DynamoDB does not touch a single one of them. Cognito's free tier and per-MAU rate changed with the Nov-2024 Lite/Essentials/Plus repricing and I am not certain whether the 10,000-MAU Essentials free tier is permanent or first-12-months; verify before the model quotes $600.

**Recommendation.** Rewrite §5 of the costing model to lead with these four lines and drop 'RDS' from the ₹15/account-month bundle into its own named line. Then set budget alarms per line (the model's §12 rule 10 already calls for this) and measure for three months before any architecture decision.

**Effort.** 2-3 days of re-modelling

Split RDS out of §2.3 and the §5 aggregates follow automatically.

Keep auth.pattadar.com as the single hosted-UI custom domain in front of that one pool. Separate subdomains for the frontends is fine; separate pools is not.

### `moderate` Aurora Serverless v2 as the hybrid option is a cost regression at this scale, not a saving

**Evidence.** Current RDS is db.t4g.micro (variables.tf:107) at ~$0.018/hr = $13.14/mo + 20 GiB gp3 @ $0.1145 = $2.29 → $15.43/mo total. Aurora Serverless v2 at the long-standing 0.5 ACU minimum costs 0.5 × ~$0.12/ACU-hr × 730 = $43.80/mo (ap-south-1 likely ~$0.14/ACU-hr → ~$51/mo) before storage or I/O. AWS lowered the floor to 0 ACU with auto-pause in Nov 2024, which idles at ~$0 compute, but resume adds ~15s of cold start and Aurora Standard bills $0.20 per million I/O requests. The codebase has 1,541 `.execute(`/`.fetch` call sites with no ORM and no batching layer.

**Impact.** At the 0.5 ACU floor the hybrid costs 3x the current instance. At the 0 ACU floor you trade that for a 15-second cold start on the first request after idle — unacceptable for a login flow — and for an I/O line that a 1,541-call-site raw-SQL app will make the dominant charge, since every un-batched query is billed I/O rather than amortised into an instance you already paid for. Aurora I/O-Optimized removes the per-request charge but raises the ACU rate ~30% and the storage rate ~125%.

**Recommendation.** Reject Aurora Serverless v2 for now. db.t4g.micro at $15/month with platform-down.sh for true zero is already cheaper than either Aurora configuration. Revisit when a measured RDS bill crosses ~$100/month, at which point a provisioned db.m6g/r6g reserved instance will likely still beat Serverless v2.

**Effort.** n/a — a decision not to migrate

> **Verifier correction.** Aurora Serverless v2 as the hybrid option is a cost regression at this scale. Note this is the up-state cost, not today's: RDS is a runtime-layer resource and the runtime layer is currently destroyed (rds.tf:1-5, scripts/platform-down.sh:100-103), so the platform already has a true-zero mode. Aurora Serverless v2 at the 0.5 ACU floor is ~$44-51/mo before storage or I/O — roughly 3x — and the 0-ACU auto-pause floor (Nov 2024) trades that for a ~15s resume on the first request after idle, which is unacceptable in front of a login flow. The I/O line is a genuine risk but not for the reason of raw call-site count: Aurora Standard bills storage-layer page reads and writes, not one I/O per SQL statement, so 1,520 raw-SQL call sites across

### `minor` RDS final snapshots accumulate with no pruning; each park cycle leaves a permanent billed line

**Evidence.** scripts/platform-down.sh:101-103 sets `TF_VAR_final_snapshot_suffix="$(date +%Y%m%d%H%M)"` so "repeated down/up cycles never collide on an existing snapshot name" (rds.tf:55-57). scripts/platform-up.sh:62-68 resolves and restores only "the latest RDS final snapshot pattadar-<env>-pg-final-*". Neither script deletes older ones; no `delete-db-snapshot` call exists in either.

**Impact.** RDS backup storage is free only up to 100% of allocated storage while the instance exists. Once the instance is destroyed — which is the entire point of platform-down.sh — all snapshot storage bills at ~$0.095/GB-month. At 20 GiB that is $1.90/month per retained snapshot, forever, growing by one snapshot per park cycle. Small in absolute terms, but it is the only line on the bill that grows monotonically with an operation the founder is encouraged to run often, and it undermines the 'park to zero' claim.

**Recommendation.** Add a prune step to platform-down.sh after the destroy: keep the newest N (2 is enough for rollback) and `aws rds delete-db-snapshot` the rest. Also check today whether stale snapshots from prior cycles are already billing.

**Effort.** half a day

> **Verifier correction.** scripts/platform-down.sh:101-103 stamps a unique `TF_VAR_final_snapshot_suffix` per run and destroys the runtime, and scripts/platform-up.sh:62-68 restores only the newest `pattadar-<env>-pg-final-*`; no code anywhere in the repo deletes older ones (no `delete-db-snapshot`, no aws_backup resource, no lifecycle rule), so one manual RDS snapshot accumulates per park cycle forever. Cost impact is ~$0.10/month per retained snapshot at today's near-empty DB (the repo's own docs/runbooks/up-down.md:73 estimates the snapshot at ~1 GB), not $1.90 — RDS bills snapshot storage on actual compressed size, not on the 20 GiB `db_allocated_storage` default (infra/terraform/modules/runtime/variables.tf:109-113). The $0.095/GB-month rate and the "free allowance disappears once the instance is destroyed" reasoning are correct. The Terraform citation should be rds.tf:48-51 (skip_final_snapshot + final_snapshot_identifier), not rds.tf:55-57. Recommendation stands: prune to the newest 2 in platform-down.sh, and audit existing snapshots now.

### `minor` AWS Config records a configuration item on every resource change, and the down/up cycle churns hundreds per run

**Evidence.** infra/terraform/modules/persistent/cloudtrail.tf:220-231: `aws_config_configuration_recorder "main"` with `all_supported = true` and `include_global_resource_types = true`, delivered to the logs bucket (line 232-242) and explicitly enabled at line 245-249, all gated on `var.manage_org_security`. The logs bucket lifecycle expires at 400 days (s3.tf:30-42).

**Impact.** Continuous recording bills ~$0.003 per configuration item. A full runtime destroy-and-recreate touches every VPC, subnet, route table, SG, ALB, target group, ECS service, task definition, RDS instance and CloudFront resource — comfortably 200-400 CIs per cycle, so a few dollars per month if the founder parks and unparks weekly. Not material against a $100 bill, but it is pure overhead for a pre-launch single-account setup with no compliance auditor reading it yet.

**Recommendation.** Either switch the recorder to daily-change recording, or scope `recording_group` to the resource types that matter for the SOC2 controls doc (IAM, S3, KMS, security groups) instead of `all_supported`. Leave CloudTrail with object lock as-is — that one is worth the money.

**Effort.** half a day

> **Verifier correction.** Claim stands; tighten two details. (1) Scope: Config recording is on in **prod only** — `envs/dev/persistent/dev.auto.tfvars:10` sets `manage_org_security = false`, so dev churn is free. The waste is confined to whichever account/env owns the singletons. (2) CI count is if anything understated: AWS Config emits a configuration item per resource *change* including relationship changes, so one ECS service create can produce several CIs across the service, task definition, target group and SG — 200-400 per destroy/recreate is a floor, not a ceiling. Dollar magnitude is unchanged (~$1/cycle at $0.003/CI in ap-south-1). Also worth noting the runtime layer is currently destroyed, so no churn is accruing right now — this becomes live spend again the moment the founder starts park/unpark cycles. Recommendation is sound as written; there are no `aws_config_rule` or conformance-pack resources in the repo, so no rule-evaluation charges to consider alongside it.

### `positive` Presigned S3 uploads are the strongest part of the proposal and are worth doing on their own merits, independent of DynamoDB

**Evidence.** services/gateway/src/routes/storage.py:6-7 states plainly: "Metadata in PostgreSQL, bytes in AWS S3 (proxied streaming — no presigned URLs)". The upload handler at storage.py:406-425 does `data = await file.read()` — the entire object into the task's heap — against `MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES = int(os.getenv("STORAGE_MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES", str(100 * 1024 * 1024)))` (storage.py:46), inside a Fargate task sized 512 CPU / 1024 MiB (variables.tf:60-70). Downloads mirror it: services/gateway/src/storage.py:325 `return body.read()`. SSE-KMS is already wired (storage.py:434 `self._s3.put_object(**self._put_args(...))`) and S3 Bucket Keys are on (s3.tf:181 `bucket_key_enabled = true`).

**Impact.** Two concurrent 100 MB uploads exhaust a 1 GiB task before any application memory is counted, so under real load the task must be sized at 2-4 GB — 2x to 4x the Fargate line — purely to buffer bytes that never needed to touch compute. Every uploaded and downloaded byte also crosses the ALB, where the processed-bytes LCU dimension is 1 GB/hour: at the costing model's 50k-MAU-scaled 6.25 TB/month that is 8.6 LCU × $0.008/hr ≈ $50/month of pure pass-through. Presigned PUT/GET removes both lines and removes the 100 MB cap as a product constraint.

**Recommendation.** Do this next, before anything else in the proposal. Generate presigned PUT with the SSE-KMS headers enforced by condition (the terraform already has `enforce_documents_sse_kms_headers`), have the client PUT direct to S3, and keep the gateway for metadata and the post-upload commit. Presigned GET for downloads. Note the GuardDuty scan gate is tag-based on new PUTs (migration.md:47-52), so confirm the scan-tag bucket policy still admits presigned uploads before cutting over.

**Effort.** 2-3 weeks including the iOS, web and mobile clients

> **Verifier correction.** Presigned S3 uploads are the strongest part of the proposal and stand on their own merits independent of DynamoDB. The gateway today proxies every byte through compute: routes/storage.py:6-7 states "bytes in AWS S3 (proxied streaming — no presigned URLs)", uploads do `data = await file.read()` (routes/storage.py:421) and downloads return the whole object from memory (services/gateway/src/storage.py:325 `return body.read()`, routes/storage.py:345 `Response(content=data, ...)`) — so it is buffered, not streamed, in both directions, inside a 512 CPU / 1024 MiB Fargate task (modules/runtime/variables.tf:60-70). The sharpest memory defect is ordering: the 413 check (routes/storage.py:424) runs AFTER the full read, so an oversized POST is buffered into the heap in full before it is rejected — MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES (routes/storage.py:46) is not a memory bound. The ALB pass-through cost is real but small at documented volumes: the costing model plans 100 GB/month download (:31) plus ~25 GB/month new bytes (:32) = ~0.17 LCU on the 1 GB/hr processed-bytes dimension, roughly $1/month, not $50 — there is no 50k-MAU or 6.25 TB figure anywhere in the costing or pricing specs. So the case for presigne

### `positive` Amplify Hosting for the two static SPAs is correct and cheap; it would not replace CloudFront for the API path

**Evidence.** apps/web/package.json `"build": "vite build"` and apps/university/package.json `"build": "bun run seo:assets && vite build && bun run seo:prerender"` — both produce pure static output with no SSR runtime. The existing CloudFront is a deliberate single-origin design: cloudfront.tf:1 "SPA hosting + edge: private S3 bucket behind CloudFront via OAC, /api/*", with cloudfront.tf:260-262 warning that dropping the www_redirect function "would let www.<domain> resolve as a second origin and break the single-origin Cognito-callback rule".

**Impact.** Amplify Hosting at $0.01/build-minute, $0.023/GB stored and $0.15/GB served is a reasonable fit for static Vite bundles and removes the S3+OAC+CloudFront+CloudFront-Function plumbing for the marketing and university sites. But Amplify's $0.15/GB served is ~38% more than CloudFront's ~$0.1090/GB India rate, so it is a convenience win rather than a cost win, and it is meaningless for document bytes. It also does not host FastAPI — 'use Amplify to build' cannot cover services/api, services/gateway or services/assistant.

**Recommendation.** Put pattadar.com and university.pattadar.com on Amplify Hosting — they are static, independently deployable and have no Cognito callback coupling. Leave platform.pattadar.com on the existing CloudFront distribution, because its same-origin /api/* behaviour is what keeps the Cognito callback allowlist to one entry. Budget Amplify build minutes explicitly if CI builds on every push across five apps.

**Effort.** 1 week for the two static sites

### `positive` The VPC deliberately has no NAT gateway and S3 Bucket Keys are already on — the two cost traps this review was asked to check for are already closed

**Evidence.** infra/terraform/modules/runtime/vpc.tf:1-7: "2-AZ public-subnet VPC. Deliberately NO NAT gateways (~$35/mo each saved): Fargate tasks run in the public subnets with public IPs and tight security groups... Each running task's public IPv4 costs ~$3.6/mo ($0.005/h) — still far cheaper than NAT." infra/terraform/modules/persistent/s3.tf:181 `bucket_key_enabled = true` on the documents bucket. ecs.tf:16-19 records that Container Insights was measured at ~$18/mo across both envs and disabled. Terraform state uses native S3 locking (envs/prod/runtime/backend.tf `use_lockfile = true`), so no DynamoDB lock table is needed either.

**Impact.** NAT Gateway ($0.045/hr = $32.85/mo each plus $0.045/GB processed, typically $70-100/mo for a 2-AZ setup) is the usual single largest surprise line in a container architecture, and it is already avoided with the tradeoff documented. SSE-KMS without Bucket Keys would bill a KMS API call per PUT and per GET at $0.03/10,000 requests — at 100,000 objects with versioning that is real money, and it is already off. The most common arguments for 'serverless is cheaper' therefore do not apply to this codebase.

**Recommendation.** Nothing to change. Cite vpc.tf:1-7 and s3.tf:181 in the costing model's §14 evidence table so the next reviewer does not re-litigate them. The only caveat: s3.tf:11-19 and cloudtrail.tf:30-40 use AES256 rather than KMS for the logs and CloudTrail buckets, which sidesteps the question entirely for those two — confirm that is an intentional compliance position, not an oversight, against docs/compliance/soc2-controls.md.

**Effort.** 0

The CloudTrail bucket (cloudtrail.tf:30-40, AES256 at :37) is the only one where the choice is unexplained and worth a one-line confirmation against docs/compliance/soc2-controls.md; CloudTrail *can* deliver to an SSE-KMS bucket, so that one is a real (if minor) decision to record rather than a constraint.
