---

## DPDP / SOC2 / data lifecycle

This codebase has an unusually good compliance substrate for a pre-launch solo-founder product, and almost all of its strength is load-bearing on PostgreSQL specifically. Erasure is not a hand-written delete script — `ownership_predicates()` derives the erasure/export scope from `information_schema` at runtime (67 of 85 tables in scope today, 19 of them reachable only through 2-3 level join subqueries), and `foreign_children()` reads `pg_constraint`'s 42 FKs to refuse cascading into another owner's rows. Neither mechanism has a DynamoDB equivalent: you lose automatic coverage of new entity types and gain a hand-maintained erasure registry that will silently drift. The audit trail is worse: `audit_events_v2` is a gapless hash chain serialized through a `FOR UPDATE` lock on `audit_chain_head`, redacted-in-place rather than deleted precisely so erasure does not punch holes in the seal — and DynamoDB TTL, the obvious retention mechanism (and the one already written into `apps/university/ARCHITECTURE.md:190`), deletes whole items best-effort within ~48h, which both breaks `verify_chain` and makes the stated 1095-day retention unevidenceable. Separately, S3 presigned URLs are sound for upload *throughput* but delete two controls that exist in code today: the 100 MB server-side byte cap (`storage.py:423`) and the `download_document`/`recipient.download` security-class audit events. Residency is largely fine and mostly a non-issue — the us-east-1 ACM cert for `auth.pattadar.com` is a certificate, not customer data, and Amplify Hosting for static Vite SPAs is residency-neutral. The real residency/architecture risk is not geography, it is that the natural Amplify pattern (browser → Cognito Identity Pool credentials → DynamoDB with `dynamodb:LeadingKeys`) removes the API from the request path and therefore removes the audit trail and the `x-user-id` trust boundary entirely.

### `blocker` Erasure scope is derived from information_schema at runtime; DynamoDB has no catalog, so coverage becomes a hand-maintained registry that drifts silently

**Evidence.** services/api/src/account.py:100-140 `ownership_predicates(catalog)` builds the per-table WHERE clause from the live column list. services/api/scripts/erase_account.py:29-34 `catalog()` reads `SELECT table_name,column_name FROM information_schema.columns WHERE table_schema='public'`; :36-45 `snapshot()` counts every scoped table; :113-117 selects `ctid` per table; :150-158 topologically orders the DELETEs. Running the real function against the live `pattadar` DB: 85 tables total, **67 in erasure scope**, **19 reachable only via nested subqueries** (`associate_areas, associate_credentials, associate_disciplines, associate_events, beneficiaries, document_parties, documents, invitations, parcel_fields, parcel_owners, parcel_photos, parcels, payment_operations, payment_webhook_events, property_owners, property_photos, service_requests, shared_kit_checks, shared_kit_items`). `documents` is three levels deep: `documents -> parcels -> passbooks -> owner_user_id`. There is no DescribeTable-equivalent that tells you which DynamoDB item types carry an owner.

**Impact.** Today, adding a table with an `owner_user_id` column puts it in export and erasure automatically, with zero code change — that property is why 67 tables are covered by ~300 lines. In DynamoDB every new item type must be manually added to an erasure registry. The failure mode is silent: the erasure job reports `completed` (erase_account.py:315) and `verify()` (erase_account.py:292-298) passes, because verify only re-runs the same registry. A DPDP erasure that leaves a person's family members or parcel photos behind is a reportable failure the code will affirmatively tell you did not happen.

**Recommendation.** Do not migrate erasure to a registry. If DynamoDB proceeds, make ownership structural, not discovered: every item gets `PK = OWNER#{ownerId}` so erasure is a single-partition Query + BatchWrite, and any item type that cannot be owner-partitioned is refused at design review. Add a CI test that enumerates every write path in the codebase and fails if it writes a PK shape not present in the erasure enumerator — that is the only mechanical replacement for the catalog. Budget the registry as a permanent tax, not a one-time migration cost.

**Effort.** 3-4 weeks to build an owner-partitioned key design plus the CI enumerator; the registry drift risk is permanent

> **Verifier correction.** Erasure scope is derived from `information_schema` at runtime; DynamoDB has no catalog, so coverage becomes a hand-maintained registry that drifts silently.

`services/api/src/account.py:94-137` `ownership_predicates(catalog)` builds a per-table WHERE clause purely from the live column list — `account.py:105` picks `owner_user_id`/`recipient_user_id` if present, with special cases for `users`, `audit_events` and `audit_events_v2`/`audit_outbox` (`:109-116`). `services/api/scripts/erase_account.py:29-33` `catalog()` reads `SELECT table_name,column_name FROM information_schema.columns WHERE table_schema='public'`; `:36-45` `snapshot()` counts every scoped table; `:113-117` selects `ctid` per table; `:146-158` orders the DELETEs topologically, and critically `:148` derives that order from the live FK catalog: `SELECT conrelid::regclass::text child,confrelid::regclass::text parent FROM pg_constraint WHERE contype='f'`.

Run against the live `pattadar` DB: **85 base tables, 67 in erasure scope, 18 correctly excluded** (all reference data: states, villages, mandals, deed_types, fee_schedule, platform_settings, …). **19 tables carry an additional nested-subquery fallback** (`associate_are

### `blocker` Whole-account erasure is one Postgres transaction with a table lock; DynamoDB caps a transaction at 100 items / 4 MB, so erasure becomes non-atomic

**Evidence.** services/api/scripts/erase_account.py:111-158: `with conn.transaction():` then `LOCK TABLE <all 67 scoped tables> IN SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE MODE`, then resolve every target `ctid`, then DELETE in topological order — all-or-nothing. The module docstring (:5-6) states the guarantee: "A failed stage leaves an honest retryable job, never a completed deletion." DynamoDB `TransactWriteItems` is capped at 100 items and 4 MB; `BatchWriteItem` at 25 items and 16 MB; a `Query` page returns at most 1 MB. An account with a few thousand items needs 40+ non-atomic BatchWriteItem calls.

**Impact.** The stage-level idempotency in `stage()` (erase_account.py:261-272) survives, but the within-stage atomicity does not. A throttle or crash mid-sweep leaves an account whose parcels are gone and whose `family_members` (which hold `aadhaar_enc`, main.py:6218) remain. The current design cannot produce that state; DynamoDB's can. Worse, the DELETE ordering at :150-158 exists so children never outlive parents — with per-item deletes you get orphan child items that no owner-scoped Query will ever find again, which means they are unerasable by the same tool that created the orphans.

**Recommendation.** Accept non-atomicity and compensate: delete leaf-to-root, write a durable per-item-type progress cursor into `account_erasure_jobs.stages` (it is already JSONB and already per-stage, erase_account.py:258-272), and make `verify()` a full owner-partition Query that must return zero items — not a per-table count against the same registry. Never mark `completed` until a fresh Query returns empty on every partition and every GSI that could hold an orphan.

**Effort.** 2-3 weeks

> **Verifier correction.** Two small tightenings, neither of which changes the finding's severity or direction:

(a) 67 tables are LOCKed (`sorted(scopes)`, :112), but only 66 have their ctids resolved and deleted — `account_erasure_jobs` is skipped in `snapshot()` (:37-38) and in the targets loop (:115-116). Additionally `audit_events_v2` and `audit_outbox` are popped out of `targets` at :141-142 because the centralized trail is REDACTED IN PLACE (UPDATE at :136-140) rather than deleted. So the atomic DELETE sweep covers ~64 tables, plus one in-place redaction and one retention INSERT, all inside the same transaction — the all-or-nothing claim holds for all of it, including the redaction.

(b) "40+ non-atomic BatchWriteItem calls" understates it: at 25 items per BatchWriteItem, "a few thousand items" is 100-200+ calls, and the deletes must first be discovered by paginated 1 MB Queries. The exposure window is larger than the finding states, not smaller.

Worth adding to the recommendation: the audit path is the sharper problem, not just the orphan path. Today the redaction of `audit_events_v2` and the DELETE of every owned row commit or roll back together under `SET LOCAL pattadar.audit_maintenance = 'on'` (

### `blocker` DynamoDB TTL is structurally incompatible with the audit hash chain and cannot evidence a fixed retention period

**Evidence.** services/api/src/audit.py:441-478 defines `audit_events_v2` with `seq BIGINT`, `prev_hash`, `row_hash`; :494-501 the single-row `audit_chain_head`; :861-891 appends under `SELECT seq, head_hash FROM audit_chain_head WHERE id=1 FOR UPDATE` so the chain is gapless. :683-714 `redact_owner()` explains the design in its own words: "Deleting the owner's rows outright ... punches holes in the middle of the chain, and a hole is indistinguishable from tampering." So retention is implemented as *redaction in place* (`audit_retention.py:42` -> `audit.redact_expired`), never deletion. Retention classes at audit.py:67-72 are 1095 days (security, standard) and 365 (short). AWS DynamoDB TTL **deletes the whole item** and is explicitly best-effort — AWS documents expired items as typically deleted within 48 hours, with no guarantee and longer tails on large tables. `apps/university/ARCHITECTURE.md:190` already writes `TTL = retention deadline` into the proposed `UniversityTutor` table.

**Impact.** Two independent failures. (1) TTL on the audit table removes a row's `seq`/`prev_hash`/`row_hash` position — `verify_audit_chain.py` exits 1 with "CHAIN BROKEN" on the next run, permanently, for every expired event. The script's own docstring (:17-19) allows a gap at the *start* of the chain from retention, but TTL expiry is per-item by `data_class`, so `short`-class rows expire at 365 days while `security` rows around them live to 1095 — that is a hole in the *middle*, which the docstring says "is not" explainable. (2) For SOC 2, a control that says "3 years" needs a demonstrable deletion date. "1095 days plus an unbounded best-effort tail" is not a retention period you can attest to, and over-retention of personal data is itself a DPDP storage-limitation problem.

**Recommendation.** Do not enable TTL on anything that carries a retention claim or a chain position. Keep `audit_retention.py` as a scheduled redaction job (EventBridge -> Lambda under a maintainer role), doing conditional `UpdateItem` that clears content attributes and refuses to touch `seq`/`prevHash`/`rowHash` — that is a direct port of the trigger's logic at audit.py:540-548. Use TTL only where late deletion is harmless and no document claims a date.

**Effort.** 2 weeks to port the redaction sweeper; the ARCHITECTURE.md TTL line should be corrected now regardless

> **Verifier correction.** Same finding, with two detail fixes: (1) per-item expiry is driven by `retention_class` (audit.py:454; RETENTION_DAYS lookup at audit.py:858 and expires_at computed at :871-889), not `data_class` (audit.py:453, which is only a data-sensitivity label feeding the audit_events_v2_security index). (2) The verifier's docstring caveat is at verify_audit_chain.py:16-19 and the ARCHITECTURE.md TTL line is :189. Additionally, the strongest supporting quote is audit.py:1184-1189 in `redact_expired`, which states the incompatibility in the codebase's own words: "REDACT, never DELETE. Retention classes differ (a low-signal event expires at 1 year, a security event at 3), so expiry order is NOT chain order: deleting expired rows would punch holes through the middle of the chain and every later verification would report tampering that never happened."

### `blocker` The natural Amplify + DynamoDB pattern (browser -> Cognito Identity Pool -> DynamoDB) deletes the audit trail and the x-user-id trust boundary

**Evidence.** services/api/src/audit.py:127-232 defines ~60 audited actions — `reveal_aadhaar` (:127, CLASS_SECURITY, security=True), `download_document` (:154), `recipient.download` (:210), `desk_read` (:138), `account.export` (:230) — every one written by the API on the request path. services/gateway/src/main.py:116-132 `StripIdentityHeadersMiddleware` removes client-supplied `x-user-id` from the raw ASGI scope; docs/compliance/engineering-checklist.md marks this `[x] [phase-1]` and notes "The api trusts this header unconditionally". `dynamodb:LeadingKeys` can scope a browser credential to its own partition, but it cannot write an audit row, cannot apply `require_purpose` consent gating (docs/runbooks/account-data.md:16-20), and cannot enforce `CONSENT_STRICT`.

**Impact.** If any read or write path goes browser-direct to DynamoDB, the tamper-evident trail goes blind for exactly the operations SOC 2 cares about most: who read whose land records. The audit trail then documents a subset of access and *looks complete*, which is worse for an auditor than no trail. DPDP consent enforcement also becomes unenforceable for those paths — an account that withdrew `document_processing` can still read its own items directly.

**Recommendation.** State explicitly, in docs/architecture/service-layout.md, that Amplify hosts static assets only and that no browser ever holds AWS data-plane credentials. Every DynamoDB call stays behind the gateway->API path. This keeps Amplify sound (see the positive finding) and keeps the audit trail whole. If a direct-access path is ever wanted for latency, it must be read-only, non-personal reference data.

**Effort.** 0 if decided now; a rewrite if discovered after the Amplify migration ships

> **Verifier correction.** Keep the finding, with two tightenings. (1) Scope of the consent point: require_purpose (services/api/src/account.py:216, CONSENT_STRICT at :235) gates only document_processing, ai_extraction and service_notifications — so the DPDP break applies to processing/extraction paths, not to every own-item read; drop the blanket "an account that withdrew document_processing can still read its own items directly". (2) Frame it as conditional: the founder proposed Amplify for build/host, not explicitly a browser-direct data plane; the blocker materialises only if the default Amplify DataStore / aws-amplify client pattern (Cognito Identity Pool credentials -> DynamoDB) is adopted. Strengthen the evidence while doing so: the trail is hash-chained and tamper-evident (audit.py:501 audit_chain_head, :792 audit_outbox, :871 audit_events_v2 with seq/prev_hash/row_hash) and audit.py:240-242 makes reveal_aadhaar, desk_read and account.export fail-closed — a browser-direct path does not merely skip logging, it silently breaks the chain's completeness guarantee that the fail-closed design exists to provide.

### `blocker` Aadhaar ciphertext is bound by KMS encryption context to the owner id AND the record id — re-keying during migration makes it permanently undecryptable

**Evidence.** services/api/src/aadhaar.py:92-101 `_context()` returns `{app, environment, purpose, owner_ref: sha256(owner)[:32], record, schema}`; :122-140 passes it as `EncryptionContext` to both `kms.encrypt` and `kms.decrypt`. :193-196 `encrypt_number(raw, owner, subject_kind, subject_id)` sets `purpose=f"aadhaar-{subject_kind}"` and `record=subject_id`. Call sites: main.py:3349-3350 `encrypt_number(value, uid, "member", member["id"])` into `family_members.aadhaar_enc`; main.py:5353 `encrypt_aadhaar(kyc_ref, uid, "account", uid)` into `users.kyc_ref_enc` (main.py:5796). KMS returns `InvalidCiphertextException` on any context mismatch and there is no override.

**Impact.** A DynamoDB migration that changes `owner_user_id` (e.g. to a `subject_...` principal, which docs/runbooks/identity-migration.md is already about) or that regenerates `family_members.id` as part of a new PK/SK scheme destroys every Aadhaar value irrecoverably. There is no decrypt-with-old-context fallback in the code — `_decrypt_bytes` (aadhaar.py:175-191) has exactly one KMS path and one legacy Fernet path. This is unrecoverable data loss on the most sensitive field in the platform, discovered only when a user tries to reveal their number.

**Recommendation.** Make it a hard migration invariant: `users.id`, `family_members.id` and `owner_user_id` are carried byte-for-byte into DynamoDB attributes, and the DynamoDB PK is *derived from* them, never a replacement. Before any bulk move, run a read-only decrypt-count harness over every non-empty `aadhaar_enc`/`kyc_ref_enc` (counts only — the runbook forbids printing inventories, docs/runbooks/aadhaar-kms-rollout.md:8-12), and re-run it after. If any id must change, you must decrypt-and-re-encrypt under the new context inside a reviewed maintenance window, which is a separate approval under that runbook's Gate 1.

**Effort.** 1 week for the harness; weeks and an approval gate if any id actually changes

> **Verifier correction.** Two refinements, both widening/narrowing rather than overturning: (1) Line range: `encrypt_number` is aadhaar.py:193-198, not 193-196. (2) Blast radius is not literally "every Aadhaar value" — it is every row whose token carries the `kms-direct:v1:` prefix. Rows written through the legacy Fernet path (`_encrypt_bytes` :161-168, gated on `AADHAAR_LEGACY_WRITE_BRIDGE=1` or local/test + `ALLOW_INSECURE_LOCAL=1`) are context-free and survive any id change. Since new KMS writes in non-local environments additionally require `AADHAAR_KMS_WRITES_ENABLED=1` (aadhaar.py:157, echoed in docs/runbooks/aadhaar-kms-rollout.md), the migration must first count how many stored tokens actually start with `kms-direct:v1:` — that count, not the row count, is the exposure. (3) Worth adding: `owner_ref` and `record` are not the only migration-fragile context members. `environment` is `os.getenv("APP_ENV", "local")` (aadhaar.py:96) and `purpose` is `f"aadhaar-{subject_kind}"` (:197), so promoting data between environments, or renaming the "account"/"member" subject kinds while reshaping entities into a single-table DynamoDB model, breaks decryption by exactly the same mechanism as an id change. The invar

### `major` DynamoDB's default AWS-owned encryption key contradicts the platform's own written SOC 2 control

**Evidence.** docs/compliance/soc2-controls.md, Encryption (CC6.1): "Customer-managed KMS CMK for RDS PostgreSQL and the S3 documents bucket (SSE-KMS); key rotation enabled — [template]". infra/terraform/modules/persistent/s3.tf:173-182 implements exactly that for the documents bucket (`aws_kms_key.main.arn`, `bucket_key_enabled = true`). DynamoDB encrypts at rest by default with an **AWS-owned** key: no key policy, no key-usage entries in your CloudTrail, no rotation you control, no ability to revoke. The AWS-managed `aws/dynamodb` key and a customer-managed CMK both give key policy and CloudTrail visibility; a CMK adds ~$1/month plus modest KMS request charges (DynamoDB caches the table key, so this is per-table-per-few-minutes, not per item).

**Impact.** Moving `users`, `family_members`, `documents` and `parcels` — the Identity and Land/property rows in the ROPA (gdpr-dpdp.md) — onto a table with the AWS-owned key is a regression against a control the company has already written down and will be audited on. It also removes the ability to prove key usage (CloudTrail) and to perform a cryptographic-erasure argument for backups.

**Recommendation.** Customer-managed CMK on every DynamoDB table holding personal data, in the same `modules/persistent/kms.tf` pattern as the S3 CMK, with rotation enabled. Use the existing dedicated Aadhaar key for the field ciphertext (unchanged — that is application-layer and independent of table encryption). Budget ~$1/month/key; the request charge is negligible at this scale. Do not use the AWS-owned key anywhere personal data lands.

**Effort.** 2 days

> **Verifier correction.** All citations verified verbatim and current (soc2-controls.md CC6.1 "At rest" row; s3.tf:173-182; kms.tf:83-88 main CMK with enable_key_rotation, kms.tf:112-121 dedicated Aadhaar key; gdpr-dpdp.md:9,11,12 ROPA rows). Two refinements: (1) the AWS-managed `aws/dynamodb` key does produce CloudTrail key-usage events and its policy is viewable, but it is not editable and its rotation is not controllable, so it is not an acceptable substitute — the recommendation (customer-managed CMK per table holding personal data, in the modules/persistent/kms.tf pattern, rotation enabled) is unchanged; (2) severity is better framed as medium/"cheap if decided now": `server_side_encryption { enabled = true, kms_key_arn = ... }` is one Terraform attribute and can even be changed on a live table, so this is a must-not-forget default rather than a hard blocker. Worth adding as supporting evidence: apps/university/ARCHITECTURE.md:126-150 specifies the proposed DynamoDB tables in detail and says nothing about table encryption keys, while the same doc (lines 44, 206) does mandate KMS for S3 — i.e. the omission is already present in the repo's own DynamoDB design.

### `major` DynamoDB is an internet-facing API endpoint; the Aadhaar ciphertext blast radius widens from VPC-only to credential-only

**Evidence.** Today `family_members.aadhaar_enc` (main.py:6218) and `users.kyc_ref_enc` (main.py:5796) live in RDS. infra/terraform/modules/runtime/ has vpc.tf + security_groups.tf, and docs/compliance/engineering-checklist.md states the api "must never be exposed directly (security-group: ALB->gateway only, gateway->api only)". A leaked runtime credential today still needs network reachability into the VPC. `dynamodb.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com` is reachable from anywhere on the internet with only a valid SigV4 signature.

**Impact.** A leaked task-role credential (or a compromised CI OIDC role) becomes immediately sufficient to Scan every Aadhaar ciphertext, every land record and every phone number from outside AWS. The ciphertext is still KMS-protected, so full digits need a second grant (`kms:Decrypt` on the dedicated Aadhaar key with the exact encryption context) — that layering holds and is genuinely good. But names, DOB, phone, addresses and the full land portfolio are plaintext attributes, and that is a DPDP-reportable breach on its own.

**Recommendation.** Attach a DynamoDB resource-based table policy (GA since March 2024) with an explicit Deny on `aws:SourceVpce` != your gateway VPC endpoint, plus a VPC gateway endpoint for DynamoDB in modules/runtime/vpc.tf. That restores the network condition you have today. Keep the Aadhaar KMS key policy constrained by encryption context as it already is (docs/runbooks/aadhaar-kms-rollout.md, 'least-privilege API permissions constrained by encryption context').

**Effort.** 3-4 days

> **Verifier correction.** Keep the finding, with two corrections. (a) The blast-radius widening applies to the *relational* PII only (family_members.aadhaar_enc at main.py:6218, users.kyc_ref_enc at main.py:5796, plus names/DOB/phone/address/portfolio). Document bytes are already credential-only reachable: services/gateway/src/storage.py:155-169 writes every scan to S3, and `grep -rn vpc_endpoint infra/terraform/` returns zero results while vpc.tf:1-7 says "Deliberately NO NAT gateways … TODO(Phase 2): add private subnets + VPC endpoints" — so api/gateway tasks already call S3 and KMS over the public internet. The Aadhaar KMS "second grant" is therefore already the sole barrier for full digits, not a network+credential pair. (b) The recommendation is net-new work, not parity restoration: there is no VPC endpoint of any kind in the Terraform today. A DynamoDB gateway endpoint is free (no hourly or data charge, unlike interface endpoints), so the cost objection is nil, but it must be built alongside the resource-based table policy denying aws:SourceVpce != that endpoint, or every task loses DynamoDB access the moment the policy lands.

### `major` Presigned PUT cannot enforce the 100 MB upload cap; only presigned POST can

**Evidence.** services/gateway/src/routes/storage.py:46 `MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES = int(os.getenv("STORAGE_MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES", str(100 * 1024 * 1024)))`; :423 `if len(data) > MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES:` — the cap is enforced on bytes the gateway actually counted. services/gateway/src/main.py:55 sets the ASGI body limit from it. AWS S3: `s3:content-length-range` is a **POST policy** condition — it exists in the browser-form presigned-POST policy document and has no equivalent for a presigned PUT signature. A presigned PUT allows anything up to the 5 GB single-PUT limit; presigned multipart allows up to 5 TB.

**Impact.** The planned per-user storage quota (engineering-checklist.md, '[ ] [phase-2] Per-user storage quotas in the gateway document-storage API') loses its only enforcement point before it is built. More concretely today: `storage_versions.size_bytes` (storage.py:437-442) becomes a client-asserted number instead of a counted one, which silently invalidates step 3 of docs/runbooks/rds-restore-drill.md:49-64 — the drill compares DB size against `head-object` ContentLength and calls a match proof that metadata and bytes are consistent. Against a client-asserted size that check proves nothing.

**Recommendation.** Use presigned **POST** with a policy carrying `content-length-range` and the `x-amz-server-side-encryption` / `x-amz-server-side-encryption-aws-kms-key-id` conditions, not presigned PUT. Then, after the client signals completion, always `HeadObject` server-side and write the *returned* ContentLength into `storage_versions.size_bytes` — never the client's number. Turn on the default-off `enforce_documents_sse_kms_headers` gate (s3.tf:281-320) before the first presigned upload, since the gateway is no longer the only writer.

**Effort.** 1-2 weeks

> **Verifier correction.** Claim stands with two corrections. (1) Scope the AWS fact: `s3:content-length-range` is a presigned-POST policy condition with no bucket-policy or presigned-PUT equivalent, so a presigned PUT cannot enforce a size *range*; it can, however, pin an *exact* size by including `Content-Length` in the SigV4 SignedHeaders (client declares size at presign time, gateway checks it against the quota before signing). A plain presigned PUT with unsigned Content-Length is unbounded to the 5 GB single-PUT limit as claimed. So the correct statement is "presigned PUT cannot express a range cap; use presigned POST with content-length-range, or sign an exact Content-Length." (2) The rds-restore-drill impact is weaker than stated: docs/runbooks/rds-restore-drill.md:49-64 queries `sum(size)` and `size` from storage_versions, but the actual column is `size_bytes` (services/gateway/sql/init.sql, written at services/gateway/src/storage.py:439) — that drill step does not currently run, so it is an already-broken control rather than one the proposal silently invalidates. The live impact is the real one: storage_versions.size_bytes stops being a counted number (storage.py:412 `size = len(data)`) and becomes 

### `major` Presigned GET blinds the download audit events and cannot express the existing share-link expiry

**Evidence.** services/api/src/audit.py:154 `_spec("download_document", CLASS_PERSONAL, "document", ...)` and :210 `_spec("recipient.download", CLASS_SECURITY, "document", "security", security=True, ...)` — the second is security-class with 1095-day retention and rides the hash chain. `share_links` carries `expires_on`, `opened_count`, `last_opened_at`, `revoked` (verified in the live schema). A SigV4 presigned URL maxes at 7 days, and when signed with temporary credentials — which an ECS task role is — it dies when the session token expires, typically well under 12 hours.

**Impact.** Three things break. (1) One `url_issued` event replaces N downloads: you can no longer answer "who downloaded this deed and when", which is the question a land-record dispute turns on. (2) The URL is a bearer capability — forwarding it grants access to anyone, and `revoked` on `share_links` cannot revoke an already-issued presigned URL. (3) `opened_count`/`last_opened_at` stop being real. S3 server access logs (configured at s3.tf:193-199) partially compensate but are best-effort, delivered hours late, and are not in the tamper-evident chain or the owner-facing activity view.

**Recommendation.** Split the two cases. For *uploads*, presign freely — that is where the latency win is and there is no read-access question. For *downloads*, keep them proxied through the gateway so the audit event is written, or issue presigned GETs with a lifetime measured in tens of seconds, one per click, with the audit event written at issuance and the URL scoped to a single object version. Do not presign anything for the `share_links` recipient path — `revoked` must mean revoked. Enable CloudTrail S3 data events on the documents bucket as the compensating record and say so explicitly in soc2-controls.md.

**Effort.** 1-2 weeks

> **Verifier correction.** Presigned GETs would collapse N audited byte-fetches into one issuance event and hand out an unrevocable bearer URL, breaking two things that exist today in code: services/gateway/src/routes/storage.py:345-366 writes `download_document` (audit.py:154, personal class) as a BackgroundTask on the response that carries the bytes, and services/gateway/src/routes/capabilities.py:85-100 writes `recipient.download` (audit.py:210, CLASS_SECURITY, security=True, 1095-day retention per audit.py:67-69, on the tamper-evident chain). `share_links.revoked` cannot revoke an already-issued presigned URL, and a URL signed by an ECS task role dies only when the session token does (role sessions cap at 12h, 1h typical) — well inside the 7-day SigV4 ceiling but still far longer than a click. TWO sub-claims must be dropped: (1) `opened_count`/`last_opened_at` are NOT affected — they are incremented in the manifest endpoint at services/api/src/capabilities.py:154-156, which a recipient hits regardless of how bytes are served; (2) CloudTrail S3 data events are already wired at infra/terraform/modules/persistent/cloudtrail.tf:186-197, but as `read_write_type = "WriteOnly"` behind `var.trail_documents_data_

### `major` PITR window grows from 7 days to 35, and the ROPA's backup-erasure sentence becomes false

**Evidence.** docs/compliance/gdpr-dpdp.md retention schedule: "RDS backups | 7-day PITR window; erased data ages out of backups within the window | Recovery". docs/compliance/soc2-controls.md Backup & recovery: "RDS automated backups, 7-day PITR". DynamoDB PITR defaults to a 35-day continuous window (configurable 1-35 days since late 2024). On-demand backups and AWS Backup copies have no automatic expiry at all — they persist until explicitly deleted or until a vault lifecycle rule removes them.

**Impact.** After an erasure completes, the erased person's data remains restorable for 35 days instead of 7 — a 5x longer residual-copy window, and a written statement in the privacy documentation that is no longer true. If anyone ever takes an on-demand backup (the obvious thing to do before a migration cutover), that copy is permanent until manually deleted and is invisible to `erase_account.py`, whose `verify()` (erase_account.py:292-306) checks live tables, live S3 prefixes and Cognito only.

**Recommendation.** Set the PITR recovery window explicitly to 7 days to preserve the existing written commitment, or change the commitment and say 35 — but do not leave the default silently contradicting the doc. Forbid on-demand backups of personal-data tables outside a named migration window, and add an explicit step to docs/runbooks/account-data.md: enumerate on-demand backups/AWS Backup recovery points taken since the erasure request and either delete them or record them as a known residual copy on the erasure receipt.

**Effort.** 2 days for the config + doc change; 1 week to add backup enumeration to the erasure runbook

> **Verifier correction.** PITR window grows from 7 days to 35 unless explicitly set, making the compliance docs' stated number stale.

Evidence (all verified): docs/compliance/gdpr-dpdp.md:50 "| RDS backups | 7-day PITR window; erased data ages out of backups within the window | Recovery |"; docs/compliance/soc2-controls.md:53 "| Database | RDS automated backups, 7-day PITR | [template] |"; the number is enforced today by infra/terraform/modules/runtime/rds.tf:42 `backup_retention_period = 7`. DynamoDB PITR defaults to a 35-day continuous window (RecoveryPeriodInDays configurable 1-35 since late 2024), so a default-configured table silently quintuples the residual window and leaves both tables naming a datastore that no longer exists.

Corrected scope: the "permanent, erasure-invisible backup copy" half of the impact is a pre-existing gap, not new. rds.tf:48-51 sets `skip_final_snapshot = false` with a `pattadar-prod-pg-final-*` identifier, and docs/runbooks/rds-restore-drill.md:10-17 restores from those snapshots — manual RDS snapshots already persist until deleted and are already invisible to `erase_account.py`, whose `verify()` (erase_account.py:291-305) checks only live API/hub/assistant rows, the two l

### `major` There is no cross-table point-in-time consistent restore; restoring audit_events_v2 and audit_chain_head separately guarantees a permanent CHAIN BROKEN verdict

**Evidence.** services/api/src/audit.py:861-891 appends by reading `audit_chain_head` FOR UPDATE, computing `chain_hash(prev_hash, integrity_hash, seq)`, inserting the event and then `UPDATE audit_chain_head SET seq=%s, head_hash=%s` — in one transaction. audit.py:1121-1133 and `verify_audit_chain.py:52-53` verify the events against the head. DynamoDB `RestoreTableToPointInTime` is per-table, restores to a **new** table name, and AWS makes no cross-table consistency guarantee — two restore operations are two independent requests. The restored table also does not carry over: PITR settings, TTL settings, stream settings, auto-scaling, IAM policies, CloudWatch alarms, or tags.

**Impact.** If head and events are separate tables, a restore that lands them a few hundred milliseconds apart yields a `head_hash` that does not match the last event, and `verify_audit_chain.py` exits 1 forever after — the platform's own tamper-evidence tool reports tampering caused by its own recovery procedure. Same class of problem across `storage_nodes` / `storage_versions` (today one DB with an FK; rds-restore-drill.md:44-46 counts both): a restore can produce a node pointing at a `current_version_id` whose version row does not exist, and the S3 object-key round-trip check at rds-restore-drill.md:49-64 then fails for reasons that are not a real inconsistency. And every restored table comes back with PITR off and TTL off, so a quarterly drill leaves an unmanaged full copy of everyone's personal data sitting in the account until someone deletes it.

**Recommendation.** Put the audit events and the chain head in the SAME table (different SK), so one restore is internally consistent and the transactional append stays a single-table TransactWriteItems. Same for `storage_nodes`/`storage_versions`. Then rewrite rds-restore-drill.md as a DynamoDB drill whose acceptance criterion is `verify_audit_chain.py --json` returning `ok: true` against the restored table, not row counts. Add an explicit teardown step with a named owner, and note that the restore is billed per GB restored (roughly $0.15/GB in standard regions) rather than the ~$0.02 the current drill costs.

**Effort.** 1 week for the key design; 1 week to rewrite the drill runbook

> **Verifier correction.** There is no cross-table point-in-time consistent restore in DynamoDB, and splitting audit_events_v2 / audit_chain_head / audit_outbox across tables makes the chain corruptible by its own recovery procedure.

Corrected mechanism: verify_audit_chain.py does NOT compare events to the head — audit.py:974-1060 (`verify_chain`) reads only `audit_events_v2` (:989) and its exit code at verify_audit_chain.py:70 comes from that alone. The real failures are:
(a) head restored BEHIND events: the next append computes seq = head.seq + 1 on an already-used seq; the `ON CONFLICT (event_id) DO NOTHING` guard at audit.py:872 does not catch it because the event_id is new, so the chain forks and verify_chain then permanently reports a sequence gap / prev_hash mismatch (audit.py:1035-1043);
(b) head restored AHEAD of events: `verify_recent` (audit.py:1146-1153) emits ok=False, "the chain head names event N but no event at or after M remains" — a false tamper verdict from the health endpoint.

Corrected storage claim: storage_nodes.current_version_id has NO foreign key — init.sql:30 says so explicitly ("no FK: insert-order"); the FK runs the other way (storage_versions.node_id -> storage_nodes(id), init

### `major` DynamoDB Streams republish the full OldImage of every erased item and retain it for 24 hours — an erasure emits the data it is erasing

**Evidence.** erase_account.py:105-158 `purge_api()` deletes owned rows; :161-170 `purge_assistant()`; :173-179 `purge_storage_metadata()`. In DynamoDB each of those deletes produces a stream record. DynamoDB Streams retain records for 24 hours. Kinesis Data Streams for DynamoDB (the alternative, and the one you would pick for any fan-out) retains 24 hours by default and can be configured up to 365 days. `apps/university/ARCHITECTURE.md:224` already proposes "DynamoDB Streams for DynamoDB writes" with versioned events.

**Impact.** The stream is a second store of personal data that `verify()` (erase_account.py:292-306) does not check and cannot check. Any consumer — a Lambda writing a read model, an OpenSearch index, a Firehose to S3 — holds a derived copy that the erasure runner has no stage for. The gdpr-dpdp.md erasure row describes the cascade as "DB rows -> storage nodes -> S3 objects including all versions and delete markers -> Cognito user deletion"; streams and stream-derived read models are simply absent from that list.

**Recommendation.** If streams are used at all: set `StreamViewType` to `KEYS_ONLY` or `NEW_IMAGE` on any table holding personal data so a DELETE does not republish the record (KEYS_ONLY is the safe default), keep Kinesis retention at 24 hours, and add an explicit `derived_read_models` stage to `STAGES` (account.py:22) that erases the owner from every projection before `verify`. Enumerate every consumer in the ROPA. Never use `OLD_IMAGE` or `NEW_AND_OLD_IMAGES` on a personal-data table.

**Effort.** 1 week, plus one stage per read model built thereafter

> **Verifier correction.** Same finding, with three corrections: (1) STAGES is at services/api/src/account.py:21, not :22. (2) KEYS_ONLY is the safest option but not disclosure-free -- the partition key will be the owner id, so the stream still holds a pseudonymous personal identifier for 24 hours and the erasure runner must still account for it. (3) The finding is stronger than stated: purge_api does not delete audit_events_v2, it redacts it in place (erase_account.py:126-140, "The centralized v2 trail is REDACTED IN PLACE, never deleted"). Under NEW_AND_OLD_IMAGES or OLD_IMAGE, that redaction UPDATE itself republishes the full pre-redaction personal-data image to the stream -- the erasure mechanism would emit exactly what it is erasing.

### `major` foreign_children() reads pg_constraint's 42 foreign keys to refuse erasing into another owner's rows — no DynamoDB equivalent exists

**Evidence.** services/api/scripts/erase_account.py:77-93 `foreign_children()` queries `pg_constraint` for every single-column FK, and for each parent in scope checks whether any child row points into it that is NOT owned by the erasing owner, raising `"Foreign-owned dependent records require operator review"`. It runs twice — at :108 inside `purge_api` and at :228 in the pre-flight. A parallel check exists for shared storage folders at :175-176 and :229-230.

**Impact.** This is the control that stops one person's erasure from silently destroying a co-owner's record — which for a land platform with `parcel_owners`, `property_owners`, `beneficiaries`, `family_members` and `shared_kits` is not hypothetical. In DynamoDB there is no constraint catalog, so the check becomes a hand-written list of "which item types can point at which", maintained by the same person who forgets to update the erasure registry. When it drifts, the failure is a deletion that succeeds and should not have.

**Recommendation.** Encode the relationship in the key, not in a catalog: a child item that can be co-owned carries an explicit `coOwners` attribute, and the erasure sweeper refuses to delete any item whose `coOwners` contains a principal other than the erasing owner (it detaches instead). Make that a conditional delete so the refusal is enforced by DynamoDB, not by a pre-flight query that can race. Add a test per co-ownable type.

**Effort.** 2 weeks

> **Verifier correction.** `foreign_children()` (services/api/scripts/erase_account.py:77-93, called at :108 and :228) is real and correctly described mechanically, but it covers 2 constraints, not 42. `catalog()` (:30-33) scopes to `table_schema='public'`, and of the 42 FKs in the database 40 live in the `app` schema and 2 in `ref`; regclass renders those as `app.x`/`ref.x`, which never match the bare-name `scopes` keys, so :81 skips them. The only public-schema FKs are `payment_operations→payment_intents` and `payment_webhook_events→payment_intents`. The co-ownership tables the finding cites as the motivation — `parcel_owners`, `property_owners`, `beneficiaries`, `family_members`, `shared_kits`, plus `parcels`/`properties`/`documents` — carry NO foreign-key constraints at all, so the pg_constraint check protects none of them. Their parent/child topology is already a hand-maintained Python dict, `CHILD_LINKS` at services/api/src/account.py:60-74 (plus the hardcoded `children["invitations"]` set at erase_account.py:147). A DynamoDB migration therefore does not introduce the "hand-written registry that drifts" failure mode — that registry already exists and is already the sole protection for co-owned land rec

### `moderate` A separate Cognito user pool for university.pattadar.com would leave a live identity behind after a completed erasure

**Evidence.** infra/terraform/modules/persistent/cognito.tf:63 declares **one** `aws_cognito_user_pool.pattadar` with three clients (:147 spa, :200 local_dev, :259 mobile); :462-470 binds the single custom domain `auth.<domain>` to that one pool. erase_account.py:250-253 derives `pool_id` from `job["issuer"]` and hard-refuses any other issuer; :287-291 `identities()` deletes users in that pool only; :304-306 `verify()` checks that pool only. The proposal has `university.pattadar.com -> university login` as a distinct login.

**Impact.** If "university login" means a second user pool, a completed erasure receipt (status `completed`, erase_account.py:315) is issued while the person's university identity, MFA registration and login history remain live in the other pool. The runbook's own instruction — "The runner deletes all explicitly linked Cognito subjects for that owner. An email match is never sufficient" (docs/runbooks/account-data.md:61-63) — cannot reach a pool the issuer does not name.

**Recommendation.** Make university.pattadar.com a fourth **app client** on the existing pool, not a second pool. One issuer, one erasure target, one MFA posture, one `auth.pattadar.com` custom domain — which is also what the founder's `auth.pattadar.com -> authentication routing` design implies. Different landing pages and scopes are an app-client concern, not a pool concern. If two pools are ever genuinely required, `erase_account.py` needs a second issuer in the job record and the manifest, and `verify()` must check both.

**Effort.** 2 days if decided now; 1 week of erasure-runner surgery if two pools ship first

> **Verifier correction.** Same claim, with two citation fixes and one strengthening: the custom domain block is infra/terraform/modules/persistent/cognito.tf:464-470 (not :462-470), bound to the single pool via user_pool_id at :468 with domain = var.custom_auth_domain (:467), set to "auth.pattadar.com" in infra/terraform/envs/prod/persistent/main.tf:39. Also note erase_account.py:275-281 (freeze) disables users in that one pool only, so a second pool's identity remains fully usable during the erasure window, and :315 blanks issuer='' and subject='' on completion, so a missed second issuer cannot be reconciled from the job record afterwards.

### `moderate` GSI erasure works, but GSI reads are always eventually consistent, so verify() can pass while a GSI still serves the erased item

**Evidence.** Correcting a premise in the review brief: deleting a base item **does** remove its GSI projections automatically — GSIs are not a separate erasure target. The real issue is that DynamoDB GSI reads cannot be strongly consistent (there is no `ConsistentRead` on a GSI Query), and propagation from base table to GSI is asynchronous. erase_account.py:292-298 `verify()` re-runs `snapshot()` counts and requires zero, then the job is marked `completed` at :315.

**Impact.** `verify()` would read the base partitions, find zero, and complete the erasure receipt while a GSI Query — which is how the mentor-queue and review-queue access patterns in apps/university/ARCHITECTURE.md:148-152 are designed to work — can still return the erased person's item for a short window. The window is normally sub-second, but under throttling or a hot index it can be longer, and there is no API that tells you a GSI has caught up. The receipt says deleted; the read model briefly disagrees.

**Recommendation.** Add a GSI re-read to `verify()`: after the base sweep, Query every GSI whose partition key can contain the owner and require zero, with a bounded retry (e.g. 5 attempts over 30 seconds) before completion. This is cheap and turns an invisible window into a measured one. Note in the receipt schema that verification is base-table-strong and index-eventual.

**Effort.** 3 days

> **Verifier correction.** Evidence stands with three fixes. (1) Line anchors: the mentor-queue GSI is apps/university/ARCHITECTURE.md:145-150 (`GSI1PK = REVIEW#{locationSlug}#{skillCode}` at :148, `GSI1SK = STATUS#{status}#{submittedAt}#{submissionId}` at :149), not 148-152, and it is a single GSI — the "review queue" and "mentor queue" are the same index. (2) Scope: services/api/scripts/erase_account.py is today a PostgreSQL script (its verify() runs `SELECT count(*)`/`SELECT 1` against psycopg connections at :293-298), so this is a requirement for the DynamoDB port of that verifier, not a defect present in the code today. (3) Remedy: the suggested "Query every GSI whose partition key can contain the owner" is not generally possible — on the mentor-queue GSI the owner (`cognitoSub`) is a projected attribute, not GSI1PK, so there is no owner-keyed Query. The workable version is to record each deleted item's GSI key tuple during the base sweep and, after the sweep, re-Query those specific index partitions (with a bounded retry, e.g. 5 attempts over 30s) asserting the item is gone; plus state in the receipt schema that verification is base-table-strong and index-eventual.

### `moderate` aadhaar_candidates' 30-minute lifetime cannot be delivered by TTL, and the ROPA states 30 minutes as fact

**Evidence.** services/api/src/aadhaar.py:28 `CANDIDATE_TTL_MINUTES = 30`; :274-279 inserts with `expires_at`; :321 `cleanup_expired()` does `DELETE FROM aadhaar_candidates WHERE expires_at<now() OR consumed_at<now()-interval '1 day'`. docs/compliance/gdpr-dpdp.md retention schedule: "Aadhaar extraction candidates | 30 minutes for use; consumed rows are cleanup-eligible after 1 day". DynamoDB TTL deletes best-effort, typically within 48 hours of expiry — roughly 96x the stated 30-minute lifetime for the platform's most sensitive data class.

**Impact.** Access is not affected: the security property is enforced by the read predicate at aadhaar.py:284-287 (`consumed_at IS NULL AND expires_at>now()`), which maps cleanly to a ConditionExpression. So this is not an exposure. It is a truthfulness problem — the privacy documentation would assert a 30-minute at-rest lifetime for KMS-encrypted full Aadhaar digits while the actual deletion lands up to two days later, with no evidence of when.

**Recommendation.** Keep an explicit sweeper (a 5-minute EventBridge-scheduled delete, a direct port of `cleanup_expired`) and use TTL only as a belt-and-braces backstop. Do not remove the sweeper on the grounds that TTL exists. The ROPA line stays true as written.

**Effort.** 2 days

> **Verifier correction.** Fixed line numbers and added the missing driver: `services/api/src/aadhaar.py:28` (`CANDIDATE_TTL_MINUTES = 30`), `:274-279` (insert with `expires_at`), `:286-289` (the read predicate `consumed_at IS NULL AND expires_at>now() FOR UPDATE` — not 284-287), `:321-322` (`cleanup_expired` DELETE, quoted correctly). Crucially, `cleanup_expired` is not a dormant helper: its sole caller is `services/api/src/ai_reading/jobs.py:212` inside `sweep()`, which `maintenance()` drives in a `while True` loop with `await asyncio.sleep(60)` (jobs.py:214-223). So expired candidate rows are physically deleted within ~60 seconds today; DynamoDB TTL's best-effort "typically within 48 hours" window would be roughly a 2880x regression against actual current behaviour, not just a 96x regression against the documented 30-minute figure. Also soften the ROPA characterization: gdpr-dpdp.md:45 reads "30 minutes for use; consumed rows are cleanup-eligible after 1 day" — eligibility/usability wording rather than an explicit at-rest deletion guarantee, so the truthfulness problem is that the line would become misleading, not that it states a deletion SLA it then breaks. Everything else stands, including the recommen

### `moderate` Data residency under the proposal is fine; the us-east-1 elements are control-plane, not customer data — but WAF logging would change that

**Evidence.** infra/terraform/modules/persistent/versions.tf:8 "Cognito custom-domain cert must live in us-east-1"; cognito.tf:427 and :458 pin `aws_acm_certificate.auth` and its validation to `aws.us_east_1`. infra/terraform/modules/runtime/versions.tf:1-2 "The us_east_1 alias exists solely for CloudFront-scoped resources (ACM cert, WAFv2 CLOUDFRONT web ACL) — both are us-east-1-only APIs." cloudfront.tf:139-175 declares the web ACL; grepping cloudfront.tf and observability.tf finds **no** `aws_wafv2_web_acl_logging_configuration` — WAF logging is not enabled today.

**Impact.** Accurate reading: an ACM certificate contains a public key and a domain name. It is not customer data and it is not a DPDP transfer. The CloudFront and WAFv2 control planes are likewise metadata. Amplify Hosting is available in ap-south-1 and, for static Vite SPAs, stores nothing but build artifacts and logs regionally. None of this is a residency problem. Two things genuinely worth noting: (1) TLS for `auth.pattadar.com` terminates at globally distributed CloudFront edges that AWS operates in front of the pool — credentials transit edge PoPs outside India, which is a disclosure item for the privacy notice, not a prohibition (DPDP s.16 restricts only blacklisted destinations, and none apply). (2) If WAF logging is ever enabled on a CLOUDFRONT-scope ACL, the logging destination is us-east-1-scoped, and WAF logs carry client IP addresses and full request URIs — that **is** personal data landing outside ap-south-1.

**Recommendation.** Say plainly in gdpr-dpdp.md's cross-border table that the us-east-1 ACM certs and the CloudFront/WAF control plane are not transfers, with this reasoning — it pre-empts an auditor's question and it is correct. Add a one-line note that Cognito custom-domain TLS terminates at global edges. If WAF logging is turned on, sample/redact the client IP field or accept and document the transfer; do not enable it silently.

**Effort.** 1 day of documentation

> **Verifier correction.** Same claim with two corrections: (1) the web ACL resource block begins at infra/terraform/modules/runtime/cloudfront.tf:138, not :139 (line 139 is its `count` expression). (2) The ACL is not unconditionally present — `count = var.enable_cdn && var.enable_waf ? 1 : 0` (cloudfront.tf:139) and it runs managed rule groups in count-only override mode unless `var.waf_block_mode` is true (cloudfront.tf:156-166). Since the whole runtime module is destroyed in prod, no WAF is deployed at all today, so the "WAF logs carry client IPs to us-east-1" concern is prospective rather than latent — which strengthens, not weakens, the recommendation to decide the logging/redaction posture before WAF is ever turned on.

### `minor` The audit chain head becomes a single hot DynamoDB item with a 1,000 WCU/s ceiling, but that is not a launch problem

**Evidence.** services/api/src/audit.py:487-501: one row, ever, locked `FOR UPDATE` per append (:861-866). The comment is candid: "Audit volume is low enough that one short row lock per event costs nothing." In DynamoDB, a single item lives on one partition and a partition sustains 1,000 WCU/s; a contended optimistic-concurrency counter (ConditionExpression on `headHash`) realistically sustains far less once retries start. Live `audit_events_v2` count in the local DB: 53.

**Impact.** Not a blocker at current volume, and probably not at launch volume either. It becomes one at the point where audited writes exceed a few hundred per second, at which time the gapless global `seq` is the thing that has to go — and `verify_chain` (audit.py:975-1050) is built entirely around gapless `seq`.

**Recommendation.** Keep the single chain as-is; it is correct and the volume does not justify sharding. Write down the ceiling now so it is a known limit rather than a surprise: if audited write rate ever approaches ~100/s sustained, move to per-day or per-shard chains with a daily anchor hash, which keeps tamper-evidence and removes the global serialization point. Do not pre-shard.

**Effort.** 0 now; 2-3 weeks if it ever binds

> **Verifier correction.** All citations check out; two refinements. (1) The live count is 54, not 53 (`select count(*) from audit_events_v2` → 54; `audit_chain_head` → seq=54) — the reviewer's number was one stale append behind, immaterial. (2) The chain append is NOT on the request path: writes land in `audit_outbox` and a background worker drains them one row per transaction (services/api/src/audit.py:837 "Worker: drain the outbox into the read model", :853-855 `SELECT * FROM audit_outbox WHERE state='pending' ORDER BY enqueued_at FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED LIMIT 1`, then :862-864 `SELECT seq, head_hash FROM audit_chain_head WHERE id=1 FOR UPDATE`, :888-890 the head UPDATE). So the ~1,000 WCU/s single-partition ceiling would bound the *drainer's* sustained append rate and show up as a growing outbox backlog (the stall signal the code already watches for at :1255-1276), not as user-facing write latency. That makes the severity call ("minor, not a launch blocker") more defensible, not less, while the structural point — a global gapless `seq` is one serialization point and `verify_chain` is built on it — stands.

### `minor` Records of Processing is stale against the code: photos are file references not data-URLs, and the 290 GB land corpus has no ROPA entry at all

**Evidence.** docs/compliance/gdpr-dpdp.md ROPA, Identity row: "photos (data-URLs in DB)". The live schema shows `parcel_photos` has `file_ref`, `sha256`, `media_kind`, `width`, `height` — a storage reference, no data-URL column (same for `property_photos`; `users` has `avatar_url`/`photo_url`). Separately, `land.party` holds ~140.5M rows with a `name` / `name_norm` column — personal names of parties to real-estate transactions — queried by services/assistant/src/public_records/database.py:106-114. The ROPA has no row for the land corpus; the only mention anywhere is the cross-border table's passing reference to "public-record lookups performed on the user's behalf" (gdpr-dpdp.md:61).

**Impact.** An auditor comparing the ROPA to the schema finds two discrepancies in the first ten minutes, which damages the credibility of the parts that are correct. On the substance: the land corpus is most likely out of DPDP scope under s.3(c)(ii) (personal data made publicly available under law), and names in it are not erasable on request — but that determination needs to be written down, not absent. A migration is exactly when someone will ask "does erasure have to touch the 140M-row party table?" and the documentation currently gives no answer.

**Recommendation.** Two small edits, worth making before any migration: (1) correct the Identity row to say photos are stored as owner-prefixed S3 references with metadata in the DB; (2) add a ROPA row for the public land-record corpus stating the source, the lawful basis (s.3(c)(ii) publicly-available carve-out), that it is read-only and never mutated by user action, and that it is explicitly out of erasure scope with the reasoning. Also note that the corpus is 290 GB of relational, join-heavy reference data and is not a DynamoDB candidate under any reading.

**Effort.** half a day

> **Verifier correction.** Drop the photos claim entirely — it is a misread — and keep only the land-corpus ROPA gap, with the citation fixed:

CORRECTED FINDING: "The ROPA has no entry for the 290 GB public land-record corpus, which holds ~140.5M personal names."

IS NOT NULL` table-existence probe and contains no name column at all.

### `positive` POSITIVE: DynamoDB's 400 KB item limit forces the document_read_jobs.source fix the Aadhaar runbook already wants

**Evidence.** `document_read_jobs.source` is `bytea` (verified in the live schema) and holds raw scanned document bytes while a read job is queued. docs/runbooks/aadhaar-kms-rollout.md, 'Remaining known exposure': "Durable Aadhaar reading jobs temporarily hold source bytes in the encrypted RDS `document_read_jobs.source` column until completion/failure cleanup ... moving queued source bytes to an owner-scoped S3 reference is a separate architecture change." A DynamoDB item caps at 400 KB; a scanned deed or Aadhaar card is typically 1-10 MB.

**Impact.** The migration makes the deferred fix non-optional. Source bytes move to an owner-prefixed S3 key, which is already the pattern `storage.py:181-183` uses (`{owner}/{node_id}/{version_id}`) and which `delete_prefix` (erase_account.py:48-74) already erases exhaustively including versions and delete markers. That is a genuine net reduction in the most sensitive exposure the runbook names.

**Recommendation.** Do this change first, independently of the DynamoDB decision. It is valuable on its own, it de-risks the migration, and it can ship against the current PostgreSQL stack today. Use the same `{owner}/...` prefix so erasure coverage is inherited with no new code.

**Effort.** 1 week

> **Verifier correction.** Claim stands; two details to tighten.

(a) Size framing: the finding says a scan is "typically 1-10 MB". The enforced ingest ceiling in code is 25 MB (`MAX_BYTES = 25 * 1024 * 1024`, services/api/src/ai_reading/jobs.py:24, checked at :65-66 and again at operations.py:201). So the worst case is ~64x the 400 KB item limit, and even inline-base64 in an item is hopeless at any realistic scan size. This strengthens the finding.

(b) "no new code" is slightly optimistic. `_key`/`delete_prefix` coverage lives on the gateway side (services/gateway/src/storage.py:181) and in the API's erasure script (services/api/scripts/erase_account.py:301,308), while `document_read_jobs` is written by services/api (jobs.py:94,108). Inheriting erasure for free requires the reading job to write under the *same storage bucket* with the `{owner}/...` prefix — which means either services/api gets direct S3 access to the gateway's bucket or it calls gateway storage. That is a real, small integration decision, not zero code. Note also `delete_prefix` hard-refuses any prefix not ending in `/` and the literal `"assistant/"` (erase_account.py:50), so the new key must be `{owner}/<something>` — not a sibling prefix

### `positive` POSITIVE: S3 owner-prefixed keys and prefix-based deletion survive presigned uploads unchanged, including orphans

**Evidence.** services/gateway/src/storage.py:181-183 `_key(owner, node_id, version_id) -> f"{owner}/{node_id}/{version_id}"`. erase_account.py:48-74 `delete_prefix()` pages `list_object_versions` AND `list_objects_v2` under `owner + "/"`, deletes every version and delete marker, loops up to 4 times, and raises if objects keep appearing; :57-58 refuses if any listed key escapes the prefix. It is driven by the S3 prefix, not by the `storage_versions` table. :231-233 separately refuses erasure if any DB `object_key` falls outside the owner prefix.

**Impact.** A presigned upload that the client abandons after the PUT but before the metadata call leaves an S3 object with no database row. Under this design that orphan is still erased, because erasure enumerates the bucket prefix rather than the metadata. Many systems get this wrong and leak exactly these orphans through erasure. This one does not.

**Recommendation.** Preserve the invariant explicitly when you presign: the server chooses the key (`{owner}/{node_id}/{version_id}`) and the presigned policy pins it exactly — never let the client supply or influence the key. Add a lifecycle rule that expires objects with no metadata row after N days to stop orphans accumulating, but keep prefix-based erasure as the authority.

**Effort.** included in the presigned-upload work above

(a) `_key` is at services/gateway/src/storage.py:181-182 (decorator at :180), not 181-183.

(b) Worth stating explicitly that prefix-based erasure is correct only while the gateway's S3 owner prefix is byte-identical to the API's `account_erasure_jobs.owner_user_id`. The guard at erase_account.py:231-233 only inspects rows in `storage_versions`, so it catches a key-scheme drift that is recorded in the DB (it hard-fails rather than silently leaking — good), but it cannot see an orphan written under a *different* prefix, which would then be neither listed by the DB check nor swept by `delete_prefix(s3, storage_bucket, owner+"/")`. That matters here because the platform already has a known identity split between the API's `shankarreddy.t`-style user id and the gateway's `subject_<sha256>` principal. So the recommendation should be: pin the presigned key to the server-chosen `{owner}/{node_id}/{version_id}` *and* pin `owner` to the same identity the erasure job keys on, otherwise the "prefix is the authority" property quietly stops holding.

### `positive` POSITIVE: IAM on a DynamoDB audit table is a stronger privilege boundary than the Postgres trigger, by the code's own admission

**Evidence.** services/api/src/audit.py:524-527 states the limit itself: "HONEST LIMIT: a trigger is not a privilege boundary. Whoever holds DDL rights can drop it. The deployment-level control is the grant set in `ROLE_BOOTSTRAP_SQL`." The MEMORY note records that audit role bootstrap still needs running as DB owner, i.e. the grant half is not yet applied in practice. audit.py:645-652 grants `SELECT, INSERT` to the writer role and revokes `UPDATE, DELETE, TRUNCATE` from the app login. In DynamoDB the equivalent — the runtime role holding `dynamodb:PutItem` and `dynamodb:Query` on the audit table and being explicitly denied `UpdateItem`/`DeleteItem`, with redaction under a separate maintainer role — is enforced by IAM outside the database, cannot be dropped by whoever holds application credentials, and every attempt is a CloudTrail event.

**Impact.** This is the one place where the proposed architecture makes a SOC 2 control genuinely stronger and easier to evidence than what exists today, and it removes a dependency (running bootstrap SQL as DB owner) that is currently outstanding.

**Recommendation.** Design the audit table's IAM split first, before any other table. Runtime role: PutItem + Query, explicit Deny on UpdateItem/DeleteItem/DeleteTable. Maintainer role (EventBridge-scheduled redaction Lambda only): UpdateItem with a `dynamodb:Attributes` condition that excludes `seq`, `prevHash`, `rowHash`, `integrityHash`. Auditor role: Query only, and keep the `auditor.read` audit event (audit.py:226). Then delete the trigger's honesty caveat from the docs, because it will no longer apply.

**Effort.** 1 week

> **Verifier correction.** Keep the finding, with the citation fixed and the mechanism corrected.

Citations: the HONEST LIMIT comment is at services/api/src/audit.py:523-526 (not 524-527), and the same caveat is repeated at :1175 and :1207. Grants are at :647 (writer: SELECT, INSERT) and :652 (REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE, TRUNCATE from the app login); maintainer grant at :649. auditor.read at :226 is exact. The "not yet applied" half is verifiable in-repo, not just from memory: role_bootstrap_sql() (:623) is referenced only by its own docstring (:612) and services/api/tests/test_audit_boundary.py:31,46 — no boot path calls it — and the trigger sets `reviewed := true` when pattadar_audit_maintainer is absent, so the gate currently fails open everywhere.

Mechanism: IAM alone does NOT make a DynamoDB audit table append-only. PutItem overwrites an item with the same key, and IAM has no condition key that can force `attribute_not_exists(pk)`, so a role holding dynamodb:PutItem can destroy prior events even with UpdateItem/DeleteItem explicitly denied. The design that actually beats the Postgres trigger is: (a) all appends go through one writer principal that issues PutItem with `ConditionExpression: attribute_not_exi

---

## How the migration would actually run

I measured the port rather than estimating it. The three Python services contain 1,156 SQL statements across 1,554 psycopg execute/fetch call sites, all of them inline in GraphQL resolvers and helper functions — there is no ORM, no repository layer, and no DAO anywhere in services/api or services/gateway. The only data-access seam in the whole repo is services/assistant/src/ports.py (54 lines of Protocols), and it deliberately does not cover the database. That means the port is 1,554 hand-edits, not a driver swap. Three things in the code are not portable at all rather than merely expensive: the 287 GB land corpus is queried with pg_trgm similarity, HNSW vector search (`embedding <=> ...`) and SUM/GROUP BY aggregates, none of which DynamoDB has any equivalent for; the audit hash-chain serialises on `SELECT ... FROM audit_chain_head WHERE id=1 FOR UPDATE` (audit.py:863) and would become a single hot partition capped at ~1,000 WCU/s with contention retries; and 39 unbounded set-deletes like delete_property (main.py:3253-3277, five tables in one `conn.transaction()`) exceed TransactWriteItems' 100-item ceiling by construction. AWS DMS cannot do this migration — its DynamoDB target cannot join, so every denormalised item shape is custom ETL. My calibrated estimate for the OLTP half alone, solo, is 40-58 engineer-weeks (10-14 calendar months), excluding the land corpus and excluding all new feature work, against a repo where prod /api/* currently returns 502 and 152 w360 UX defects are open. Two parts of the proposal are genuinely sound and should ship regardless: the S3 presigned-upload path (uploads today buffer 100 MB through the gateway process — routes/storage.py:423), and the repository seam itself, which is the highest-value refactor whether or not DynamoDB is ever adopted.

### `blocker` No data-access seam exists: 1,554 psycopg call sites are inline in resolvers, so the port is 1,554 hand-edits

**Evidence.** Measured per service (grep over services/*/src/*.py):

| service | src LOC | SELECT | INSERT | UPDATE | DELETE | total stmts | execute/fetch call sites | REST endpoints | GraphQL resolvers | test files / test fns |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| api | 27,296 | 523 | 144 | 257 | 89 | 1,013 | 1,425 | 24 | 270 (110 @strawberry.field + 160 @strawberry.mutation) | 27 / 378 |
| gateway | 3,942 | 46 | 7 | 13 | 5 | 71 | 56 | 31 | 0 | 19 / 104 |
| assistant | 4,607 | 53 | 4 | 12 | 3 | 72 | 73 | 14 | 0 | 5 / 27 |
| **total** | **35,845** | **622** | **155** | **282** | **97** | **1,156** | **1,554** | **69** | **270** | **51 / 509** |

Top files: services/api/src/main.py 509 stmts / 797 call sites; services/api/src/web360.py 333 stmts / 476 call sites; audit.py 54; payments.py 48; gateway/src/storage.py 40.

Shape of a typical call site — services/api/src/main.py:3222-3231 (updateProperty):
```python
async with pool.connection() as conn:
    own = await (await conn.execute(
        "SELECT 1 FROM properties WHERE id=%s AND owner_user_id=%s", (id, uid))).fetchone()
    if not own: raise NotAuthorized(...)
    cols = ", ".join(f"{k}=%s" for k in sets)
    await conn.execute(f"UPDATE properties SET {cols} WHERE id=%s", (*sets.values(), id))
    row = await (await conn.execute("SELECT * FROM properties WHERE id=%s", (id,))).fetchone()
```
The connection pool is a module global (`pool = AsyncConnectionPool(...)`, main.py:110) injected into siblings by `_pool = pool` (web360.py:54, audit.py:1317, account.py:43, aadhaar.py:47, payments.py:28). Every sibling module reaches the driver directly.

I searched for any seam. The only one in the repo is services/assistant/src/ports.py (54 lines) — and its own docstring scopes it away from the database: it defines AgentRuntime, ModelCatalog, PromptRepository, and explicitly says "These describe existing behaviour; they are not a new abstraction layer." services/assistant/src/public_records/database.py:34 is `ReadOnlyDatabase` — a thin psycopg wrapper that takes raw SQL strings from service.py, not a repository. Nothing in services/api or services/gateway has an equivalent. Zero occurrences of `dynamodb` or `boto3.*Table(` in any service source.

**Impact.** A datastore swap with a repository layer is ~85 method bodies. Without one it is 1,554 individually-reasoned edits, each of which must re-derive the access pattern (which key? which GSI? is this a Query or a Scan?) from surrounding code. At an optimistic 12 call sites per productive day — 25 minutes each including reading context, choosing keys and adjusting a test — that is 130 working days of pure porting for one person, before any new data modelling, tooling, dual-write, or test work. There is also no compiler or type checker that will find a missed site: `conn.execute("...")` takes a string, so a forgotten resolver silently keeps talking to Postgres after cutover.

**Recommendation.** Build the seam FIRST and independently of DynamoDB. Copy the pattern that already exists in-repo: a `ports.py` of Protocols plus `adapters/` implementations (services/assistant/src/ports.py + services/assistant/src/adapters/). Concretely: define one Protocol per aggregate (PropertyRepository, DocumentRepository, FamilyRepository, TicketRepository, PaymentRepository, AuditRepository, AssociateRepository, GeographyRepository — roughly 12-15 for the 85 tables), each with explicit typed methods (`get_property(id, owner) -> dict | None`, `update_property(id, fields) -> dict`), and a `PostgresXRepository` adapter that holds today's exact SQL verbatim. Move code, do not rewrite it. Inject via FastAPI dependency or a module-level registry so tests can substitute a fake. This is worth doing on its own merits: it collapses 1,554 call sites to a countable API surface, makes the 16 DB-backed test files runnable without Postgres, and makes web360.py's 476 call sites reviewable. Only after that does "could aggregate X live in DynamoDB?" become a question you can answer by writing one adapter and A/B-ing it, rather than a 10-month commitment.

**Effort.** 6-8 weeks for the seam alone (mechanical but large); pays for itself regardless of the DynamoDB decision

> **Verifier correction.** Two citation fixes, neither changing the conclusion: (1) the updateProperty snippet is at services/api/src/main.py:3219-3229 (not 3222-3231), and the quote omits line 3224 `if sets:`. (2) services/assistant/src/ports.py is NOT the only ports file in the repo — services/api/src/ai_reading/ports.py (45 lines) also exists, defining VisionExtractor and ConsentGate Protocols. It is a model-provider/consent seam, not a data-access seam, so the substantive claim stands and is in fact stronger: the ports/adapters pattern is already established in services/api itself and has simply never been applied to the datastore. Also worth citing: services/api/src/web360.py:341-342 ("These columns map directly to a future DynamoDB FEATURE item; legacy flat columns remain readable during migration") and services/api/src/feature_schema.py:4 show DynamoDB intent already documented in comments with zero implementing code.

### `blocker` The 287 GB land corpus cannot run on DynamoDB — its three query shapes (trigram similarity, HNSW vector search, SQL aggregates) have no DynamoDB equivalent

**Evidence.** Measured in the live DB:
```
land.party                140,527,280 rows   35 GB
land.real_estate_records   45,144,496 rows   28 GB
land.bv_p0..bv_p7        ~44,360,000 rows   28 GB each = 224 GB
-> ~230M rows, ~287 GB
```
Indexes that carry the product:
```
land.idx_party_name_trgm   gin (name_norm gin_trgm_ops)
land.idx_village_trgm      gin (village_norm gin_trgm_ops)
land.idx_bv_hnsw           hnsw (embedding halfvec_cosine_ops) m=16 ef_construction=64
```
Installed extensions: pg_trgm, vector, vectorscale. (No PostGIS — geometry is stored as text.)

The queries in services/assistant/src/public_records/service.py:
- Vector search, line 490-501: `SELECT rid, s_no, embedding <=> CAST(%(query_vector)s AS halfvec(N)) AS dist FROM land.boundary_vectors ORDER BY embedding <=> ... LIMIT %(fetch_limit)s` then `JOIN real_estate_records USING (rid, s_no)`.
- Fuzzy party search, line 402-408: `SELECT p.rid, ..., similarity(p.name_norm, %(query)s) AS score FROM land.party p JOIN land.real_estate_records r USING (rid, s_no) WHERE ... ORDER BY score DESC` with `local_settings={"pg_trgm.similarity_threshold": threshold}`.
- Aggregates, line 269-283: `SELECT extent_unit, COALESCE(SUM(extent_val),0) AS s, count(*) AS c FROM ... GROUP BY extent_unit ORDER BY s DESC`, plus `_AGGREGATES[metric]` (avg/sum) and lines 749-786: `count(DISTINCT rid)`, `GROUP BY` over txn_class / land_class / extent_unit / village / year.

DynamoDB has: no similarity function, no vector index, no SUM/AVG/COUNT/GROUP BY, no join. Query returns at most 1 MB per page; counting 45M rows means 45,000+ paged Scans.

**Impact.** This is not a slow path or an expensive path — it is a removed feature. The assistant's public-records tools (village resolution, party name search, boundary similarity, price rates, corpus stats) all stop working. There is no amount of key design that recovers `similarity()` or `embedding <=>`. Cost framing is also unfavourable even ignoring capability: DynamoDB standard storage is $0.25/GB-month, so 287 GB is ~$72/month before DynamoDB's per-item attribute-name overhead (every attribute name is stored in every item; with 20-30 columns this realistically inflates to 400-500 GB and ~$100-145/month), versus ~$33/month for the same bytes on gp3 EBS at ~$0.115/GB-month — 3-4x more money for strictly less capability.

**Recommendation.** Carve the land corpus explicitly out of scope and say so in writing. It stays on PostgreSQL + pgvector + pg_trgm, or it moves to S3/Parquet + Athena if the goal is to stop paying for a hot 287 GB instance (Athena does the SUM/GROUP BY fine; vector search then needs a separate small index, e.g. only the boundary vectors for active districts). Either way it is a separate decision with a separate cost model, and it should not be entangled with the OLTP question. Note that `land` is already excluded from the migration path: docs/runbooks/migration.md step 0 dumps with `pg_dump -N land`.

**Effort.** 0 weeks if scoped out; 8-12 weeks if the corpus is moved to S3+Athena as its own project

   party: 16 GB heap + 19 GB index = 35 GB. real_estate_records: 21 GB heap + 7.4 GB index = 28 GB. Each bv_pN: 447 MB heap + 14 GB HNSW index + TOASTed embeddings = 28 GB.
   So the corpus is ~230M rows / ~155 GB of actual payload, not 287 GB of payload. The DynamoDB cost line should read: ~155 GB at $0.25/GB-month ≈ $39/month base (inflating to maybe $60-80 with per-item attribute-name overhead), versus ~$18/month for 155 GB on gp3 at $0.115/GB-month. Still ~2-3x more money for strictly less capability, but the "$72-145/month vs $33" figures are wrong.
