### `blocker` 39 unbounded set-deletes and 59 multi-statement transactions exceed TransactWriteItems' 100-item / 4 MB ceiling by construction

**Evidence.** services/api/src/main.py:3253-3277, deleteProperty — the code's own comment states the guarantee being relied on:
```python
# Six statements on an autocommit pool are six chances to stop
# half-way: one transaction, so the property and everything filed
# against it go together or not at all.
async with conn.transaction():
    await conn.execute("DELETE FROM property_owners WHERE property_id=%s", (id,))
    await conn.execute("DELETE FROM documents WHERE property_id=%s", (id,))
    await conn.execute("DELETE FROM document_parties WHERE document_id IN "
                       "(SELECT id FROM registered_documents WHERE property_id=%s AND owner_user_id=%s)", (id, uid))
    await conn.execute("DELETE FROM registered_documents WHERE property_id=%s AND owner_user_id=%s", (id, uid))
    await conn.execute("DELETE FROM properties WHERE id=%s", (id,))
```
Counts across services/*/src: 59 `conn.transaction()` blocks, 39 `DELETE FROM <table> WHERE <non-id-column>` set-deletes, 27 `IN (SELECT ...)` subqueries, 11 `EXISTS (SELECT ...)`, 45 JOINs.

AWS limits: TransactWriteItems accepts at most 100 action items and 4 MB aggregate, and no item may appear twice in one transaction. BatchWriteItem accepts 25 put/delete requests (16 MB) and is NOT atomic. There is no DELETE-WHERE — you Query then delete each key.

**Impact.** A property with more than ~95 documents/owners/deeds cannot be deleted atomically. The implementation becomes: Query each child index, page at 25 items, issue N non-atomic BatchWriteItems, and hope. A crash mid-way leaves orphaned documents and registered_documents pointing at a property that no longer exists — precisely the bug the code comment says was already fixed once. This is a GDPR/DPDP-relevant path (docs/compliance/gdpr-dpdp.md; account_erasure_jobs exists in schema) where a partial delete is a compliance finding, not just a bug. The same ceiling applies to the account-erasure sweep and to any owner-scoped cascade (`DELETE FROM registered_documents WHERE owner_user_id`, `DELETE FROM record_tags WHERE owner_user_id`).

**Recommendation.** If any aggregate does move, restrict it to ones whose entire cascade fits inside a single DynamoDB item collection (same partition key) and is provably bounded — and write the bound down as a hard invariant enforced on the write path (e.g. max 100 documents per property), because DynamoDB will not enforce it for you. Do not attempt to port deleteProperty, the erasure job, or the audit outbox worker. For unbounded cascades the correct DynamoDB pattern is a soft-delete tombstone plus an async sweeper with a resumable cursor — which is a new subsystem with its own failure modes, not a translation.

**Effort.** 2-3 weeks per unbounded cascade to design, build and test the sweeper; ~6 such paths exist

> **Verifier correction.** Evidence stands; three secondary counts are slightly off and one characterization needs narrowing. Corrected: "40 (not 39) set-deletes on a non-id column, 59 `conn.transaction()` blocks (exact), 27 `IN (SELECT ...)` subqueries (exact), 10 (not 11) `EXISTS (SELECT ...)`, 40 (not 45) JOINs across services/*/src." Also: the owner-scoped deletes at services/api/src/web360.py:7093/7096/7533 are each further narrowed by a record id or entity_type predicate, so they are record-scoped cascades rather than account-wide sweeps — still unbounded in child count, but the phrase "any owner-scoped cascade" should read "any record-scoped cascade whose child count is unbounded by the schema." The core citation (main.py:3253-3277, comment and SQL verbatim) and the TransactWriteItems 100-item/4 MB and BatchWriteItem 25-item/non-atomic limits are all correct as stated.

### `blocker` The audit hash chain and the payments state machine depend on FOR UPDATE row locks (49 sites) — in DynamoDB the chain head becomes a single hot partition capped at ~1,000 WCU/s

**Evidence.** services/api/src/audit.py:496-500 (the design intent, in the code):
```
# One row, ever. Appending locks it FOR UPDATE, which serializes chain
# writes: without that, two workers (or two API replicas) could read the
# same prev_hash and both claim seq N, forking the chain into two branches
# that no verifier could reconcile.
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS audit_chain_head (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT 1, seq BIGINT, head_hash TEXT, CONSTRAINT audit_chain_head_single CHECK (id = 1))
```
services/api/src/audit.py:853-863, the outbox worker takes two locks in a fixed order inside one transaction:
```python
"SELECT * FROM audit_outbox WHERE state='pending' ORDER BY enqueued_at FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED LIMIT 1"
"SELECT seq, head_hash FROM audit_chain_head WHERE id=1 FOR UPDATE"
```
49 `FOR UPDATE` sites total, concentrated in audit.py (3), payments.py (9: payment_intents, payment_operations, work_requests), web360.py (4: work_requests, service_order_intents), aadhaar.py, notify.py. 5 of them use `SKIP LOCKED` for queue draining (notify.py:285, payments.py:371, audit.py:854). 57 `ON CONFLICT` upserts, including `ON CONFLICT (owner_user_id) WHERE status <> 'completed' DO NOTHING` (account.py:268) and `ON CONFLICT(intent_id,kind) DO NOTHING` (payments.py:119).

AWS limits: a single DynamoDB partition sustains ~3,000 RCU/s and ~1,000 WCU/s. Conditional writes (`ConditionExpression`) are the only concurrency primitive — there is no lock, no SKIP LOCKED, and no read-then-write inside TransactWriteItems (a transaction's ConditionCheck cannot return the value you then write).

**Impact.** The chain head is by definition one item. Every audit event in the platform serialises through it via optimistic `ConditionExpression: seq = :expected` with client-side retry. Under contention you get a retry storm with no fairness guarantee, and the gapless-seq invariant that the verifier depends on becomes probabilistic rather than enforced. This is a SOC 2 control surface (docs/compliance/soc2-controls.md) — 'we cannot prove the audit chain has no gaps' is an audit finding. The payments paths are worse in kind: `SELECT * FROM payment_intents WHERE id=%s FOR UPDATE` followed by a state transition (payments.py:418-419) is a read-modify-write on money; reimplementing it as conditional writes is doable but every one of the 9 sites is a place to introduce a double-capture bug.

**Recommendation.** Keep audit and payments on PostgreSQL permanently, regardless of what happens to the rest. These are the two subsystems where Postgres's serialisation is the product, not an implementation detail. If the platform ends up split, this is a clean boundary: a small RDS instance for money + audit, DynamoDB for whatever high-volume read-mostly aggregates justify it. If you insist on porting the chain, the DynamoDB-native shape is a sequencer item per tenant (not one global item) plus an SQS FIFO queue with a single message group for ordering — which changes the chain's meaning from 'global' to 'per tenant' and requires re-approving the compliance story.

**Effort.** not portable; 4-6 weeks to re-derive and re-certify the chain semantics if attempted

> **Verifier correction.** Corrected counts and scope: there are 50 `FOR UPDATE` occurrences in services/, distributed main.py 22, payments.py 9, web360.py 8, audit.py 5 (3 of which are prose in comments; only :854 and :863 are SQL), gateway/src/ai_catalog/routes.py 2, and one each in notify.py, aadhaar.py, ai_reading/jobs.py, assistant/scripts/migrate_attachments.py. The finding's per-file breakdown (audit.py 3, web360.py 4) is wrong and omits main.py entirely, which is the largest concentration at 44% of sites — the porting surface is larger than the finding states, not smaller. SKIP LOCKED queue-draining appears at 4 SQL sites (payments.py:371, notify.py:285, audit.py:854, ai_reading/jobs.py:141), not 5. There are 60 `ON CONFLICT` upserts, not 57. The audit.py citation should read 488-500 rather than 496-500. Finally, soften the compliance claim: docs/compliance/soc2-controls.md names the application audit trail as a control (line 45, `audit_events`, marked phase-1; line 26 as a solo-founder compensating control) but says nothing about a hash chain or gapless sequencing, so "we cannot prove the chain has no gaps" is a forward-looking risk about a control the code implements more strictly than the doc curr

### `major` 16 partial unique indexes enforce business invariants DynamoDB cannot express at all — a GSI key is not unique

**Evidence.** Queried from the live DB (`pg_indexes` where `indexdef LIKE 'CREATE UNIQUE%' AND indexdef LIKE '%WHERE%'`), 16 of them:
```
property_photos_one_cover_idx  UNIQUE (property_id) WHERE is_cover
parcel_photos_one_cover_idx    UNIQUE (parcel_id)   WHERE is_cover
uq_dispatch_one_accept         UNIQUE (ticket_id)   WHERE offer_state = 'accepted'
uq_dispatch_one_per_round      UNIQUE (ticket_id, associate_id, offer_round) WHERE purpose='offer' AND associate_id <> ''
account_erasure_active         UNIQUE (owner_user_id) WHERE status <> 'completed'
idx_family_self_grp            UNIQUE (owner_user_id, group_id) WHERE is_self
uq_service_payments_idem       UNIQUE (idempotency_key) WHERE idempotency_key <> ''
idx_share_links_token          UNIQUE (token_hash) WHERE token_hash <> ''
idx_ticket_dispatches_token    UNIQUE (token_hash) WHERE token_hash <> ''
uq_associates_contact/_token   UNIQUE (contact_key)/(token_hash) WHERE <> ''
uq_desk_tasks_dedupe           UNIQUE (dedupe_key) WHERE dedupe_key <> ''
idx_states/districts/mandals/villages_lgd_code  UNIQUE (lgd_code) WHERE lgd_code <> ''
```
The source shows the intent, e.g. main.py:5493-5495: `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS property_photos_one_cover_idx ON property_photos (property_id) WHERE is_cover` with the comment 'one database-enforced cover per property'.

Across the whole public schema: 119 unique indexes, 85 primary keys, 7 unique constraints, 4 check constraints, 201 indexes total. Only 2 foreign keys — referential integrity is already application-enforced.

DynamoDB enforces uniqueness on the table's primary key only. GSI keys are explicitly non-unique. There is no partial index and no filtered constraint.

**Impact.** Sixteen invariants that are currently impossible to violate become possible to violate. `uq_dispatch_one_accept` is the one that matters commercially: it is what stops two associates both accepting the same job. `account_erasure_active` stops a duplicate erasure. `idx_share_links_token` / `idx_ticket_dispatches_token` stop token collisions on security-bearing links. Reimplementing each requires a separate 'lock item' with a conditional PutItem (PK = `TICKET#<id>#ACCEPT`) written inside the same TransactWriteItems as the business item — doable, but it is 16 new invariants to design, 16 new item types, and each one is a race condition you have to find in testing rather than one the engine refuses.

Partial credit where due: the low FK count (2) means a DynamoDB move loses less referential integrity than an 83-table relational schema usually would, because the cascades are already hand-written.

**Recommendation.** Enumerate all 16 before any migration decision and decide, per invariant, whether an application-level lock item is acceptable. Several are cheap (token uniqueness folds naturally into a PK on the token hash). `uq_dispatch_one_accept` and `account_erasure_active` are the ones to argue about, because a violation is a customer-visible or compliance-visible incident. If more than ~4 of them need bespoke lock items, that is a signal the aggregate belongs in Postgres.

> **Verifier correction.** Two precision fixes, neither changing the conclusion: (1) the property_photos DDL is at services/api/src/main.py:5496-5497 (parcel twin at 5791), and the comment "one database-enforced cover per property" is at main.py:5474 in the ensure_property_photos_schema docstring, not on the index lines. (2) uq_dispatch_one_accept is liveness-scoped, not history-scoped — an unassign downgrades the prior accepted row to 'ended' in the same transaction so a job can legally be re-offered (associates.py:398-403). A DynamoDB replacement therefore needs a releasable/re-acquirable lock item, not a write-once marker, which is harder than the finding suggests. Also worth adding: the codebase already asserts these indexes exist at boot (REQUIRED_INDEXES, associates.py:995-1000) because a silently-skipped CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS would leave the backstop absent — DynamoDB offers no equivalent startup check.

### `major` AWS DMS is not usable for this migration — its DynamoDB target cannot join, so all 85 tables need custom ETL

**Evidence.** DMS's DynamoDB target requires an object-mapping rule per source table, each defining a partition key, optional sort key, and attribute mappings. Hard constraints that decide it here:
- Object mapping maps ONE source table to ONE target item. It cannot join. A DynamoDB single-table design for this schema needs `properties` + `property_owners` + `documents` + `document_parties` + `registered_documents` + `property_photos` collapsed into one item collection — that is a join, per aggregate.
- DMS DynamoDB target does not support LOBs, and item size is capped at 400 KB.
- Composite natural keys in this schema are multi-column: `parcel_fields_pkey`, `payment_operations_intent_id_kind_key`, `document_read_jobs_owner_user_id_request_key_key`, `governance_policy_sets_scope_key_revision_key`, `uq_dispatch_one_per_round (ticket_id, associate_id, offer_round)` — each needs a hand-written key-composition rule in JSON.
- The source schema is created by boot-time DDL, not by migration files: 89 `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` and 225 `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` across main.py (39/52), web360.py (16/100), associates.py (10/26), geography.py (3/29), ticketing.py (4/9), audit.py (3/4), governance.py (2/5), payments.py (3/0), gateway/sql/init.sql (9/0), all inside `pg_advisory_lock(918273645)` (main.py:5576). DMS task definitions would have to be regenerated whenever a boot ALTER adds a column.

For the land corpus: DynamoDB import-from-S3 costs $0.15/GB of uncompressed source and consumes NO write capacity, versus live writes. Doing the arithmetic on the real corpus (140.5M party @ ~250 B = 1 WRU each; 45.1M records @ ~620 B = 1 WRU; 44.4M boundary vectors @ ~5 KB = 5 WRU each) gives ~408M write request units. At on-demand write pricing that is roughly $255-$500 one-pass, versus ~$43 for import-from-S3 on 287 GB. But import-from-S3 works ONLY into a table that does not yet exist — you cannot import into an existing table — so it is usable exactly once per table, for the initial load, and is useless for backfill-after-drift or for a second attempt.

**Impact.** There is no managed migration path. Every aggregate needs a bespoke extractor (Postgres → denormalised JSON → S3), a bespoke transformer, and a bespoke loader. Budget ~1 week per aggregate for extract+transform+verify, times 12-15 aggregates. The import-from-S3 'new table only' rule is the trap: the moment you discover a key-design mistake after cutover you are re-importing into a NEW table with a new name and re-pointing the application, or paying full write capacity to backfill.

**Recommendation.** Assume custom ETL from the start and budget for it explicitly; do not put DMS in the plan. Build the extractor on top of the repository seam (finding 1) so the same code path produces both the live read and the migration export — that is the only way the exported shape is guaranteed to match what the application expects. For the initial load, use import-from-S3 and accept it is one-shot: rehearse the key design against a full-size copy of the data BEFORE the real import, because the primary key cannot be changed afterwards.

> **Verifier correction.** Two detail fixes, neither weakening the finding:

1. `uq_dispatch_one_per_round` is a partial UNIQUE INDEX, not a table constraint: services/api/src/associates.py:410-412 — `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS uq_dispatch_one_per_round ON ticket_dispatches (ticket_id, associate_id, offer_round) WHERE purpose = 'offer' AND associate_id <> ''`. This is worse for DynamoDB than the finding states: a composite key can be string-concatenated into a sort key, but a *conditional* uniqueness predicate has no DynamoDB equivalent and must be re-implemented as an application-level conditional write with its own tombstone/sentinel scheme.

2. The ALTER count is 227, not 225, and the per-file split is main.py 51 (not 52), web360.py 101 (not 100), associates.py 27 (not 26), ticketing.py 10 (not 9); geography.py 29, audit.py 4, governance.py 5, payments.py 0 are correct. CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS is exactly 89 as claimed. Public schema is 85 tables live (the "83" in the brief is stale), plus gateway's 9 in its own init.sql.

### `major` Dual-write has no distributed transaction here, and after the first DynamoDB-only write there is no rollback without a purpose-built reverse-ETL

**Evidence.** Nothing in the repo does cross-store writes today. The one two-phase pattern that exists is the audit outbox — `audit_outbox` → `run_one()` → `audit_events_v2` (audit.py:838-890), and it works only because both sides are in the same Postgres transaction. `idempotency_keys` (main.py:6443-6464, 6935-6964) gives per-owner replay protection for GraphQL mutations, which is a usable building block but is scoped to one store.

DynamoDB Streams retention is 24 hours — that is the entire replay window for a Streams-based reverse-writer. DynamoDB export-to-S3 requires PITR to be enabled and produces a point-in-time snapshot that is not transactionally aligned with a Postgres snapshot. PITR retention is 35 days.

The drift surface is large: 1,156 SQL statements, 165 `SELECT *` reads (meaning the code consumes whatever columns the row has), and 282 UPDATEs, many of them dynamically constructed (`cols = ", ".join(f"{k}=%s" for k in sets)`, main.py:3228) so the written column set varies per call.

**Impact.** Phase-by-phase failure modes: (a) Dual-write: a write that lands in Postgres and fails in DynamoDB (or vice versa) leaves silent divergence, and with no distributed transaction the only honest implementation is write-Postgres-first-then-enqueue-to-an-outbox, which means DynamoDB is always eventually consistent with Postgres and shadow-read comparison will produce false-positive diffs during the lag window. (b) Backfill racing live writes: a backfill row can overwrite a newer live write unless every item carries a monotonic version and the backfill uses `ConditionExpression: attribute_not_exists(pk) OR version < :v`. (c) Shadow-read: with 165 `SELECT *` sites the comparator must normalise NULL-vs-absent (Postgres stores NULL, DynamoDB omits the attribute) or it will report diffs on every nullable column. (d) Rollback after the first DynamoDB-only write: your Postgres copy is now stale by however long you ran, and recovering it requires a Streams consumer that was already running (24h window) or a PITR export + reverse ETL that cannot reconstruct deletes.

**Recommendation.** If this goes ahead: (1) Postgres remains the system of record for the entire dual-write period — every write goes to Postgres inside its existing transaction and enqueues to a `dynamo_outbox` table modelled exactly on `audit_outbox`, drained by a worker. Never write DynamoDB first. (2) Add a `version bigint` and `updated_at timestamptz` to every migrating table before any backfill, and make every DynamoDB write conditional on it. (3) Shadow-read comparator on the read path, logging normalised JSON diffs (NULL≡absent, numeric coercion) to a `shadow_diffs` table with the resolver name; require zero diffs over 14 days of real traffic per aggregate. (4) Nightly full reconcile: DynamoDB export-to-S3 (needs PITR on) + Postgres snapshot, diffed in Athena, watermarked by `updated_at` to tolerate lag. (5) Stand up the reverse Streams→Postgres writer BEFORE the cutover, not after, and leave it running for at least 30 days — the 24-hour Streams retention means it cannot be started retroactively. (6) Rehearse the rollback on a copy before doing the forward cutover.

**Effort.** 6-8 weeks for the dual-write, backfill, comparator and reverse-writer tooling, before any aggregate is actually migrated

> **Verifier correction.** Same finding, with three evidence fixes:

### `major` 509 tests would need re-platforming; 16 files stand up a real PostgreSQL and encode SQL-level behaviour

**Evidence.** 51 test files, 509 `def test_` functions, 8,583 lines: api 27 files / 378 tests / 6,437 lines; gateway 19 / 104 / 1,572; assistant 5 / 27 / 574.

16 of them create a real database, e.g. services/api/tests/test_ai_reading_dedupe.py:36-49:
```python
dsn = os.getenv('TEST_PG_DSN', 'host=localhost port=5432 dbname=postgres user=rhub password=rhub-dev-pwd')
admin = await psycopg.AsyncConnection.connect(dsn, autocommit=True)
pool = AsyncConnectionPool(dsn, min_size=1, max_size=4, open=False, configure=configure, ...)
```
Same shape in test_capabilities_settlement.py:40, test_import_jobs.py:25, test_web360_trail.py:66, plus test_audit.py, test_audit_boundary.py, test_payments.py, test_ticketing.py, test_associates.py, test_idempotency.py, test_account_data.py, test_property_photos.py, test_invitation_security.py, test_notification_retention.py, test_api_misc_hardening.py, assistant/test_attachment_migration.py.

CI runs them against a `postgres:17` service container (.github/workflows/ci.yml:41-43, 78-84). Locally they need `LC_ALL=C` because Postgres 17 on macOS cannot start an ephemeral cluster without it. services/api/tests/conftest.py is 4 lines — it only fixes `sys.path`; there is no shared DB fixture, so each of the 16 files carries its own duplicated bootstrap.

The tests that matter most are exactly the ones that assert SQL-level guarantees: test_audit.py (440 lines) verifies the hash chain; test_idempotency.py (438) verifies the `ON CONFLICT` replay path; test_payments.py (392) verifies the `FOR UPDATE` money state machine; test_ticketing.py (1,049) verifies dispatch uniqueness.

**Impact.** These tests do not port — they assert behaviour that Postgres provides. A DynamoDB adapter needs an entirely new suite asserting the equivalent guarantees through conditional writes, run against DynamoDB Local or moto (neither is in any requirements file today; `grep -rn 'moto|dynamo' services/*/requirements*.txt` returns nothing). During the dual-write period you need BOTH suites green simultaneously, so the test surface roughly doubles before it shrinks. The duplicated per-file bootstrap means the parameterisation work ('run this suite against adapter A and adapter B') has to be done 16 times unless the fixtures are consolidated first.

**Recommendation.** Before anything else, consolidate the 16 duplicated bootstraps into one `conftest.py` fixture that yields a repository instance rather than a pool. Then the same 378 api tests run against PostgresRepository today and against DynamoRepository later, parameterised, which is what makes shadow comparison credible. This is a 1-2 week job that is worth doing this month whether or not DynamoDB ever happens.

**Effort.** 3-4 weeks to re-platform the DB-backed suites; 1-2 weeks for the fixture consolidation that should happen regardless

DynamoDB Local is AWS's own build and is the correct target for `ConditionExpression` semantics, but models neither throughput throttling nor partition behaviour, so hot-partition failures on a Telangana-first access pattern will not appear there. ### `major` Calibrated effort: 40-58 engineer-weeks solo (10-14 calendar months) for the OLTP half, during which nothing else ships

**Evidence.** Derived from the measured surface above, at ~4 productive engineering days/week for a solo founder who also does everything else:

| phase | scope | weeks |
|---|---|---|
| 0. Access-pattern inventory + single-table key design | derive access patterns from 270 resolvers across 85 tables; write the PK/SK/GSI design doc | 4-6 |
| 1. Repository seam | move 1,554 call sites behind ~12-15 Protocols + Postgres adapters | 6-8 |
| 2. Test fixture consolidation + DynamoDB Local harness | 16 bootstraps → 1 parameterised fixture | 2-3 |
| 3. Dual-write outbox, backfill tooling, shadow comparator, reverse Streams writer | new subsystem, no prior art in repo | 6-8 |
| 4. First 3 aggregates (property, document, family) — adapter + tests + 14-day shadow soak each | learning curve is here | 6-8 |
| 5. Remaining aggregates incl. ticketing/associates/governance | 9-12 more aggregates | 12-20 |
| 6. Cutover, soak, rollback rehearsal per aggregate | | 3-4 |
| — audit + payments | recommended NOT to port (findings 3, 4) | excluded |
| — land corpus 287 GB | not portable (finding 2) | excluded |
| **total** | | **39-57** |

The repo state this competes with: prod runtime is destroyed (no ECS/ALB in ap-south-1; pattadar.com serves the SPA but /api/* is 502 and api.pattadar.com does not resolve), 152 verified w360 UX defects open of which 87 are dead or lying controls, the iOS M-series redesign has filing/maps/people/money clusters unbuilt, associates desk phases 2-5 are designed but not built, and the platform is pre-launch with zero paying users. Row counts in the live public schema confirm pre-launch scale: the largest business table is `audit_events` at 687 rows; `property_photos` 50; `work_requests` 16.

**Impact.** A 10-14 month datastore migration for a pre-launch product whose largest table has 687 rows is a year spent optimising a bottleneck that does not exist yet. The opportunity cost is the launch itself: restoring /api/*, closing the 87 dead controls, finishing the iOS clusters, and shipping associates desk are all things that produce users; the migration produces the same product on different storage. Meanwhile the migration is being designed against zero production traffic, which means the access patterns you commit to in phase 0 are guesses — and the DynamoDB primary key is the one thing you cannot change later.

**Recommendation.** Do phases 0-2 (access-pattern inventory, repository seam, test fixture consolidation — 12-17 weeks, and genuinely valuable on their own), then STOP and re-decide. That sequence is strictly dominant: it is the prerequisite for the migration if you want it, it is the highest-value refactor if you don't, and it is the only way to defer the irreversible key-schema decision until real traffic exists to inform it. Reassess after ~6 months of production load, when you can point at an actual Postgres query that is actually too slow.

**Effort.** recommend 12-17 weeks (phases 0-2) instead of 40-58

### `major` Irreversible vs reversible: only three decisions in the proposal are one-way doors, and all three are on the DynamoDB side

**Evidence.** IRREVERSIBLE:
1. **The DynamoDB primary key schema.** PK and SK cannot be altered on an existing table — changing them requires creating a new table and re-importing everything. LSIs can only be created at table-creation time (max 5). GSIs can be added online (20 per table by default) but backfilling one across 45M items consumes write capacity for hours-to-days. Committing to a key design before production traffic exists means committing on guesses.
2. **The first DynamoDB-only write.** After you stop writing to Postgres, your Postgres copy diverges permanently. Streams retention is 24 hours, so a reverse-writer cannot be started retroactively; PITR (35 days) + export-to-S3 gives a lossy reconstruction that cannot recover deletes.
3. **Decommissioning the Postgres instance / dropping the `land` schema.** 287 GB, and the HNSW indexes (28 GB per partition) take many hours to rebuild from scratch. docs/runbooks/rds-restore-drill.md exists — it should be re-run before anything is dropped.

REVERSIBLE (and therefore cheap to try):
- S3 presigned uploads — purely additive; the existing gateway route (routes/storage.py:403) stays as the fallback.
- Amplify hosting — a CloudFront distribution swap plus DNS; the existing infra/terraform/modules/runtime/cloudfront.tf can be re-pointed.
- The five-subdomain topology — DNS records plus Cognito callback allowlist entries; reversible in an hour. (Caveat: callback allowlist drift is already a known operational bug in this project, so treat each new subdomain as a live-config change that must be applied to the prod app client, not just terraform.)
- The repository seam — a pure refactor with no runtime or data consequences.

**Impact.** The proposal bundles three genuinely one-way decisions with four cheap reversible ones. Approving it as a package means accepting the one-way doors to get the cheap wins, when the cheap wins do not depend on them at all.

**Recommendation.** Unbundle. Ship the four reversible items now — presigned uploads, hosting, subdomains, repository seam — none of which requires a single line of DynamoDB. Defer all three irreversible ones behind a written trigger condition (e.g. 'a specific Postgres access pattern exceeds X at Y QPS'). For the key schema specifically, when the time comes, rehearse it against a full-size data copy and prove the top 20 access patterns before the first real import, because import-from-S3 only works into a table that does not yet exist.

(28 GB is the size of a whole `bv_p*` partition including its heap, not the index.) `docs/runbooks/rds-restore-drill.md` exists but drills only the application Postgres snapshot (`pattadar-prod-pg-final-*` → db.t4g.micro); it does not mention the `land` schema anywhere, so it gives no evidence that the 425 GB corpus can be restored. A separate restore rehearsal for `land` is required before anything there is dropped."

### `moderate` The boot-time DDL (89 CREATE TABLE, 225 ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN) is the real migration liability today, and DynamoDB would make it disappear for the wrong reason

**Evidence.** There is no migration tool in this repo — no Alembic, no sqitch, no numbered SQL files. Schema is applied at process start inside `pg_advisory_lock(918273645)` (services/api/src/main.py:5576) via idempotent DDL:
```
main.py       39 CREATE TABLE   52 ALTER TABLE   33 CREATE INDEX
web360.py     16              100              19
associates.py 10               26              26
geography.py   3               29               4
ticketing.py   4                9               9
audit.py       3                4               5
governance.py  2                5               4
payments.py    3                0               1
gateway/sql/init.sql  9         0              17
-- 89 CREATE TABLE, 225 ALTER TABLE, 118 CREATE INDEX
```
The live DB has 85 public tables and 201 indexes. web360.py alone carries 100 `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` statements — the accumulated history of every column ever added to the w360 surface, re-executed on every boot.

**Impact.** This is already a problem: schema changes are un-reviewable as a diff (they are scattered across 8 files), un-rollback-able (an ADD COLUMN has no DROP counterpart), and there is no record of which version of the schema a given deploy expects. It also inflates boot time and makes any 'is prod's schema correct?' question unanswerable except by inspection. The DynamoDB pitch will be attractive partly because it makes all 225 ALTERs vanish — DynamoDB is schemaless, so adding an attribute is free. That is a genuine benefit, but it is the wrong reason to switch datastores: the same benefit is available from a JSONB attributes column, and the underlying problem (no migration tool, no versioning) follows you to DynamoDB in a worse form, because there you also lose the ability to backfill with a single UPDATE statement.

**Recommendation.** Independently of the DynamoDB decision, adopt a migration tool (Alembic in `--sql`/offline mode is enough — you are not using an ORM and do not need autogenerate) and freeze the boot DDL as migration 0001. Then: no new `ALTER TABLE` in application source. This is roughly a 1-2 week job and it is a prerequisite for anything that has to reason about 'what shape is the data in production right now' — which is the first question any migration, dual-write or shadow comparator has to answer.

**Effort.** 1-2 weeks

> **Verifier correction.** Claim stands as written, with one scoping clarification: of the 89 CREATE TABLE / 118 CREATE INDEX, 9 and 17 respectively come from services/gateway/sql/init.sql, which the gateway applies in its own init path — not inside the api's pg_advisory_lock(918273645) block. The api-side boot DDL under that lock is 80 CREATE TABLE, 225 ALTER TABLE (all ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS), 101 CREATE INDEX. The repo does contain one unnumbered hand-run script, scripts/migrate-org-id.sql, but no migration framework of any kind.

### `moderate` Item-size and page-size limits need measuring before commitment: 8 jsonb columns include full AI deed-extraction payloads

**Evidence.** jsonb columns in the public schema: `document_read_jobs.result`, `payment_operations.payload`, `payment_operations.result`, `payment_intents.settlement_plan`, `audit_events_v2.metadata`, `audit_outbox.metadata`, `account_erasure_jobs.stages`, `account_consents.purposes`. Plus large text columns: `idempotency_keys.response`, `ticket_deliverables.payload`, `notes.body`, `notification_log.body`, `states/districts/mandals/villages.source_payload`.

`document_read_jobs.result` holds a full deed extraction — the vault design requires extraction prompts to emit spine/review/fields/links inside the fields dict, so a multi-page sale deed's result is the largest single value in the schema. The local DB is a dev copy and does not contain representative production rows, so I cannot measure the real distribution from here; the real corpus is at ~/Land-Records/01-Sale-Deeds.

Relevant hard limits: DynamoDB item max 400 KB including attribute names; Query/Scan returns at most 1 MB per page; TransactWriteItems aggregate 4 MB; BatchWriteItem 16 MB / 25 items.

**Impact.** If any deed extraction result exceeds 400 KB, that item simply cannot be stored — it has to be split across items or offloaded to S3 with a pointer, which is a schema change to the aggregate and a change to every reader. The wide tables also matter for the 1 MB page limit: `properties` has 43 columns, `family_members` 41, `parcels` 41, `registered_documents` 39, `documents` 32, `ticket_dispatches` 31, `associates` 31. A list query that returns 100 properties with all 43 attributes plus DynamoDB's per-item attribute-name overhead will page more often than the equivalent Postgres query, meaning more round trips for the same screen.

**Recommendation.** Before any key-design work, run a measurement pass over the real corpus: serialise a representative sample of `document_read_jobs.result`, `payment_operations.result` and `audit_events_v2.metadata` to DynamoDB JSON and report p50/p99/max sizes. If p99 is above ~200 KB, plan for S3 offload with a pointer attribute from day one rather than discovering it after import (which, per the import-from-S3 'new table only' rule, means re-importing).

> **Verifier correction.** Item-size limits are a HARD BLOCKER in one place and a non-issue in the place the finding named.

Hard blocker: `document_read_jobs.source BYTEA` (services/api/src/ai_reading/jobs.py:35) stores the uploaded document bytes inline in the row, capped at `MAX_BYTES = 25 * 1024 * 1024` (jobs.py:24, enforced jobs.py:65-67). DynamoDB's item limit is 400 KB including attribute names, so this row cannot exist as a DynamoDB item at up to 62x over. The bytes are cleared on completion (`source=NULL`, jobs.py:171 and jobs.py:184), so the blob is transient, but the job row must hold it for the whole queued/running window — which is exactly when the worker reads it (jobs.py:141 `SELECT * FROM document_read_jobs WHERE state='queued' ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`). Note also that `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` queue claim has no DynamoDB equivalent and needs a conditional-update lease. Fix: the file goes to S3 on upload (the gateway already does SSE-KMS — services/gateway/src/storage.py ~line 165) and the job item carries only an S3 key. This is a schema change to the aggregate plus a worker change, and it should be designed before key design, not discovered after import.

Non-issue, no measurement needed:

### `positive` POSITIVE: the GraphQL schema is a real contract seam — a datastore swap does not touch any of the four frontends

**Evidence.** 270 server resolvers (110 `@strawberry.field` + 160 `@strawberry.mutation`) over 124 `@strawberry.type` definitions, exposed through one router (`app.include_router(graphql_app)`, main.py:7141). The web app issues 189 named GraphQL operations and reaches them only through gateway-relative paths — apps/web/src/api/client.ts:1-9: 'All paths are GATEWAY-RELATIVE ('/api/...'): the browser never talks to a service host directly.' Client surface totals: apps/web 176 files / 63,268 lines, apps/ios 130 / 29,941, apps/mobile 75 / 15,001, apps/university 44 / 7,303 — roughly 115,000 lines of client code, none of which knows what the datastore is.

A second, smaller mitigation: `to_type(cls, row)` (main.py:120-128) maps a row dict onto a strawberry type by `__annotations__`, dropping unknown keys — and the types default nearly every field (`PropertyType`, main.py:413: only `id` and `owner_user_id` lack defaults; the other 40 fields default to `""`/`0.0`). So DynamoDB's sparse items (absent attribute where Postgres stores NULL) would mostly survive `cls(**out)` rather than raising TypeError. 102 `to_type(` call sites in main.py benefit from this.

**Impact.** This meaningfully shrinks the port: the 165 `SELECT *`-driven resolvers have a tolerant row→type boundary, and zero client changes are needed. It also means the shadow-read comparator can run at the GraphQL response level — compare the serialised response from a Postgres-backed resolver against a DynamoDB-backed one — which is a far better equivalence test than comparing rows.

**Recommendation.** Use the GraphQL response as the comparison unit in any shadow-read harness; it is the actual contract and it is already stable. Note the asymmetry though: web360.py has 0 `to_type` call sites (it constructs types directly), so its 476 call sites do not get this tolerance for free — check each one for required fields before assuming sparse items are safe there.

> **Verifier correction.** Numbers to fix: 39 defaulted fields on PropertyType (not 40); apps/web 175 files / 63,166 lines and apps/university 42 / 7,087 (ios 130/29,941 and mobile 75/15,001 are exact; ~115k total still holds); 157 `SELECT *` occurrences across services/api/src, not 165; 191 named-operation occurrences / 165 unique names in apps/web, not 189; ~346 web360 type-construction call sites, not 476.

Substantive refinement: the `to_type` tolerance should be recorded as a RISK, not a mitigation. Because nearly every field defaults, a sparse DynamoDB item does not raise TypeError — it silently resolves to ""/0.0/False. A missing `purchase_price` renders as ₹0 on a land record rather than failing loudly. Separately, the web360 caveat is understated: its 77 strawberry types are not named `*Type` at all (`Tile`, `WaitingItem`, `RecordCard`, web360.py:669+) and many have zero defaults (`WaitingItem` = 7 bare required fields), so that half of the surface fails loudly while main.py's half fails silently — two different failure modes in one migration. This makes the recommendation stronger, not weaker: a GraphQL-response-level shadow-read comparator is mandatory rather than merely preferable, since row-leve

### `positive` POSITIVE: S3 presigned uploads are the right call and are independent of DynamoDB — but the bucket needs CORS and signed SSE-KMS headers first

**Evidence.** Today every upload is buffered through the gateway process — services/gateway/src/routes/storage.py:423-427:
```python
data = await file.read()
if len(data) > MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES:
    return JSONResponse(status_code=413, content={"error": "File too large"})
```
with `MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES = 100 MB` (routes/storage.py:46) and a body-limit middleware at `MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES + 8 MB` (gateway/src/main.py:55, 205). `grep -n 'presign\|generate_presigned' services/gateway/src/storage.py` returns nothing — there is no presigned path anywhere. The write path is `self._s3.put_object(**self._put_args(...))` (storage.py:434, 456) with `ServerSideEncryption: "aws:kms"` + `SSEKMSKeyId` already set (storage.py:165).

Two prerequisites the code does not have yet:
- **No CORS configuration exists on any bucket**: `grep -rn 'aws_s3_bucket_cors' infra/` returns 0 matches, and cloudfront.tf:3 records the reason — 'https://pattadar.com, so no CORS anywhere'. A browser PUT to `s3.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com` is cross-origin and will fail preflight until an `aws_s3_bucket_cors_configuration` exists with the right AllowedOrigins, `PUT`, and `ExposeHeaders: [ETag]`.
- **The bucket policy denies unsigned SSE-KMS**: infra/terraform/modules/persistent/s3.tf:277-315 gates on `s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption = aws:kms` and `s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption-aws-kms-key-id = <key arn>` when `enforce_documents_sse_kms_headers` is on. Those headers must be included in the signature when generating the presigned URL AND sent by the browser on the PUT, or S3 returns 403.
- GuardDuty Malware Protection tags objects with a scan verdict and the policy denies `THREATS_FOUND` (s3.tf:250-271). Presigned PUTs are still scanned (it is a bucket-level plan), so this is fine — but the gateway must not create the `storage_versions` row until the upload is confirmed, or the vault will list files that are quarantined or never arrived.

**Impact.** Done right this removes 100 MB of buffering per upload from the gateway process, removes the 108 MB body-limit ceiling, and makes large deed scans upload at the client's full bandwidth rather than the gateway's. For an Indian land-records product where users photograph multi-page deeds on phones, this is a real user-visible win and it costs days, not months.

**Recommendation.** Ship this now, independently, in this order: (1) add `aws_s3_bucket_cors_configuration` to modules/persistent/s3.tf scoped to the real origins; (2) add a `POST /storage/upload-intent` route that returns a presigned PUT (or `create_multipart_upload` + presigned parts above ~100 MB — S3 requires multipart above 5 GB and it is the right choice above ~100 MB anyway) with `ServerSideEncryption` and `SSEKMSKeyId` in `Params` so they are signed; set an expiry of 15 minutes or less; (3) add `POST /storage/files/{id}/complete` that verifies the object exists and its ETag/size before inserting the `storage_versions` row; (4) keep the existing `POST /files` route working for iOS and as fallback. Note the presigned URL must be minted by a principal whose credentials outlive the URL — an ECS task role rotation invalidates outstanding URLs, so keep the expiry short and let the client re-request.

> **Verifier correction.** Same finding with two details fixed: (a) the read-buffer block is services/gateway/src/routes/storage.py:422-424, not 423-427; (b) the SSE-KMS deny statements at infra/terraform/modules/persistent/s3.tf:277-315 are gated on `var.enforce_documents_sse_kms_headers`, which is set to `false` in BOTH environments (infra/terraform/envs/prod/persistent/main.tf:46, envs/dev/persistent/main.tf:47) — so an unsigned-SSE presigned PUT would succeed today; the signed `ServerSideEncryption`/`SSEKMSKeyId` params are a forward-compatibility requirement for when that flag is turned on, not a current 403. Also note the gateway only sets those headers when `not self._allow_unencrypted_local` (storage.py:163), so the presign path must mirror that conditional. Everything else — no presigned code anywhere, no `aws_s3_bucket_cors_configuration` in infra/, the 100 MB / 108 MB ceilings, the GuardDuty THREATS_FOUND read-deny — checks out verbatim.
